--- Artificial Neural Networks Section Introduction ---

In this section of the course, we are going to introduce the idea of artificial neural networks or
Anns for short.
Specifically, we're going to talk about a certain kind of artificial neural network called a feedforward
neural network.
This is the most basic kind of neural network, but as you'll see, the concepts involved go quite deep,
and they also form the basis for other kinds of neural networks such as convolutional neural networks
and recurrent neural networks.
To begin this discussion.
Let's start with why and how neural networks came to be in the first place.
This is something that a lot of teachers skip over, but it's something that I find quite interesting
and inspirational.
If you think neural networks are a cool new model to help you pick stocks or play Mario Kart, then
you are still looking for the small fish.
In fact, neural networks are way more interesting than that.
As you may have realized, the name artificial neural network means that we are trying to artificially
create a neural network in a computer.
Okay, so then what's a real neural network?
As in a non artificial neural network.
The name neural network derives from neurons which are the cells in your brain and extend throughout
your nervous system.
Now, this is probably too obvious for most of you, but let's state it anyway, just in case.
Your brain is what you use to think.
Neurons in your brain are connected to each other and can communicate with each other via electrical
and chemical signals.
Amazingly, this simple physical and chemical system is what makes you you.
All of your thoughts and aspirations, your emotions, and every action you take throughout the day.
It's all driven by your neurons, which in turn are just sending electrical and chemical signals around
amongst themselves.
Once scientists realized what the brain was doing and what it was responsible for, the next question
was almost obvious.
In hindsight, can we build a brain?
I mean, if the brain is just a network of neurons and we can simulate neurons in a computer, if we
connect a bunch of neurons through a computer simulation, it seems that it might be possible to create
some form of intelligence, an artificial intelligence, you might say.
So let's take our model of a single neuron logistic regression.
Now let's imagine that we have multiple neurons, all taking in the same inputs, but computing something
different.
Now let's do it again.
Now we have multiple logistic regressions.
Now let's imagine that all these neurons are connected to more neurons.
So we just repeat the process, pretending that the new layer of neurons are actually inputs to more
neurons.
That's basically a very tiny model of the brain neurons connected to neurons.
Of course, this is necessarily simplistic.
One side is the input and one side is the output.
Of course, the actual brain is much more complex.
There are many inputs and many outputs in the middle.
Wires can criss cross If we have a later neuron connecting back to an earlier neuron, we call that
a recurrent connection.
The neural networks we are about to discuss in this section contain no such complexities inside said
because the input is on one side and the output is on the other side and we go from input to output
in a layer wise fashion.
We call this a feedforward neural network.
In the rest of this lecture, we are going to outline what we will discuss in this section of the course.
First, we are going to start out again by discussing the model architecture.
As you know, the model will be discussing in this section is the feed forward neural network.
The next step after discussing the model architecture will be to go back to the geometric picture.
If you recall, my model goes machine learning is nothing but a geometry problem.
So how do neural networks extend the capabilities of a basic linear model in terms of solving this geometry
problem?
Next, we're going to go more in depth and discuss activation functions.
Activation functions are very important in neural networks.
They are what make neural networks more expressive than the simple linear models you saw in the previous
section.
After that, we're going to discuss how to do Multi-class classification using neural networks.
If you recall in the previous section we only discussed binary classification.
This works if we only have two classes dog or cat fraud or no fraud, purchase or leave the store and
so on.
But what if we have more classes?
For example, we might be working on a self-driving car that needs to be able to recognize multiple
kinds of objects on the road.
In this case, binary classification is not good enough and we need Multi-class classification.
Next, we'll switch gears from talking about the neural network itself to a new kind of data images.
Deep learning excels on unstructured data such as images, text and sound.
We'll look at how images are represented in a computer so that we can get back to the situation where
we can say all data is the same.
Finally, we'll look at some notebooks that demonstrate how to do image classification using a neural
network for multiple classes.
We'll also look at neural networks for regression and see how they can be much more expressive than
a simple linear model.

--- Forward Propagation ---

In this lecture, we are going to discuss forward propagation as it relates to neural networks.
Neural networks are all about making predictions, and so making a prediction in a neural network is
what we refer to as going in the forward direction.
Previously we looked at logistic regression and how this might be seen as a computer simulation of a
neuron.
Well, let's consider an interesting scenario.
Here we have one neuron, but let's say these same inputs propagate to another output.
Now we have two neurons.
Each of them could be calculating something different given the same inputs.
If you want a sort of real world analogy of that, suppose we're looking at a face.
One neuron might be looking for the presence of an eye and another might be looking for the presence
of a nose.
In other words, different neurons are finding different features from the input.
Well, how about we add another neuron?
Now we have a whole layer of neurons.
The key insight in deep learning made decades ago by researchers such as Geoffrey Hinton was that we
could stack more neurons on top of the existing neurons.
Remember, that's kind of like how our brain is.
We don't just have one neuron in our brain.
We have many.
So one neuron might be connected to another neuron, which might then be further connected to another
neuron and so forth.
So you end up with this long chain of neurons.
The key idea is that we can think of neurons as uniform, although these days we know a lot about the
brain.
And so this is not physically the case.
Some parts of the brain are different than others, but for our simple computational model, we can
assume the brain is uniform.
There's no difference between a neuron in one part of the brain compared to another part.
As you might expect, we can just keep doing this so we have one layer going to another layer going
to another layer and so on.
So what have we done?
Well, we expanded our concept of neurons in two important ways.
First, we said that the same inputs could be attached to multiple different neurons, each calculating
something different.
This means more neurons per layer.
Second, we said that neurons in one layer could act as inputs to another layer of neurons.
So the first concept is to make the neural network more wide.
The second concept is to make the neural network more deep.
And believe it or not, this is all it takes to build a neural network.
Take a neuron, add more neurons across the same layer to make it wide, and then add more layers to
make it deep.
Previously we looked at the equation for a line and that became our model.
So if you recall, that was AX plus B.
We then said in order to make this into a neuron, we're going to have multiple inputs and multiple
weights.
We're also going to attach a sigmoid at the end in order to map the output to a value between 0 and
1, representing something like the probability of an action potential.
That became the sigmoid of W transpose x plus B.
The next question to consider is what should we do if we have multiple neurons per layer?
Well, suppose I have some output at the next layer.
Let's call that Z sub J to represent the jth neuron in that layer.
Then z sub j would just be the sigmoid of w sub j transpose x plus b sub j.
Let's say that j goes from one to M so that we have m neurons in this layer.
That also means we'll have m w j's and m BJs.
It turns out that we can make this calculation more efficient if we consider Z to be a vector of neuron
values instead of just a scalar.
Then we can write this more compactly.
Now, you might find this a little confusing if you're not used to working with matrices and functions
of matrices.
So we have to clarify a few things first.
Z is a vector of size.
M X is as before, a vector of size D or equivalently, you can think of Z as a column vector of size,
M by one and X to be a column vector of size D by one.
In order to make this a valid matrix multiplication, we have the following.
W is a matrix of size D by M, so when you transpose it, you get M by D, and then when you multiply
that by X, which is of size D, you get out a vector of size m.
In order to add the bias term B, it is also a vector of size.
M The sigmoid here is meant to be an element wise operation, so whatever the size of the input is,
it has the same size output.
In other words, it would be as if you applied the sigmoid to each component of its argument individually
and then organize them into a vector or matrix of the same shape.
Using these concepts.
Let's think about how we might mathematically express going from the input to the output of a neural
network.
First, let's consider that we are going to have multiple layers, so it's not enough to call our weights
just W and B instead we're going to give them indices.
So let's say W superscript one and B superscript one are the weight and bias of the first layer.
W superscript two and B superscript two are the weight and bias of the second layer and so forth.
Z Superscript one will be the output of the first layer.
Z Superscript two will be the output of the second layer and so forth.
Now, there are two things to notice about the final layer here.
First, you'll notice that I've used the capital letter L to denote the total number of layers.
The letter L is common in deep learning.
So if you see the letter L elsewhere, don't be surprised.
Just pay attention to the context.
In this context, we mean the number of layers.
And of course, the input to the last layer will be the output of the second last layer.
So we have this nice repeating pattern at each layer.
Each layer is just the same equation over and over again.
So that's what we mean when we say the neural network is uniform.
The second thing to notice is that for now, we'll assume that we are still doing binary classification.
Therefore, the final sigmoid maps the output of the neural network to be a single number between a
zero and one.
You can imagine that if we are doing regression we would not want to have the sigmoid there.
So, of course, this brings us to the next question.
What does a neural network for regression look like?
Surely we are not limited to just binary classification, and luckily it's very similar.
All we do is simply remove the final sigmoid.
One interesting thing we can see from this, if we look at only the final layer here is that it appears
just to be regular old linear regression.
And that brings us to our next major point.
This is a very helpful perspective when you're thinking about neural networks.
So I think it's important to learn about early on.
If you think about what we just looked at, neural networks for binary classification and neural networks
for regression, they are not that different from what we looked at previously.
If we look at just the final layers, they look just like regular old linear regression and logistic
regression.
That's what we studied in the previous section.
So here's a new way to think about neural networks.
Instead of merely a network of layers of neurons, really, we're just doing a series of feature transformations.
So we go from feature to feature to feature.
And then finally we just have a plain linear regression for regression or logistic regression for classification.
Using these uniform layers, researchers noticed that neural networks were able to learn hierarchies
of features.
Each layer seemed to be learning something more complex than the last, and this was the motivation
for making neural networks deep.
And that's why this field is called deep learning.
One of my favorite examples of this is with facial recognition networks by visualizing what each layer
has learned.
Researchers found that the initial layers just learned some basic lines and strokes.
And in the middle, the neural networks started to learn about specific features of faces such as eyes,
nose, mouth and so on.
Finally, at the final layers, the neural networks started to see entire faces.
In other words, neural networks are able to break down a problem into smaller subproblems or, in other
words, a hierarchy.

--- The Geometrical Picture ---

At this point, you are probably sick of me saying machine learning is nothing but a geometry problem.
But here is where it gets really important.
The question we want to answer right now is why are neural networks so important?
Why can't we just use a single neuron?
I mean, it seems to be a pretty good model.
It's nice and interpretable.
Large weights means the corresponding input is important and a very small or zero weights mean the input
is not important.
Unfortunately, this type of model only gets us so far.
You'll recall that when we discussed linear regression and logistic regression, I said there are two
different ways we can make things more complicated.
The first way we can make things more complicated is to add multiple inputs.
That's something we encountered already.
This is still pretty simplistic because you can always picture a plane in your head, a straight non
curving surface that separates data between classes.
The second way we can make things more complicated is that the decision boundary or regression function
we are looking for is not a straight line or plane.
This is what we are interested in when we discuss neural networks.
The reason the equation W transpose x plus B equals zero gives us a hyperplane is because this is the
actual definition of a Hyperplane.
There's no way around this.
There's no possible setting of W and B that could give you a curved surface.
So how can we get a curved surface so that we can solve more complicated problems?
One method you might have thought of is to use feature engineering.
For example, let's take linear regression.
Suppose that for whatever reason, salary is proportional to the square of the years of experience.
Or put another way, that salary is a quadratic function of years of experience.
Then we could say that y hat equals to a x squared plus b, x plus C.
Believe it or not, this is still just linear regression in disguise.
It's easy to see this by doing the following.
Take x the years of experience and call that the input feature x one.
Then take x squared the years of experience squared and call that the input feature x two.
Then my y hat becomes an equation of the form y hat equals W1X1 plus W2X2 plus b.
Of course, we've already learned that this is just a linear regression.
The problem with feature engineering is that there are many different possible features.
Squaring the inputs is common, but so is combining the inputs.
So if I have the inputs x one and x two, then I can make one of the features x one times x two.
We call these interaction terms, but what happens if I have three input features?
Now I have to consider x one squared x two squared and x three squared, but we also have to consider
X1X2X1X3 and X2X3.
In total, there are six quadratic terms.
As an exercise, you might want to list out all the possible combinations we could have if we had four
inputs or five inputs.
You should discover that the number of terms we have to consider grows quite fast and thus feature engineering,
even in this simple form, seems to be a somewhat clumsy solution.
But now let's remember our neuron.
If we consider just the first layer of a neural network, we can see that there are multiple neurons.
Importantly, they are all multiple different neurons.
Each neuron is a different non-linear feature derived from all the inputs because we've applied the
sigmoid activation function.
The feature is not just a simple linear combination of the input features, so you can think of these
as feature one, a feature two feature three all the way up to feature m.
Now, a lot of people wonder, how does this make a neural network nonlinear?
Remember that a linear function always takes the form W transpose x plus B.
Our neural network takes the form of the equation you see here for a two layer neural network.
This is what we get if we drop the Z term and just make the whole thing into one equation.
W two transpose sigmoid of w one transpose x plus B one, and then plus B two.
The important thing to notice about this is you cannot simplify this into a linear function.
If you could, then we would have a linear decision boundary.
So let's see what would happen if we had no sigmoids at all.
Let's suppose we have a regression network with no sigmoid.
Then it's just w two transpose times W1 transpose x plus b one plus B two.
Of course, using basic arithmetic, we can expand the product due to the fact that there is no sigmoid.
It's just straightforward multiplication.
Then we just get W prime transpose x plus b prime where I've substituted w prime equals w two transpose
times w one transpose and B prime equals W two transpose times B one plus B two.
And so you can see that if we have no Sigmoids, this just reduces to a linear equation.
There is an added bonus to using neurons for our features.
If you recall, all the weights and biases are randomly initialized and found using an iterative algorithm
called gradient descent.
When we call the model dot fit function, what this means is that instead of having to do manual feature
engineering to try and guess what features might be good, a neural network will do this automatically.
In the olden days of machine learning, feature engineering at times used to be the only method of really
applying machine learning successfully, and usually this would require domain knowledge.
So if you wanted to build a good image classifier for, say, lung cancer detection, you would have
to be very knowledgeable about different computer vision techniques.
But not only that, you would also have to be very good at looking at x rays of lung cancer.
By the way, note that I'm not a doctor, so I don't know if they actually look at x rays, but you
get the idea.
If you wanted to build a good stock predictor, you would have to be very knowledgeable about finance.
If you wanted to build a good fraud detector, you would have to be very knowledgeable about the insurance
industry.
And as you may have heard, deep learning is so popular because it allows people who are not domain
experts to build very good machine learning models.
Nowadays we have people who are not expert radiologists building state of the art image classifiers
for medical diagnosis.
All they are experts in is machine learning itself.
That is pretty powerful.
Once will I really like is the TensorFlow playground.
You can find it at playground.tensorflow.org.
Basically, this lets you train a neural network on some synthetic data right in your browser.
It lets you see for yourself that the neural network is learning a non-linear decision boundary.
So if you go there, it looks like the default is the donut problem where you have one circle inside
another circle.
And of course, the decision boundary should be a circle.
Your inputs are only the raw data x one and x two, but you could also use feature engineering if you
so choose.
So you could select x one squared, x two squared X1X2, and even the sine of x one and x two.
But we're not going to do that because we want to show that neural networks are able to learn non-linear
features using only the original inputs.
So you never have to manually create things like this yourself.
And so by default, we have a two hidden layer neural network with four hidden units.
So there's one, two, three, four.
So let's just run this and see what happens.
All right.
So as you can see, it learns the linear decision boundary quite efficiently without the need for any
manual feature engineering.
You're encouraged to play around with this yourself.
So try switching datasets so you can pick, let's say, this one and then run it again.
Also try different numbers of hidden layers and different numbers of units per hidden layer.
This should help give you a stronger intuition for how neural networks work and lets you observe for
yourself that they are finding non-linear decision boundaries.

--- Activation Functions ---

In this lecture, we are going to discuss activation functions.
Previously, we learned that the sigmoid function allows us to build neural networks and it does several
important things for us.
First, it maps its inputs to go from 0 to 1.
This is nice because it mimics the biological neuron.
So our neural network is literally a network of artificial neurons.
Second, and this is more practical.
It makes our neural network nonlinear.
The function itself is nonlinear, and it also makes it so that we can't reduce the neural network into
a simple linear equation.
At the same time, in modern, deep learning, we've discovered that there are some problems with the
sigmoid, and so it is no longer used as often except in some specific cases.
If you recall, something we discussed earlier was the importance of standardization.
We don't want to have one input in the range, 1 million to 5 million, and then another input going
from 0 to 0.0001.
Instead, we would like to have all of our data centered around zero and in approximately the same range.
Now, the sigmoid is problematic in this regard.
If you recall, the output of a sigmoid is always between 0 and 1, and its middle value is therefore
0.5.
Therefore, the output of a sigmoid can never be centered around zero.
This goes back to our idea of the uniformity of a neural network.
One layer of a neural network takes as input the output of the previous layer.
So if the previous layer is outputting numbers centered around 0.5, that's not quite right.
This is because this output is going to become the input to the next layer and the next layer wants
to also see inputs which are standardized.
Therefore, we want both the inputs and the outputs of each layer to be standardized.
Luckily there is a solution to this, although it takes away from this idea that a neural network is
made up of this idealized neuron simulation.
Instead of using the sigmoid, we'll use a function that has the exact same shape as the sigmoid, just
centered around zero.
This function is called the tan H, which is short for hyperbolic tangent.
If you recall, there are hyperbolic versions of all the trigonometric functions, so there's a hyperbolic
sine, hyperbolic cosine and so forth.
Well, it turns out that the hyperbolic tangent analogously to the trigonometric tangent is just the
hyperbolic sine over the hyperbolic cosine.
And it has the equation you see here.
The sigmoid has a similar equation one divided by one, plus the exponent of minus x.
The major difference between the sigmoid and the tan h is that the sigmoid goes between 0 and 1, while
the tan h goes between minus one and plus one.
As an exercise, you may want to try and prove the relationship between the sigmoid and the tan H.
In particular, that the tan h is just a scaled and vertically shifted version of the sigmoid.
Now, although the tan H is a little better than the sigmoid, the story is not over.
In modern, deep learning, researchers have figured out that there's actually a problem with both of
these activation functions.
I like to tell this story as a bit of a cautionary tale.
For a long time, researchers were very attached to the beauty of neural networks, as is.
They liked this idea of a uniform neural network made up of idealized neurons.
They liked this idea that the sigmoid and hence also the tan H were smooth differentiable functions.
It is mathematically convenient and beautiful.
The problem is they don't work that well.
But nobody wanted to break away from this classic way of doing things.
The major problem with Sigmoids and ten H's is called the vanishing gradient problem.
Now, this part requires a little bit of calculus knowledge.
So if you're not comfortable with that, then feel free to skip ahead.
Remember that our method of training a neural network is gradient descent.
And obviously this involves finding the gradient of the cost with respect to the parameters.
The problem is when you have a very deep neural network, your gradient has to propagate backwards throughout
the neural network, starting from the end.
What happens is your output is made up of a bunch of composite functions, basically a sigmoid of a
sigmoid of a sigmoid and so on.
When you take the gradient of composite functions, you get the chain rule.
So composite functions become multiplication in the derivative.
So what does this end up looking like?
Well, the further you go back in the neural network, the more terms you have in the chain rule to
multiply.
So you're multiplying by the derivative of the sigmoid over and over again.
What happens when you do that?
Why is the derivative of the sigmoid a problem?
Well, consider what the sigmoid actually looks like.
Most of the sigmoid is flat.
In other words, the derivative of the sigmoid is very nearly zero at most points.
Only in the center is it non-zero.
It also turns out that the maximum value of the derivative is only 0.25.
This is the highest possible value of the derivative of the sigmoid.
Okay.
So why is that a problem?
Well, what happens when you multiply numbers that are very small?
The answer is you get an even smaller number.
Let's say you multiply 0.25.
The maximum possible value, five times.
The result is 0.25 to the power five, which is approximately 0.001.
What happens if more realistically you have a value like 0.1 multiplied by itself five times?
That's 0.1 to the power five, which is 0.00001.
In other words, this leads to the result that the further you go back in the neural network, the smaller
the gradient becomes.
We call this the vanishing gradient problem.
So how does this problem manifest?
Well, take a look at this graph, which shows the magnitude of the gradient at each layer of the neural
network as it is trained.
You'll notice that the further you go back, the smaller the gradient gets.
Remember that the training algorithm is to take small steps in the direction of the gradient.
Well, if the gradient is nearly zero, that means the update to the weights is also nearly zero.
The end result is that weights close to the input of the neural network are almost not trained at all.
This was a problem in the olden days which prevented us from building very deep neural networks like
we have today, which can have hundreds of layers.
Back in the day, there was lots of theory around how to solve this problem.
One method was called greedy layer Wise Pre-training, which was invented by Geoffrey Hinton and his
students.
Since all the layers of the neural network could not be trained all at once, the idea was you could
train each layer one at a time.
So what you would do is train the first layer using some alternative loss function.
By the way, if you want to know what that loss function is, it's basically an autoencoder.
But hold that thought for now as it's not an important detail.
Then once you were done training the first layer, you would add on a second layer and train that layer
by itself not touching the first layer anymore.
Then you would add a third layer and train only the third layer, leaving the first two layers alone.
Once you got to the last layer, all of the previous layers would already be trained to some extent.
Now, despite all this theory which generated lots of theoretical research into new models such as restricted
Boltzmann machines, deep Boltzmann machines and so forth, today we no longer require such complicated
models in order to train very deep neural networks.
Although that's not to say they're not worth learning about.
In fact, the solution was simple Just don't use activation functions that have vanishing gradients.
So throw away these beautiful, smooth differentiable functions like the tan H and the sigmoid.
Instead, let's use this ugly looking, not completely differentiable function called the Relu.
The Relu is short for rectified linear unit.
Basically it looks like a hockey stick and it has a corner at zero where the derivative is technically
not defined.
The fact that value is greater than zero never have a zero gradient makes training neural networks a
lot more efficient.
Now, you might be wondering, wait a minute.
If a zero gradients are so bad, then why does the Relu work at all?
It appears that half the function any input less than zero has a derivative that is exactly zero.
Never mind vanishing.
The gradient is already vanished.
Indeed, this is somewhat of a problem as it leads to a phenomenon called dead neurons.
Dead neurons are neurons that always output zero because the weighted sum of its inputs are always less
than or equal to zero.
However, and this is important in deep learning, what we care about is experimental results.
In other words, the most important thing is that it works, not how theoretically satisfying it is.
Because as we now know, that can lead to lots of wasted time.
The fact that just the right side doesn't vanish seems to be good enough according to what we've observed.
Of course, some researchers have tried to make modifications to the simple relu to solve this problem
of dead neurons.
One alternative is the leaky Relu, which has a slope of less than one like 0.1 for values less than
zero.
Importantly, this is still a non-linear function, so we're able to learn nonlinear geometrical patterns
using the leaky relu.
Your derivatives will always be positive, just like the sigmoid and the tan H.
There are other options as well, such as the Elu or Exponential linear unit, which has a more steadily
decreasing value on the left side.
The authors claim that this activation function speeds up learning and leads to higher accuracy than
the real you.
One interesting aspect of the Elu is that it allows its outputs to be negative, which goes back to
the idea that we like the mean of the values to be close to zero.
Another option which is very similar is the soft plus activation, which because you're taking the log
of the exponent, looks very linear when the input is reasonably large.
Now for both of these previous activation functions, there is the vanishing gradient on the left side,
but we've established that it's not so much of a problem since we know that the Relu already works and
it has gradients that are equal to zero.
Also, although we initially stated that we would like the inputs at each layer to be centered around
zero, we can see that the Relu and Softplus do not accomplish this for the Softplus and the Relu.
The minimum value is zero, while the maximum value is infinity.
This definitely means they won't be centered around zero.
So is the real you Not a good choice in the end?
Now, despite all this work to find alternatives to the rail you activation these days, most people
still use the rail you as a reasonable default choice.
It works well.
And sometimes you'll find that using other alternatives such as the leaky Relu or the Elu offer no benefit.
Sometimes they do.
Which is why you always have to experiment for yourself.
My motto, which a lot of my students are tired of hearing by this point, is that machine learning
is experimentation and not philosophy.
Never use your mind to try and predict the outcome of a computer program.
If you have a computer that is always the suboptimal course of action.
Why not simply run the computer program with a computer?
Your mind is not suitable for running computer programs, but computers are.
Therefore, follow the rule.
Don't use philosophy.
Use experimentation.
Interestingly, some researchers have talked about the biological plausibility of the Relu activation
function.
In fact, it may even be more biologically plausible than the sigmoid.
To understand this, you have to understand a little more about how action potentials encode information.
What is the difference between an action potential when you hear a quiet sound versus an action potential
when you hear a loud sound?
The answer is there is no difference.
An action potential is just an action potential.
The key really is in the frequency of action potentials.
When you hear a very quiet sound, your neurons are only activated a little bit, so you'll get some
action potentials, but they won't be very frequent.
If you hear a very loud sound like say you're at a concert or a party, then your neurons are getting
lots of stimulation.
The action potentials are going to be very frequent.
In other words, closer together in time.
So what we're saying is the more intense a stimulus is, the higher the frequency of the action potentials.
We call this frequency coding.
What does this mean in terms of the value?
Well, you can think of it as the value is just encoding the action potential frequency itself.
Of course, the minimum frequency is zero and it's not possible to have a negative frequency.
That's why the minimum value of the value is zero.
Then as the inputs into the neuron get larger because they are being stimulated more, the receiving
neuron also gets stimulated more increasing its frequency.
Now, one thing to note is that we can go even deeper than this, although this is not part of mainstream
deep learning quite yet.
As a side note, if you don't want to listen to this part, it's not required.
It's something that I think is interesting to those of us who are interested in modeling brains.
But if you were a hardcore statistician, then perhaps you may think differently.
What we know based on neuroscience is that unlike the Relu activation action, potential frequency is
not linearly related to its input stimuli.
Instead, we have a non-linear relationship.
It can be modeled using the log function or a root function like the square root.
A good way to understand this is with sound.
Something that is twice as loud does not sound twice as loud.
Using measurements of sound intensity.
This is the intuition behind the decibel scale.
As you recall, the decibel scale is a logarithmic scale.
For example, a 90 decibel sound would be like sitting inside a moving bus.
A 100 decibel sound would be like standing by an electric saw.
A 110 decibel sound would be like a loud orchestra.
A 120 decibel sound would be like standing near a jet engine.
A 130dB sound would be like being close to artillery fire.
And this value is the threshold of pain.
So when you hear a sound this loud, it actually hurts physically.
To put that in perspective, 100dB is ten times more powerful than 90dB.
110dB is 100 times more powerful.
120dB is 1000 times more powerful and one 30dB is 10,000 times more powerful.
Some work has been done to experiment with activation functions that more accurately model the frequency
characteristics of real neurons such as the brew.
But as a whole, they haven't yet caught on in the deep learning community.
I've attached the paper on the brew to extra reading dot txt in case you want to check it out.
The author reports that the brew activation function led to better results than the value and the blue,
so it may be something worth trying in your own project.

--- Multiclass Classification ---

In this lecture, we are going to discuss another very important topic, Multi-class classification.
Previously we discussed binary classification, which used a sigmoid at the output.
We also had sigmoids for the hidden layer activation functions, but we just got rid of that in favor
of the Relu, which tends to work better for the output.
However, if we are doing binary classification, the sigmoid is still the right choice.
Binary classification has many applications we can predict disease or notices.
Is the user fraudulent or not?
Will the user click my ad or not?
Will the user accept a friend request or not?
And so on and so forth.
But there are situations that binary classification cannot handle, such as when we might have multiple
categorical outcomes.
Let's think of some examples.
One example is optical character recognition or handwriting recognition.
If we want to recognize alphanumeric characters, then there are at least 36 possibilities ten digits
and 26 letters.
What about speech recognition?
If we want to recognize words, then each word counts as a separate category.
Image classification is another one.
And for a long time this was the standard benchmark which proved that deep learning worked better than
other machine learning models.
You may have heard of a famous dataset called the ImageNet Dataset, which is made up of millions of
images and a thousand categories such as bagel vending machine and soccer ball.
Every year there is a contest to see who can build the best image net classifier.
And of course, these are all deep learning models.
So how does Multi-class classification work?
Well, suppose we have just calculated the activation at the final layer of our neural network, but
have not yet applied the activation function.
So we have a superscript l equal to W superscript l transpose dot z superscript L minus one.
So that's the Z at the previous layer, plus B superscript l.
It's important to remember that if the layer size is K, then A superscript L is a vector of size k.
What we would like to do is map these values to probabilities for each of the k possible output categories.
Okay, so that's your first lesson here.
If we have k possible outcomes, then we should have k output nodes.
The question now is what function can actually map the vector A superscript l to probability values?
Let's think about what the requirements are for these probabilities.
We need a probability distribution over K distinct values.
As a side note, we assign these values the integers zero to k minus one.
First, since they are probabilities, they must be non-negative.
In other words, they must be greater than or equal to zero.
But there is also an upper limit.
The maximum value of a probability is one.
But more importantly, for this to be a proper probability distribution, all the possible different
values must sum to one.
So the second requirement is that all the probabilities must sum to one.
Luckily, there is a function that accomplishes exactly this.
It is called the Softmax function.
First, let's drop the superscript L on the activation A for now so we can just say A is a vector of
size K.
Then the softmax of this vector A is the exponential of a divided by the sum of the exponential of a
across each component.
As you can see, this satisfies both of our requirements.
The exponential of any number is always positive, so therefore each probability must be non-negative.
Second, because the denominator is just the sum of all the possible values of the numerator.
Then if we sum all the possible values of the numerator, we just get the same sum and therefore we
get the same sum on the top and the bottom and they cancel out to give us one.
Now that we know how the Softmax works, let's answer the most important question.
How do we use it in TensorFlow?
Well, just like most of the other functions we've applied so far, it's very simple.
We just pass in the string Softmax.
In other words, the only requirement is that you spell it correctly.
Of course you can always implement the softmax yourself, but in TensorFlow there's no need.
As a side note, the Softmax is considered an activation function, but unlike the Relu sigmoid and
Tanh H, it is not meant for hidden layer activations.
If you want to try using the Softmax as a hidden layer activation you are most welcome to, but you'll
generally find that it doesn't work that well.
So the Softmax is technically an activation function, but we normally only use it when we're trying
to get an output probability from a vector of activation values such as at the end of a neural network.
The last thing I want to do in this lecture is summarize the three types of tasks we've encountered
so far that neural networks can perform and their corresponding activation functions.
Two of them you already saw in the previous section of this course, and now we have a third.
So first we have regression where we use no activation function at all.
Sometimes we also call this the identity function, which is just a fancy way of saying a function that
returns its input.
Second, we have binary classification where we use the sigmoid activation function using the sigmoid.
We only need one output node because since we know the probability of the output being one, the probability
of the output being zero is just one minus that.
This is the same type of thing you see when you're looking at the Bernoulli distribution versus the
categorical distribution.
The Bernoulli distribution is a categorical distribution where there can only be two outcomes.
Thus it only has one parameter.
The categorical distribution.
On the other hand, if it has k outcomes, it also has K parameters.
And that brings us to our third scenario, which is multi-class classification.
For this we use the Softmax activation function.
One important thing to mention that sometimes confuses people is that these three activation functions
apply whether you are using a feedforward neural network, a linear model, or even a more complicated
network like a CNN or an RNN.
It doesn't matter what kind of model you have.
These three activation functions always correspond to these three tasks.
So, for example, if I don't want to use a neural network, but just a simple linear classifier for
multiple classes, then I would have just a single dense layer with a softmax activation.
This is still called logistic regression, by the way.
It's just not binary logistic regression.
Instead, it's called multi-class logistic regression.
It's important to remember that what came before the final layer in the model is not related to the
task.
You can have a CNN or an RNN or any other kind of complicated neural network.
It is still the case that the final layer of the network will be a dense layer with one of these three
activation functions corresponding to the task being done.
Another important thing to remember is that the softmax function is more general, whereas the sigmoid
can only handle binary classification.
The Softmax function can handle multiclass classification, which includes binary classification.
So it's possible if you want to just use the softmax for all types of classification.
If you set K equals two, then you're doing binary classification.
It's just that in this scenario your outputs are a bit redundant because you already know that they
both sum to one and hence only one of them is really needed.

--- How to Represent Images ---

In this lecture.
We are going to switch gears a bit and discuss the kind of data we'll be working with next.
Previously, we discussed topics related to the model itself how a neural network is structured and
the different kinds of functions involved.
That's the model.
But just as important is the data where deep learning and neural networks really shine is on unstructured
data such as images, sound and text.
And so for the next code example, we'll be moving on to classifying images.
Of course, this presents a challenge.
Previously you learn my rule machine learning is nothing but a geometry problem, and all data is the
same.
We looked at some pretty simple examples with tabular data, such as predicting whether a student would
pass or fail an exam based on how many hours they spent studying and how many hours they spent playing
video games.
Another example was predicting salary based on factors such as years of experience and degree type.
The question now is if your input data X is an image, how does that fit into this picture?
As usual, we're going to start by doing the dumbest thing possible.
But first, we have to understand how images are represented in a computer in the first place.
So if we just look at an image, let's say the famous Lena image, we can see that the image has two
properties.
Height and width.
Therefore, your first thought might be let's store this in a matrix.
The next question is what do we store in this matrix?
Well, suppose I have a matrix A and I'm looking at the position ij.
This refers to the ith row and the jth column of the image.
You might think, okay, so IJ should store the color that appears at this exact position of the image
at position IJ.
The next problem we have to consider is how do we store colors?
In fact, there are quite a few ways to store colors in computers, but in this course we'll only discuss
the most common scheme called RGB.
This stands for Red, Green, Blue.
Now, I want you to think back to kindergarten.
Yes.
All the way back to kindergarten.
I bet you didn't know that what you learned there would come in handy.
Now, if you forgot what you learned in kindergarten, well, I'm not sure if I can help you.
You recall that there's this idea of primary colors.
Primary colors are a set of colors from which we can form other colors by combining them.
A common set of primary colors is red, blue and yellow.
Although red, green and blue also makes a set of primary colors.
So what do you get if you mix red and blue?
You get purple.
What do you get if you mix blue and yellow?
You get green.
What happens if you mix red and yellow?
You get orange.
Now, this one you might not know, but if you mix all the primary colors together, you get white.
In fact, from the primary colors, it's possible to combine them in different proportions to get any
color.
So how do we represent colors?
Well, in fact, it's not just a number.
It's a set of three numbers.
This set of three numbers tells us how much red, how much green and how much blue.
By mixing these colors in different proportions, we can get different colors.
So what does this tell us about how to represent images?
Well, in addition to height and width, we need another dimension always of size three, which represents
the three components of color.
So our image will actually be a three dimensional tensor, a IJK.
This is the component of the image representing the ith row, the Jth column and the KTH color.
The next important thing we have to discuss about how images are stored in computers has to do with
quantization.
Physically, we know that color is light and we measure it with light intensity.
It's a physical value in the real world and therefore it's pretty much continuous.
If it's continuous, that means it has an infinite number of possible values.
At the same time, we know that computers don't have infinite precision.
And even if they did, we might not want to make use of it because that would make our image files take
up lots of space.
Well, it just so happens that people have already figured out that eight bits is good enough.
Eight bits gives us two to the eight possible values, which is equal to 256 possible values.
These are encoded with the numbers zero up to 255.
So in total, considering that we have the red, green and blue channels, this gives us two to the
power, eight times, two to the power, eight times two to the power, eight possible colors, which
is equal to about 16.8 million.
Now using this, we can actually calculate how much space it would take to store a raw image.
Suppose we have an image of size 500 by 500.
Then the number of bits it requires would be 500 times.
500 times three times eight.
That's 6 million bits.
If we divide that by eight to get bytes, we get 750,000 bytes.
That's equal to about 732kB, which is almost one megabyte.
You might realize that this is quite large for a 500 by 500 image, which is why we have image compression
algorithms such as Jpeg that help us store image files in a much more compact format.
As a side note, for those of you who are web developers, this is where hex colors come from.
These also happen to use the same color scheme and quantization.
So we have three bytes with each byte representing the value for the red, green and blue color channels.
Importantly, one byte can be represented with two hex digits.
Hex digits go from zero up to nine and then A up to F.
So it's a base 16 number system to base 16 numbers give us 256 possible values.
So that's why we need to base 16 numbers or two hex numbers to represent one byte.
Then a color which is represented by three of these bytes would require six hex digits.
So that's why when you're coding in HTML, colors are represented by six digits where the digits can
be zero up to nine and A up to F.
Now we can simplify this a little bit.
If we have an image that does not have color, we call these grayscale images because each pixel can
only be black, white or something in between.
Typically we assign black the value of zero and white, the value of 255.
Any value in between is a different shade of gray.
And because we only need one value instead of three, grayscale images are stored in two dimensional
arrays rather than three dimensional arrays.
In Matplotlib.
If you try to use the show function to plot a grayscale image, it will end up assigning its own color
scheme.
But note that this is not the true color of the image.
This is just a heat map where we assign one color such as red to the high values and then another color
such as blue to the low values.
So that's why you have to pass in cmap equals gray when you want to show a grayscale image.
The last thing I want to mention about how images are represented in computers is that sometimes we
don't use eight bit unsigned integers from 0 to 255.
If you recall, neural networks don't like values on such a large scale.
So for neural networks, it's more convenient to scale image values, to be floating points between
0 and 1.
Zero represents no intensity and one represents full intensity.
And yes, the astute among you might notice that if we represent images as values between 0 and 1,
these are not standardized values because they are not centered around zero.
There are always exceptions in deep learning for images.
This representation is particularly convenient because it's possible to interpret these values as probabilities.
As mentioned, there are always exceptions in deep learning.
So here is another one.
One of the most famous neural networks today is called Vgg, which is used for many computer vision
applications such as image classification.
Transfer learning Style Transfer and object detection.
Vgg also previously won the ImageNet contest in multiple categories, which, as you recall, is a competition
held every year for research groups to present state of the art image recognition models.
The interesting thing about Vgg is that the original authors did not scale the input data, but they
did shift it by its mean across each color channel.
Thus, for Vgg, images are centered around zero, but the range of values is still to 56.
So if you're using Vgg and TensorFlow, you'll also want to use the function tf.keras applications.
Vgg 16 dot Preprocessinput.
This ensures that your images are in the right format for this neural network.
So far we've discussed how images are represented in a computer.
However, this still begs the question how is an image represented as input into a neural network?
If you recall, our input data is usually represented by a matrix X of size, N by D, where N is the
number of samples and D is the number of features.
But this doesn't make sense in our case if we have an image sample.
How can it have D features?
An image itself is a three dimensional object.
Therefore, it would seem that in order to represent a data set of images, we would need to have a
four dimensional tensor of shape and by H, by W by C, where N is the number of samples.
H is the image height, w is the image width, and C is the number of color channels, which is always
three.
Now, this is a little bit of foreshadowing since this is what we'll do with later.
But for now, we are going to do something simpler.
To keep things simple.
Let's say we have a black and white image of size.
Three by three.
A very tiny image.
Well, we can just number each pixel in a zig zag fashion, going from left to right and top to bottom.
So we have pixel one pixel, two pixel three all the way up to pixel nine in the bottom right corner.
Well, if we want to represent this as a vector, why not just line them up in a row and call it a vector?
So we have a vector of size nine containing pixel one pixel, two pixel three all the way up to pixel
nine.
We call this process flattening.
In this way, we can still store all of our images in an N by D array.
Here N is the number of samples and D is height times width, times color.
Each row of this matrix is still one sample, and each sample just happens to be a flattened image.
The features of the image just happen to be the individual pixel values.
And so here we encounter again this idea that all data is the same.
We saw previously how we can represent all sorts of different tabular data as an N by D array, from
breast cancer detection to Moore's Law and so on.
Now we can represent images in this format as well.
So it is a truly powerful concept.

--- Color Mixing Clarification ---

So at this point, some of you might be wondering, well, I remember when I was a kid, I mixed red
paint, blue paint and yellow paint together and I never got white.
So why is that?
So just as a side note, to clarify this, please be aware that not all color schemes result in a white
if you mix all the primary colors.
So this goes far beyond the scope of this course.
But importantly, it does work with RGB, which is what we will be using to represent images inside
computers.
So if you did try to mix a bunch of blue paint or red paint and yellow paint, the result would not
be white, but a sort of brownish color.
I encourage you to try this on your own if you're curious and also play around with RGB in a computer
as well.

--- Code Preparation (ANN) ---

At this point, we've done most of the legwork to describe what we are about to implement in code.
Now we just have to discuss a little more about what the code is going to look like and then we can
look at the actual code.
So let's again recap all the usual steps in the context of our data and our new model.
So step number one is to load in the data.
For the coming example, we'll be looking at the classic Mnist dataset, which is a dataset containing
handwritten digits.
Our job is to accept as input an image of the digit and then classify what the digit is.
Since there are ten possible digits zero up to nine, this is a multi-class classification problem.
Also note that even though these data are technically images, they don't come from actual image files
like JPEGs or PNGs.
Instead, the Mnist dataset is actually already included in TensorFlow, which makes things a bit more
convenient for us.
Also, they are grayscale images, so we don't have to worry about color right now.
Later in the course we'll look at how to load in actual image files.
Step number two is to build our model.
As per this section of the course.
The model is a feed forward neural network.
Basically, it's going to be multi-class logistic regression with a few more layers before it all chained
together sequentially.
Step number three is to train the model.
Now, this is the beauty of deep learning and deep learning frameworks.
There is zero additional work to do here.
We know that we have to specify the cost function, which ultimately is just a string, and then we
have to call the fit function.
It's that simple and nothing changes.
Step number four is to evaluate the model.
Again, this is exactly the same.
You could say that these steps are model agnostic.
They don't care what the actual form of the model is.
Step number five is to use the model to make predictions.
We'll do something interesting in this section, which will be to look at which predictions the neural
network is getting wrong.
This might give us some insight on why the neural network is doing what it's doing.
So let's start by breaking down step number one.
As mentioned, this data is going to come from the TensorFlow library itself, which will keep things
simple for us.
TensorFlow contains some data sets in the module tf.keras dot datasets.
Of course, one of these data sets is the famous Mnist dataset, which is a standard machine learning
benchmark of handwritten digits.
This is an image classification problem.
So the data are images and the targets are what digit the image is of.
Each image is the same size 28 by 28 pixels, which is equal to 784 pixels in total.
The Mnist dataset contains only grayscale images, so the images are just of size 28 by 28, not 28
by 28 by three, which is what they would be if they had color.
Luckily, when we load in our data by calling the load data function, it returns X, train y train
X test and y test already converted into numpy arrays and split into train and test sets.
So x train is of shape n by 28 by 28, whereas y train is a 1D vector of length.
N.
X test is of size n test by 28 by 28 and y test is a 1D vector of length n test.
The image pixels are stored as integers from 0 to 255, so we'll have to scale them to go from 0 to
1.
One important note is that we're not going to convert the input data into matrices of size N by D,
or in other words, two dimensional arrays before passing them into the neural network.
As you can see, we can use Keras itself to handle this.
Step number two is to instantiate the model.
As mentioned previously, Keras has functionality for handling input data, which are shapes like images
and converting that into the usual two dimensional N by D input data that we typically expect in a machine
learning model.
Specifically, this is through a special layer called Flatten.
Flatten doesn't have to have any input arguments, but since it's the first layer of the sequential
model we have to pass in the input shape anyway, which as you know, is 28 by 28.
Next we specify the neural network layers.
First, we have a layer of type dense with 128 hidden units and the activation function.
Relu.
Now, you might be wondering, why did I choose the value 128?
And of course there is no special reason.
This is just a matter of hyper parameter selection for which you should use trial and error as well
as past experience to guide your decisions.
Also note random search is another popular method for Hyperparameter selection.
Next, we add a dropout layer, which is a type of regularization in neural networks.
Intuitively, you can think of it as you don't want the neural network to depend on any single input
too strongly.
This is the machine learning equivalent of putting all your eggs in one basket.
So what dropout does is it randomly drops input nodes by setting them to zero so that they have no influence
on the next layer.
And because it's random, the nodes that are dropped will change every time we look at the data.
Even if we see the same sample twice, the nodes that are dropped will be different each time.
This will teach the neural network layer to learn more evenly from all its inputs.
The one argument into the dropout layer is the dropout probability, which we've set to 20%.
This means that every time we go through the neural network, there's a 20% chance of dropping a node
in that layer or equivalently setting it to zero.
Once we've done our dropout regularization, we add a second dense layer.
As mentioned previously, this is Multi-class classification, and for Multi-class classification we
use the Softmax activation function.
And of course there are ten classes because there are ten digits.
Step number three is to train the model.
As you know already, this just means calling the compile function to specify the optimizer loss and
metrics and then calling the fit function.
The one thing that changes with this example is that we'll be using a loss called the sparse categorical
cross entropy.
Now, this might seem strange to you.
What does this mean?
Why is it sparse?
To understand this, you have to understand a little more about the cross entropy loss function.
First, let's start with what the loss function looks like for a single sample.
Recall that for a single sample we have k different probability values corresponding to the k possible
classes.
In the Mnist dataset this is ten.
In order to build a loss for this sample, we are going to sum up all the log probabilities multiplied
by a one hot encoded version of the target.
To keep our notation compact, let's call y hat the probability that y equals k given x, the target
is y.
One hot encoding means that if the value of the target is equal to K, then the one hot encoded version
of Y.
Let's call that Y superscript zero H is an array of size big K with all zeros except with a one at location.
Little K.
Note that we will not discuss the cross entropy in depth in this lecture.
If you would like to learn more about it, I would suggest checking out the in depth section of this
course focusing on loss functions.
At the very least, if you want to check that this loss function makes sense, that would be a good
idea.
So imagine that our prediction is perfect for the value of K where y superscript zero h sub k equals
one.
We also have y hat sub k, also one.
So both the target and the prediction are one.
Then we have for the cost minus one times log of one which is equal to zero.
Now suppose our prediction is totally wrong, so that for the target k y hat sub k equals zero.
Then we have minus one times log of zero, which is equal to infinity.
And you can do this for any number in between.
So y hat could be 0.9, 0.8, 0.5 and so forth.
And you should find that the cost increases as y hat sub K becomes more and more wrong or further and
further away from one.
Therefore, we can confirm that this loss function is zero when our prediction is perfect and infinity
when we are as wrong as possible.
Now, this still doesn't explain why we have this loss function called sparse categorical cross entropy.
Well, we looked at so far is just the categorical cross entropy.
So why is it sparse?
To understand this, you have to think about shapes using the loss function as we just described it.
We require that both the one hot encoded target as well as the prediction are arrays of size.
Big K.
That seems suboptimal because we know that our target is just a single integer.
We shouldn't need an entire array of size Big K to represent it, especially since it's an array of
mostly zeros except one value, which is a one.
In fact, that's the idea behind the sparse categorical cross entropy.
The name comes from the fact that the one hot array is sparse.
Sparse means an array that contains mostly zeros.
The key is recognizing that out of all the big K values, only one of them matters.
The one corresponding to the target.
It's the only one that actually affects the loss.
For all the other probabilities, you're taking the log and then multiplying by zero, which obviously
gives you zero.
Therefore, they do not contribute to the loss at all.
And therefore we shouldn't have to calculate this summation.
Instead.
Suppose our non one hot encoded target y is equal to K star.
Then the cross entropy loss is just minus log of y hat of K star.
In other words, all we need to do is index y hat at K Star and forget about all the other values contained
in y hat.
Now, instead of doing a summation over all big K values, we just need to calculate one value.
And furthermore, we don't need to one hot encode the targets which reduces the size required to store
them by a factor of Big K.
Note that this works because the labels are by convention assigned to be integers.
So it doesn't matter if your labels are car truck and boat or dog and cat and giraffe.
In a computer we still call them zero one, two and so forth.
If we're talking about math, we usually start indexing by one.
But this is irrelevant at the moment because we're talking about how we're going to implement this in
code.
Finally, step four and step five are to evaluate the model and make predictions using the model.
Luckily, this doesn't change when you use a different model.
You still call model dot evaluate and model dot predict.
This corresponds to another rule of mine, which is that all machine learning interfaces are the same.
It doesn't matter if you're using naive Bayes neural networks or random forests, you always have the
same API with the same functions.
The only thing that changed for us in this lecture is how we built the model, because in TensorFlow
we have to actually build the model.

algorithm.
You want to be able to see where it succeeds and where it fails.
The important key word there is see?
We'll be creating a data set which we can actually visualize and look at so we can observe that the
neural network will create a prediction surface that corresponds to the true function that we generated
the data from.
Of course, don't take my word for it that this is important.
One great example of this is in the field of clustering.

examples of this.
In particular, it shows a set of some pretty contrived two dimensional data, and then it compares
how each algorithm clusters this data.
By doing this, we can see where each algorithm is successful and where it is not.
In fact, this is the same thing you saw in the TensorFlow playground without being able to see the
decision boundary and the corresponding data with your own eyes.
You can imagine that this website would be much less engaging.
