--- What is Convolution? (part 1) ---

In this lecture we are going to discuss convolution in preparation for building convolutional neural
networks.
Of course, given the name, it's pretty obvious that a convolutional neural network is a neural network
with convolution.
And so in order to understand CNNs, we must first understand convolution.
First I want to mention that people sometimes treat convolution as this mysterious thing.
But as with our discussion of machine learning, we want to take the dumb as possible approach.
Sure, convolution can be complex.
It's a central operation in signal processing and computer vision, but in fact it's quite simple.
There are only two requirements.
First, can you add?
Second, can you multiply?
If your answer to these two questions is yes, then you understand convolution.
Believe it or not, convolution is just adding and multiplying.
So let's start by not doing any math.
Let's just look at convolution qualitatively with convolution.
There are three objects you want to pay attention to.
First is the input image.
Second, there's the filter.
Note that another name for the filter is the kernel.
So we're going to use those two terms interchangeably.
Finally, there's the output image.
The output image is what you get when you convolve the input image with the filter.
In other words, if I perform the convolution operation on the input image and the filter, I get the
output image.
The mathematical symbol for convolution is a star or asterisk.
Not to be confused with the asterisk that we use in computer programming for multiplication.
So if you're writing convolution in code, it will not be an asterisk because in code we already use
the asterisk for multiplication.
It's helpful to think of some examples of convolution to understand what it can do.
There are two examples I really like.
The first example is blurring.
So the input image is just the original image and the output image is a blurred version of that image.
You mightrillionecognize this operation from applications such as Photoshop.
The second example I really like is edge detection.
As input, we have the original image and as output we get white lines where there are edges in the
original image and we get black where there are no edges.
So the output image highlights all the edges in the original input image.
So that's your first lesson on how to view convolution.
This perspective is that convolution is an image modifier.
The input is the original image, and the output is a modified or transformed version of that image.
In other words, you might think of this as a feature transformation on the input image.
You know, that sounds curiously like what a neural network does.
In any case, what makes blurring an edge detection actually work if they are both convolution?
What makes one convolution different from another convolution?
Well, the answer is the filter.
When you use a Gaussian filter, this blurs the image.
When you use an edge detection filter, you get edge detection.
You can think of that as sharpening the image.
Your next question might be how do we find or design these filters in the first place?
That's something we'll discuss later in this section.
But the answer, as you might have come to expect, is just another dumb as possible approach.
Okay, so let's get into the nitty gritty of how convolution works.
I promised you that all you need to know is how to add and multiply.
Let's see if that's true.
We're going to use very tiny images for this example, although in reality, the actual images we work
with will be much bigger.
This is just so we can feasibly do these calculations by hand.
So let's start with an image zero, ten, ten, zero, 20, 30, 30, 20, ten, 20, 2010 and 0550.
And we also have the filter.
1002.
So how do we convolve this image with this filter?
Let's imagine overlaying the filter at the upper left corner of the image.
Then all we need to do is element wise multiplication and add up all the results.
So we get one times zero plus zero times ten plus zero times 20 plus 30 times two.
That is equal to 60.
This gives us our first output in the output image.
And as promised, all you needed to do was multiply and add.
Now let's move our filter over one space to the right.
And then let's do our element wise multiplication and add up the results again.
So we get one times ten plus zero times ten plus zero times 30 plus two times 30.
That's 70.
This gives us our second output, which goes to the right of the first output.
Now let's move the filter over one more space to the right and do the same operation again.
We get one times ten plus zero times zero plus zero times 30 plus two times 20, and that's 50.
This gives us our third output, which goes to the right of the first two outputs.
Now you realize that there's no more space to move our filter to the right anymore.
So let's instead zigzag back to the left.
But go down one row and then let's repeat our calculation.
We get one times 20 plus zero times 30 plus zero times ten plus two times 20.
That's equal to 60.
This gives us our first output in the second row.
So you can see that the output location corresponds to where we've placed the filter along the original
image.
So we're not going to go through the rest of the calculations since that would be a bit redundant.
But what I want you to do is if you don't understand this, do the rest of the calculations by hand
to make sure that these results are correct.
So we have 60, 70, 50, 60, 70, 50, and then 20, 30.
20.
One helpful exercise to get a better idea of how convolution works is to write pseudocode and even real
code to implement the algorithm we just described.
This will help us uncover a few hidden details you may have not considered.
Just by starting the code, you realize one thing you need to take care of, which is what size should
I initialize the output array as?
If you recall in our example, the input image had length four while the kernel had length two.
The output had length three.
So what's the pattern?
I claim that if you have an array of length n and a filter of length k, then there are n minus k plus
one distinct possible positions you can put the filter into.
You might want to draw this on paper if you don't see right away why this is true.
So the first step in our pseudocode is to initialize the output array.
But here's another detail you may have not considered.
The output height will be the input height minus kernel height plus one.
The output width will be the input width minus kernel width plus one.
But in our example, both our image and kernel were square.
So you might be wondering, is this always the case?
What's the convention?
And the answer is that for images, usually these are not square.
This just has to do with cameras and screens.
Most screens, like your computer screen and your TV screen are not square, and therefore cameras do
not take square pictures.
Therefore, images we find in the wild that we want to use as data are typically also not square.
Some neural networks do use square images for convenience, so when you build a dataset, you make them
square.
And one example of this is Mnist, which we've already seen.
On the other hand, kernels are almost always square by convention.
Let's move on.
The next step is just to fill in the output array by performing the convolution algorithm.
So first we loop through zero up to output height with the index i.
Inside that we looped through zero up to output width with the index j.
So the pair i j will always index our output.
Then we loop through each position of the kernel.
So we have I going from zero up to kernel height and we have j j going from zero up to kernel width.
Finally, inside all these loops we have our main calculation which as promised is just multiplication
and addition.
We multiply the input image at position i plus i j plus j j by the kernel at position i j j.
And we add this result to the output image at i j.
Now it's okay to accumulate using plus equals because we initialize the output image to be all zeros.
As an exercise.
You might want to try and put this into code so that you can confirm that it works and returns the expected
result.
The inner part of the pseudocode is key because it helps us understand the equation that defines convolution.
So here we can see that the ijth entry of the output a convolved with W is the sum over I prime and
the sum over j prime of a at I plus I prime j plus j prime times w at i Prime j Prime.
Now you might ask why are we looking at these complicated equations if TensorFlow already does all this
work for us?
And the answer is that this will help you immensely in understanding the different perspectives on convolution
that we are going to discuss later.
Moreover, if you're a curious person and you go on Wikipedia to read about convolution, you'll see
something very similar to this.
In this example, you can think of X as the filter.
Y is the input image and Z is the output image.
Now, you might notice something weird about this equation from Wikipedia, which is that instead of
plus signs, we have minus signs.
Why is that?
Is the lazy programmer wrong?
And the answer is, in fact, all of deep learning is wrong.
But for better or worse, that's just the way we do things.
In the end, it doesn't make a difference because the filters we use will be learned using gradient
descent, in other words, automatically.
So the process of finding the filter will be done automatically using gradient descent, which will
find the best values that optimize our loss function.
So it doesn't matter if the filter is reversed or not.
In fact, if you use a library like scipy, you'll notice that it already has a function called Convolve
2D.
The problem is, if you use this function as is, you'll get a totally different answer than we did.
And as a side note, you might want to try that yourself to confirm that what I'm saying is true.
Now this is because Convolve 2D does a proper convolution and not the deep learning version of convolution
with plus instead of minus.
In order to make Scipy's convolution work the same way, we have to flip the filter both horizontally
and vertically and set the mode argument equal to valid.
Now, as a side note, convolution is a commutative operation.
A convolve with W is the same as w convolved with a.
Therefore it doesn't matter which input we flip.
In fact, what we are doing in deep learning is actually known as the cross correlation.
I actually think this is a much more helpful and descriptive name compared to convolution.
Convolution in and of itself probably doesn't mean much to you, but the word correlation does.
You probably think of the word correlated as sameness.
So if I say X and Y are correlated, that means to you that there's some degree of similarity between
X and Y.
Therefore, you might think of CNN's as a correlation neural network rather than a convolutional neural
network.
The only difference between correlation and convolution is that convolution reverses the orientation
of the filter, whereas correlation does not.
The final topic I want to talk about in this lecture is this mode argument.
To understand this, it's helpful to look at these animations which kind of summarize how convolution
works.
Basically, you're sliding the filter across every possible position in the input image.
In this animation, the motion of the filter is bounded by the edges of the image.
Because of this, the output image is always smaller than the input image.
But you might wonder what if I want the output image to be the same size as the input image?
In this case, what you can do is add padding.
This is equivalent to adding a virtual array of zeros around the input image so that the filter can
extend out to those values.
The reason I say it's virtual is because you wouldn't really want to allocate the space in code.
There's no reason to, since you already know that anything multiplied by zero is still zero.
So in other words, to quote unquote add padding, you can just pretend there are zeros surrounding
the input image, as many zeros as you need to ensure that the output size is equal to the input size.
You may have noticed, however, that even with padding, we still lose some information.
What I mean by that is there are outputs we could calculate if we extended the padding even further,
that would be non-zero outputs.
So another thing you can do which is not as common these days is to extend the padding further so that
you can catch all these non-zero outputs.
This results in an output size of N plus K minus one If your input image has length n and your filter
has length k.
Again, you should draw this out on paper if you're not convinced this is true.
To summarize the three modes of convolution, let's look at this table.
The first one we discussed was called valid convolution.
This applies when the kernel can only touch the original input image.
The output size is n minus k plus one.
The second one we discussed was called same convolution.
In this scenario, we add some padding just enough so that the output size is the same as the input
size.
N.
The third mode we discussed is called full convolution.
In this scenario, we extend the filter out as far as possible so that at least one point on the filter
overlaps with one point on the input image.
The output size is n plus K minus one.
Normally in deep learning we use valid or same convolutions.

--- What is Convolution? (part 2) ---

In this lecture, we are going to continue our discussion of convolution by looking at a totally different
perspective on how it works.
This is very helpful for understanding convolution, but it doesn't teach you anything new mechanically.
So this lecture is optional if you want to move on and just learn about how to make scenes.
Something I mentioned very often is victimization.
We like to victimize operations in code because using numpy functions is a lot more efficient than writing
your own python for loops.
The pattern you're looking for when you want to vector arise in operation is usually of the form of
a dot product.
A dot product is an element y's multiplication and then the summation of those results.
So whenever you see the sum over ei of I times by that's a dot products.
This also applies to matrix multiplication, since if A and B are matrices, then the matrix multiplication
of A and B is just a dot b.
You'll notice that for convolution we yet again have something similar.
The only difference is that there are two summations.
However, this is not really a relevant detail since the outcome is still the same.
Instead of summing over one axis or something over two axes.
But it's still an element.
Why some in add?
The question is why is the DOT product important?
One definition of the DOT product other than the element why some in ad is that it's the magnitude of
aid multiplied by the magnitude of B multiplied by the cosine of the angle between A and B.
We sometimes call this the cosine similarity or cosine distance, depending on the sign that you use.
So how does this work?
Geometrically.
Consider just the angle for a moment.
If the angle between the two vectors is zero, then the cosine of that angle is one.
That's the maximum value of the cosine.
Now imagine that the angle between two vectors is 90 degrees.
Then the cosine of that angle is zero.
Finally imagine that the angle between a two vectors is 180 degrees.
This is basically as far apart as possible.
Then the cosine of that angle is minus one, which is the minimum value of the cosine.
So if you're just using raw cosine, then it's a similarity.
The larger the number, the closer the two vectors are.
The smaller the number, the further away they are.
The maximum value when A and B are parallel is one.
The minimum value when A and B are anti parallel is minus one.
And if A and B are orthogonal, then the cosine similarity is just zero.
Okay.
So why is that important?
Consider now how you would find that the cosine of the angle between two vectors that's just a be divided
by the magnitude of a and the magnitude of B.
So I just rearrange the equation that we had before.
Now let's compare this to another popular measurement, the Pearson correlation.
The Pearson correlation is defined as what you see here.
But notice how similar this is to cosine similarity.
The only difference is that the Pearson correlation uses mean subtraction.
And so now you have two hints that convolution is really correlation.
The first one from before was that we were actually doing what is called the cross correlation.
And second, now you have that the DOT product is actually very similar to the Pearson correlation.
So I hope that by this point you are convinced that the DOD product, while it seems like an abstract
concept, can be thought of as a correlation measure.
It tells me how correlated is the first thing with the second thing.
If they are highly, positively correlated, then the DOT products should be large and positive.
That means two vectors pointing in nearly the same direction.
If they are highly negatively correlated, then the DOT product should be large and negative.
That means the two vectors are pointing in nearly opposite direction.
Finally, if the two vectors are orthogonal or at right angles, then the DOT product should be zero.
The reason why this is important is you don't have to think of a filter as an abstract concept.
In fact, it's just a pattern finder.
This actually makes the term filter make a lot more sense.
It filters out everything not related to the pattern contained in the filter by setting them to zero
and keeps everything that is related to the pattern.
So what convolution is doing is it's passing this filter along each point on the original input image
and sliding it along.
At each point it asks Is the pattern, here is the pattern, here is the pattern here and so forth.
Then it gives us a high number in the positions where the pattern is found and then a small number where
the pattern is not found.
And thus, this is your first alternative perspective on convolution.
It's just a sliding pattern finder that passes through an entire image looking for a particular pattern.

--- What is Convolution? (part 3) ---

In this lecture, we are going to continue our discussion on alternative perspectives of convolution.
Again, this is very helpful for understanding convolution, but it doesn't teach you anything new mechanically.
So this lecture is optional.
If you want to just move on and learn about how to make scenes.
So in this lecture, what we want to talk about is the equivalence of convolution and a matrix multiplication.
In other words, in what scenario are they actually the same thing?
Let's do an example.
This is easier to see if we only do a one dimensional convolution.
So let's first go over how that would work.
This should be easy since two dimensional convolution is just a generalization of this.
So let's start with a one dimensional image.
A equals a one, two, three, four.
We also have a filter.
W equals W one.
W two.
Then the convolution output will be B, which is a key involved with W, and the elements are a one
times w one plus a two times w2a2 times w one plus a three times W two and a three times w one plus
a four times w two.
So in general we can write one dimensional convolution as the sum from ei prime equals one up to K of
at ei plus prime times w of prime.
You'll notice that this is the same equation we had for a two dimensional convolution, just with the
second index dropped.
Using this, it's pretty easy to see how we would implement the same thing using matrix multiplication.
What we've done here is we created a matrix where we repeat the filter along each row, but on each
row we shift it to the right by one space.
Now, I recommend you do this by hand so you can see that it works.
If you multiply the matrix by the original input vector A, you get the correct output of the convolution.
So what's the lesson here?
It's that by repeating the same filter again and again inside a matrix, we can implement convolution
without actually doing convolution.
Instead, we can just use matrix multiplication.
But there is another problem with this particular method of doing matrix multiplication, and this is
that the equivalent matrix repeats the filter multiple times.
So the result is that the matrix takes up a lot more space than the original filter, which was just
a two element array originally.
In other words, while this was a helpful perspective, you don't necessarily want to implement convolution
this way.
Instead, it's helpful to look at this from the opposite direction.
What if there are instances where instead of doing a full matrix multiplication, we can replace it
with convolution?
This is the idea behind parameter sharing or weight sharing.
Think of it this way Inside a neural network, we are constantly doing this operation A equals W transpose
X Here A is the output activation and x is the input feature vector W is the weight matrix.
Well, what if instead of a full weight matrix, we just had the same two weights repeating over and
over again?
Then we have less parameters.
Our neural network takes up less memory in RAM, and thus we can make the computation more efficient.
In other words, convolution saves both space and time by using less weights.
Why might we want to do this?
Consider what we did in the previous section where we had a fully connected neural network looking at
a single image.
Luckily, those images were just gray scale images of size 28 by 28, which gives us 784 features.
But what if we had a slightly larger image and add color?
The key for ten data set is one such example.
Those images are 32 by 30, two by three.
In this case, we have 3072 features.
That's quite a large increase from 784.
And keep in mind a 32 by 32 image is quite modest in size, to say the least.
Modern scenes such as VG look at images of size 224 by 224.
If you use a full weight matrix, then you would have 150,528 features.
Suppose you're looking at an image with modern HD resolution that's 1280 by 720.
In this case you would have about 2.8 million features.
As you can imagine, this is much too large for a neural network.
But there is another good reason to use weight sharing, which is that you don't need different weights
for each part of the neural network.
Remember, that convolution is actually correlation and the filter is really a pattern finder.
In this scenario, we actually want the same filter to look at all locations on the image.
This is the idea behind Translational Invariance.
Suppose we are building a dog versus cat recognizer.
Here is an image containing a cat.
Here's another image containing a cat.
But what's the difference between these two images?
The only difference is that the cat is in a different position.
Now, if we use the fully connected or dense neural network, we would have to learn the weights for
each of these positions separately.
And on top of that, this neural network won't generalize.
Well, because if we come across the same cat, but in a new position, the neural network would have
failed to recognize it.
So, in fact, it's better to have a shared pattern finder because this pattern finder looks at all
locations on the image.
You don't need to learn the weights to look at a cat at every single possible point, which would actually
be infeasible.

--- Convolution on Color Images ---

In this lecture, we are going to extend our understanding of convolution so that it looks a little
more like what goes on in a neural network.
First, remember that in general, a neural network accepts as input color images.
We know that these are three dimensional objects height by width by color.
But for all the examples of convolution we just looked at, both the image and the filter were two dimensional.
So how can we reconcile this discrepancy?
Previously we conceptualized an image as a square and the filter also has a square.
Then we would slide this little square along the big square and do a multiply add, and that's convolution.
So if our image has three dimensions, we just go with the same analogy.
Our color image is a big box and our filter is a little box.
Then we slide this little box along the big box, and at each position we do a multiply add and that's
a convolution.
Here are the details.
At each position we used to sum over the height and width of the kernel because the kernel was 2D.
But now since the kernel is three D, we're going to sum over the Color Channel as well.
Note that both the kernel and the image for now have three color channels.
If you recall, that's red, green, blue.
The way that you can think of this is that instead of the filter being a grayscale pattern finder,
it's now a color pattern finder.
Suppose the filter is looking for a red circle.
So there is a circle in the red channel.
Now it's only going to match red circles.
It won't match blue circles or green circles.
This is opposed to the black and white filter, which has no color at all.
If it saw a circle, then the pattern would be found.
There's no concept of color in a black and white image.
Ultimately this picture is still not complete using what we just learned.
This is what would happen.
Our input image is a three dimensional object.
Height by width by three.
Our kernel is also a three dimensional object.
K by K by three.
The output of this convolution is only two dimensional height minus K plus one width minus K plus one.
And this is because we're summing over all three dimensions.
And so the third dimension kind of gets dotted out.
Now, there's nothing necessarily wrong with the output being two dimensional, although this should
offend your sensibilities to a certain extent.
Remember this idea of the uniformity of a neural network and how we have repeating structures?
The feedforward, A and N works because you can add any number of layers one dense layer followed by
another, followed by another.
This is okay because the input and the output are of the same type.
The input is a vector and the output is also a vector which can then be the input into the next layer.
But for convolution, so far this doesn't seem to work.
If the input is three dimensional, but the output is only two dimensional, then we do not have this
uniformity.
We can't plug in the same kind of convolution right after the first one, since the input is not the
same as the output.
But here's a question to consider.
Remember that the filter can be thought of as a pattern finder.
So it's looking for a particular pattern, or, in other words, looking for a particular feature.
But we know that in machine learning we have multiple features, just like how each hidden unit in a
neural network represents a different feature based on the inputs.
That kind of makes sense.
If we're looking at an image, we would want to identify multiple features in the image.
For example, let's say you're building a facial recognition network when a filter might be looking
for an AI and another filter might be looking for a nose.
So it makes sense then that if we want to find multiple features, we should have multiple filters to
find them.
So consider for a second what would happen if you had two filters acting on the same input image.
Call the input image a call the first filter one call the second filter w two.
The output of a convulse with one is v one and the output of a can involved with W two is b two.
Let's say for simplicity's sake that we're using same mode convolution so that b one and b to have the
same height in width as A, so of A is H by W by three, then B one is H by W and B two is also H by
W.
Now we have two outputs B one and B two, but with different features found in each.
What should we do with them?
Well, how about we simply stack them together?
Now all of a sudden our output is three dimensional.
It's height by width by two.
And in fact, we can add any number of filters to find any number of features.
So nose, eyes, lips, eyebrows, chin, so on.
Now our input looks like our output.
They are both three dimensional.
This means we can have multiple layers of convolution, one followed by the other in a repeating pattern,
just like we do with a and NS.
But of course, just like with the A and N dense layer, we want to valorize this operation so that
it can be done all in one go rather than doing, say, ten different convolutions and then stacking
them together.
So here is the formula that represents how to do a complete convolution in a deep neural network so
that both the input and the output can be three dimensional objects.
Now.
This is a lot to look at all at once.
So let's try to consider each part one step at a time.
First, let's consider the shape of everything.
We know that A will b h by W by c one.
You can think of c one as the number of color channels the image A has.
Then we have the filter W which has the shape C one by K, by K by C to note that this is now a four
dimensional tensor.
Then we have the output image.
B Assuming that we're doing same mode convolution, the height and width dimensions will be the same
H by W, but the third dimension will be C two.
So in our equation for the convolution, we can see that it's pretty much the same as before just with
more indices.
B IGC represents the image pixel value at a row, i.e. column J and color channel c.
Note that I use the term color loosely here because it doesn't really represent color anymore.
We can have an arbitrary number of features, so c two can be ten, 100 or 1000.
Conceptually, however, we're still just doing what we talked about previously, just individual convolutions
which output two dimensional images and then stacking all those images together to get a three dimensional
object.
So let's just summarize what we learned in this lecture, since that was a bit long.
First, we started with realizing that we only defined convolution for grayscale images and with corresponding
two dimensional filters.
This is, of course, not flexible enough for CNN's, which must accept color images as input.
So then we said, okay, let's just extend that by doing a three dimensional dot product between a color
image and a three dimensional filter with the same number of color channels.
So we're dotting over the Color Channel.
Next, we realize that this breaks uniformity so we can't stack layers together to build a deeper neural
network.
And this is deep learning, after all.
So we kind of need to be able to do that.
And this is because when you can evolve a 3D image with a3d filter, you still get a 2D image as output.
This is just like when you dot a1d vector by another one d vector, you get a scalar.
So we extended this by realizing that we don't just want one filter that's going to find one single
feature.
We need to find multiple features.
Therefore we need multiple filters which will output multiple to DX images, which of course can be
stacked into a3d image.
Then we vector arise this operation so that both the input and the output are three dimensional.
Now you might be wondering, is it correct to call the final dimension color?
That may be the case for the input into the neural network, which is always height by width by three.
But this breaks down at every other layer.
For example, if you have height by width by ten, then the ten dimensions don't really represent color.
But as we discussed conceptually, we're just finding different features in an image.
So our terminology changes to cover this more general case.
We call these feature maps.
This makes sense because you can think of each tutti image as a map, as in a literal map you can look
at which is the result of a convolution with some filter which is looking for some feature.
So this map is telling us where in the original image that this feature can be found.
So just like a map, the final output is just a stack of all these different maps, one map for each
feature.
And so the size of the final dimension is referred to as the number of channels or the number of feature
maps rather than colors.
Finally, I want to talk about how convolution is going to look.
As a neural network layer.
If you recall, you can think of convolution as just matrix multiplication, but with shared weights.
And as you know, in a feed forward and then we don't just have matrix multiplication at each layer,
we do some other things as well.
In particular, we can add a bias term and apply an activation function.
Well, a convolution layer works the same way.
We're going to add a bias term and apply an activation function.
Because remember, this allows us to find a nonlinear features or nonlinear functions of the input which
are more powerful than simple linear functions.
Importantly, we have to consider what the shape of the bias term should be, given that the output
of the convolution is a three dimensional image in a dense layer.
If we have a vector of size m then our bias term is also a vector of size.
M In other words, there is one scalar for each element in the result, but for convolution, the bias
term will not have the same shape as W involved with X, which is a three dimensional image.
Now you might think, isn't this equation invalid?
If B does not have the same shape as W, consult with X.
Technically, this shouldn't be allowed by the rules of matrix arithmetic.
If we want to add two matrices together, they should have the same shape.
Technically this is true, but this is conceptual only, and this will actually work in code due to
the rules of broadcasting.
So the bias vector b is only one dimensional and it has the size c two.
If W consult with X has a shape H by W by C two.
In other words, the same bias term applies to every pixel for each feature map.
As a side note, convolution is generally a commutative operation, so it doesn't really matter if you
write W Star X or x star w, although this is not entirely true in the deep learning case, since W
is four dimensional and X is three dimensional, nonetheless, this is just conceptual only.
So let's pretend that W comes first so that it looks more like the dense layer equation.
All right.
So now that you understand how convolution and a full convolution layer works in a neural network,
let's consider our savings.
Remember that one way to look at convolution is that it's parameter sharing.
Sharing parameters means that we use the same weights in multiple places, reducing the number of total
weights we need.
So let's do a quick calculation to determine our savings.
Suppose our input image is of size 32 by 32 by three, a modest size image.
Now let's suppose the size of the filter is three by five by five by 64.
So there are 64 output feature maps.
Then our output image will have the shape 28 by 28 by 64.
This is because 32 minus five plus one is 28.
And let's just assume we're using valid mode convolution.
The number of parameters we needed to compute this output is just three times, five times five times
64, which is 4800.
Now let's compare that to the case where we would use a feedforward neural network with a flattened
image like we did in the previous section.
Then our input would be 32 times, 32 times three, which is 3072.
Our output again would be of size 28 by 28 by 64, which is 50,176.
So if we use the dense layer, we would have a weight matrix of size 3000, 72 times 50,176, which
is approximately 154 million.
Now, that's a lot of parameters for just one layer of a neural network.
In fact, compared to using convolution, we have about 32,000 times more parameters.
As you can imagine, this would require a huge amount of RAM and a huge amount of compute.
It would take longer and would perform suboptimal because as we stated earlier, convolution is a pattern
finder.
We use the same filter to find the same pattern in multiple places without shared weights.
Every weight needs to learn the pattern at every location.
The pattern could appear, which is suboptimal.
This would be bad for a generalization.
Using this concept of convolution just being a part of a neural network layer, it's easy to see how
we are going to find these convolution filters through the process of training.
At this point, we've come quite a long way since our first introduction to convolution, where we had
the perspective that convolution is just an image modifier.
The examples we use where blurring and edge detection, we took an image and then convolution modified
it in some way.
But now we have a deeper understanding of convolution.
It's in fact, a pattern finder and a shared parameter matrix multiplication.
It's just a feature transformer.
In this way, it's just like the neural network layers we'd seen earlier in the course.
And so having this perspective, it becomes pretty obvious how these filters or feature transformers
will be found during the training process.
The answer is that it's all going to happen automatically when we call model fit.
This is going to end up doing gradient descent on our new weights and biases, which for convolution
just happen to be a different shape than what we had before in the dense layer.

--- CNN Architecture ---

Now that you understand how exactly a convolution layer works, including the bias term and activation
function, we can now consider the architecture of a convolutional neural network and why it's that
way.
So as a little bit of a history lesson, modern, CNNs essentially all originated from the same model,
the linear.
This is named after Yann LeCun, one of the original deep learning pioneers, along with Geoffrey Hinton
and Yoshua Bengio.
What we're going to do in this lecture is just start with what the architecture is and then go through
it piece by piece so that you understand why it is the way it is.
Okay.
So let's look at a typical CNN.
A typical CNN has two stages.
The first stage is a series of convolutional layers.
Importantly, these convolutional layers are usually followed by pooling layers, which is another type
of layer we'll discuss shortly.
The second stage is a series of dense layers, also called fully connected layers.
These are just our regular feedforward neural network, as you saw in the previous section.
If you recall, I said in the previous section that neural networks have this hierarchical structure.
Each layer is always looking at features that were found in the previous layer.
So you can even think of the first stage as just another feature transformer.
It's a feature transformer that specifically works on images and finds image features.
Then once it's found those image features, it passes those into a neural network, and then the neural
network does what it does best non-linear classification or regression.
So let's have a closer look at the convolutional stage.
Now I just added a new layer without really explaining it.
The pooling layer.
Why would we want to do that?
At a high level, pooling acts as a Downsampling operation.
What is Downsampling?
That's when you make a smaller image out of a bigger image.
So imagine your image is 100 by 100.
After pooling, if the pool size is two, then you would get a 50 by 50.
That's Downsampling by two.
Let's go over mechanically how this works and then we'll explain why it works and why we would want
to include it in our neural network.
So there are two kinds of pooling, max pooling and average pooling.
Choosing which one to use is, as you probably guessed, a hyper parameter choice.
Let's talk about max pooling first since I think this one's a bit more common.
The best way to see how it works is by example.
So if we start with an image, let's say it's a 4x4 and contains these numbers one, two, three, four,
five, six, seven, eight, 16, 15, 14, 13, 12, 11, ten, nine.
So just the numbers 1 to 16, but organized non sequentially.
What max pooling will do is take each two by two square of which there are four and just return the
maximum value in each of those squares.
So the max of one, two, five, six is six.
The max of three, four, seven and eight is eight.
The max of 16, 15, 12, 11 is 16 and the max of 14.
13.
Ten.
Nine is 14.
So the output image is six, eight, 16.
14.
As you might have guessed, average pooling is similar, but instead of taking the max, we take the
average.
So for the same input image, we have the following.
The average of one, two, five and six is 3.5.
The average of three, four, seven and eight is 5.5.
The average of 16, 15, 12.
11 is 13.5 and the average of 13, 14, ten.
Nine is 11.5.
So the output image is 3.5, 5.5, 13.5.
11.5.
Now that you know how pooling works, let's talk about why we should use it.
The first advantage of pooling is practical.
If we downsample the image, the image shrinks and therefore we have less data to process.
Less multiplications to do.
And of course that will speed things up.
But the second and more important advantage has to do with image processing itself.
Recall this idea of translational invariance.
This is the idea that I don't care where in an image the feature occurred, I just care that it occurred.
For example, both you and I can recognize this as an A.
We also recognize this as an A.
It doesn't matter where I put the A on the screen.
So translational invariance is important from a biological point of view and how we as humans learn.
We don't have to learn how to recognize A's at each point in our field of vision.
Once we know what something looks like, we can recognize it no matter if it's up, down, left or right.
So what is Max pooling doing?
Well, consider a small two by two box.
Remember that convolution tells us whether or not a feature has been found.
It's a pattern finder.
So imagine that in this two by two box, I have these flags pattern found.
Not found.
Not found, not found.
So it's a high number and a small number.
And a small number and a small number.
The pattern being found returns a higher number.
So when I take the max, it says the pattern has been found.
That's good because it tells me that the pattern has been found without caring where it was found.
Average pooling is the same idea, but I think max pooling is a little more intuitive in this regard.
As a side note, keep in mind that in this lecture I've assumed that we're using a pool size of two
and that at each pooling layer we select boxes which are two spaces apart.
Now, pooling layers have some flexibility in this regard.
First, it's not necessary for the pool size to be the same in both directions.
For example, you could look at a two by three box or a three by two box, although generally that's
unconventional.
Second is that it's possible for the boxes to overlap.
The hyperparameter that controls this is called the Stride.
It tells us how many spaces to move the pooling box for each output.
Previously we looked at the situation where we had a pool size of two by two and the stride was also
two.
This is a pretty common scenario.
If you had a stride of one, then our two by two box would overlap.
This is not really too common.
So while you have these options, which I want to make you aware of, they're usually not changed too
often.
So what you see in this lecture is what we do most often.
We use a pool size of two in each direction, and we have a stride of two so that we don't have any
overlapping boxes.
So now that you know what pooling does and why it works, let's discuss why the convolutional part of
the CNN is organized the way it is.
Why should we have a sequential pattern consisting of a conv layer followed by a pooling layer, followed
by a conv layer, followed by another pooling layer and so forth.
If you recall, I told you earlier that one cool thing researchers discovered is that CNN's are able
to learn features hierarchically.
So the initial layers end up learning basic strokes.
The next layer ends up learning individual facial features such as nose, eyes and lips.
The next layer ends up learning whole faces.
The key point to keep in mind is that after each pooling, the image shrinks.
But the filter sizes generally stay around the same.
Common filter sizes are three by three, five by five and seven by seven.
Generally speaking, they are much smaller than the input image.
But what happens as the image shrinks as it passes through each layer?
Well, let's suppose we start with a 32 by 32 image and we have four convolutional and pooling layers.
Let's assume we do convolution and same mode so it doesn't change the size of the image.
And let's assume all our convolution filters are sized three by three.
Although this doesn't come into play just yet.
In this case, after the first convolution and pooling, the image shrinks down to 16 by 16.
After the second convolution and pooling, the image shrinks down to eight by eight.
After the third convolution and pooling, the image shrinks down to 4x4.
And finally, after a fourth convolution and pooling, the image shrinks down to two by two.
What happens after this is still a mystery.
So let's just leave that for now and focus on the image shrinking.
As you can see, if you take a three by three filter and place it over a 32 by 32 image, it occupies
only a very small portion of the image.
In other words, it's looking for very tiny patterns to match, like, for example, simple edges and
strokes.
Then the image becomes size 16 by 16.
Now all of a sudden, the three by three filter takes up four times as much space in the image.
It takes up twice as much space in each direction, and two times two is four.
In any case, it occupies a much larger portion of the image relative to the first filter.
So now it's looking for patterns which take up more space on the image patterns like nose eyes and lips.
Next we get to an eight by eight image.
A three by three filter occupies almost a quarter of the image.
After that convolution and pooling, we get a 4x4 image.
Now, a three by three filter takes up most of the image.
So what have we learned?
We've learned that in a convolutional neural network convolution and pooling results in two things which
are corollaries of each other.
First, the image input shrinks.
Second, since filters generally stay the same size, the filter looks for larger and larger patterns
in the image.
This is what leads to the CNN learning hierarchical features of the input.
It first learns things that are relatively small compared to the total size of the image.
Then it learns to find bigger patterns and bigger patterns and so forth.
The last concept regarding the convolutional layers we have to think about is are we not losing information
at every layer if we shrink the image?
And the answer is yes, that's true.
We are losing spatial information because the image keeps shrinking.
We're saying we don't care where in the image the feature was found, just that it was found somewhere
in the image.
So we're losing information about where it was.
But there's another component of the convolution process we haven't considered yet, which is the number
of feature maps.
Again, we have a general pattern to follow, which is that while the size of the image generally decreases,
the number of feature maps generally increases.
In other words, I don't care where the feature is found, I just care that it was found.
But I do care about different possible features I can find.
So while you're losing spatial information, you're gaining information in terms of what features were
found in the image.
One last note on this convolution and pooling pattern.
One of the hardest things to grasp for a new students in deep learning is that we have so many choices.
We call these hyper parameters.
Previously for Anns, we looked at the learning rate, the number of hidden layers and the number of
hidden units.
But now with CNNs, we have a ton of more choices.
With convolution, we have to choose the filter size.
We have to choose the number of feature maps.
We have to choose the pool size and so forth.
But luckily, I think this is one instance where there's a general pattern that most people follow.
So you're not going in completely blind.
So stick with these guidelines and you can be sure that you're at least doing things per accepted convention
that is choosing small filters relative to the image like three by three, five by 5 or 7 by seven.
Another guideline is to repeat the pattern convolution, followed by pooling.
Another guideline is to increase the number of feature maps at each convolution.
So you might start with 32 and then 64, then one, 28 and maybe 128.
Again, the best way to learn about how other people are doing it is to read lots of papers and check
out which hyper parameters they selected.
Generally, you'll find that this is the pattern followed by most convolutional networks.
Next, I want to mention something a little funny, but in fact, pooling is sometimes not what we are
going to end up using in our CNNs.
Researchers have found that we can do something similar, but which is more efficient and sometimes
works just as well.
In particular, recall that for pooling, we have this concept of stride that tells us how far apart
each box should be when we take the max.
In fact, convolution has a stride option as well.
So in this animation you can see how convolution works with stride.
Remember, convolution just means multiply and add with a sliding window.
When we have convolution with a stride parameter, the stride tells us how far apart each window should
be.
And of course, our intuition tells us that if we use a stride of two, then the output image length
will be half of what the input was.
In other words, we get the same reduction in size by using a strided convolution instead of convolution,
followed by pooling.
The intuition behind why this works is that if you consider what an image looks like, an image is just
large patches of stuff.
What I mean by that is each pixel is likely to have a similar value to its neighboring pixels.
For example, suppose I'm looking at a red car.
I find a red pixel.
Now ask yourself, is the pixel above that also red?
Yes.
Is the pixel below that also probably red?
Yes.
The same can be said of the pixel to the left and to the right.
That's just the nature of images.
Pixels near each other are often very highly correlated.
You won't have this random jumping around of pixel values because something like that wouldn't look
like an image at all.
That would probably just be noise.
And so using a stride of two simply means having your filter skipping every other pixel.
It's saying, we don't care what those skipped pixels are because they probably are very close to the
pixels we were already looking at.
So to summarize, the convolutional part of our convolutional neural network now looks like this.
We can have a series of strided convolutions all with approximately the same filter size with increasing
feature maps at each layer.
Or we can have a series of convolutions followed by pooling again, all with approximately the same
filter size and with increasing numbers of feature maps at each layer.
The last part of this lecture will focus on the second half of the CNN, which is the feedforward neural
network part.
Now, there's not much to discuss here except that we have to consider the shape of an image is not
appropriate for a dense, feedforward neural network.
Remember that when an image comes out of a convolution, it's going to be three dimensional height by
width, by number of feature maps.
But a feedforward neural network takes in a feature vector, a one dimensional object.
Luckily, we've already discussed how this works.
We can turn a three dimensional object into a one dimensional object using the flatten layer in Keras.
One alternative to the flatten layer, which I really like, is the global max pooling layer.
This is a little different from the regular kind of pooling we discussed earlier.
Consider the question what if we have different sized images?
The largest source of images is the Internet.
Generally speaking, images we find on the Internet are not all the same size, so it makes sense to
wonder, is it possible to build a convolutional neural network that is capable of handling different
image sizes?
And the answer is yes, using a global max pooling layer.
Let's consider first why a simple flattened layer would not work.
Suppose again we have an input image of size 32 by 32 and four convolutions.
Assume again, we use convolution and same mode and we have a stride of two.
So after the first convolution, we get a 16 by 16.
After the second convolution, we get an eight by eight.
After the third convolution, we get a 4x4, and after the fourth convolution, we get a two by two.
But now let's say we have an input image of size 64 by 64.
In this case, the output after four convolutions would be 4x4.
Let's say for simplicity's sake, the number of output feature maps is 100.
Then for the 32 by 32 image, doing a flatten operation would yield an output of size two by two by
100, two times two times 100, which is 400 for a 64 by 64 image.
Doing a flatten operation would yield an output size of four times, four times 100, which is 1600.
And of course, a feedforward neural network is not capable of handling vectors of different sizes.
Now let's consider how global max pooling works.
The idea is simple.
If our input image is H by W by C, then the output is one by one by C, or equivalently just a vector
of size C.
In other words, it takes the max over the entire image along the spatial dimensions over each feature
map.
Since we have C feature maps, we end up with what is essentially a one dimensional vector of size C
since the one dimensions are redundant.
In fact, TensorFlow and Keras get rid of the redundant dimensions for us.
This follows the same general idea of pooling where we're saying, I don't care where in the image the
feature was found, just that it was found somewhere.
Global max pooling is the extreme of this, saying that the feature can be found anywhere in the entire
image across the entire height and width.
Since the output is always a vector of size c, it does not depend on the size of the image, meaning
that it allows the network to handle images of any size.
As a side note, we also have global average pooling, which is just the global version of regular average
pooling.
The only exception to this is if your images are too small, in which case you'll just get an error
when you try to run your code.
For example, if your image starts out as a two by two, then it's not possible to reduce the dimensions
by half four times.
You'll notice this happens if you do something like add too many convolutions to your neural network.
All right, so let's summarize the basic architecture of a modern convolutional neural network.
The first stage is a series of strided convolutions or optionally convolution, followed by pooling
and repeating that pattern in the interface between convolution layers and dense layers.
We have a flatten.
We also optionally have a global max pooling that reduces the image to just the max for each feature
map.
Finally, once we flattened our data into a one dimensional vector, we have a regular feedforward neural
network made up of a series of dense layers, as usual.
And as you learned in the previous section, the activation and number of nodes for the final layer
depends on the type of task being done.
So if you're doing scalar regression, then you'll only have one output node with no activation function.
If you're doing binary classification, you could have one output node with a sigmoid.
However, for K equals two or more classes, you can have k output nodes with a softmax activation.

--- CNN Code Preparation ---

In this lecture.
We are going to take a quick look at the code we're about to use to implement convolutional neural networks
for image classification.
Thanks to the new TensorFlow API, this is going to be a relatively straightforward task, although
I'm going to show you a few new tricks to make writing this code a little more convenient.
As usual, I'm going to repeat the steps of our analysis, just to be sure.
Step number one is to load in the data for the following examples.
We'll be using more difficult datasets in particular fashion Mnist and Cifar ten Fashion Mnist was designed
to be a drop in replacement for the Mnist dataset, which today is considered to be too easy for modern
machine learning algorithms.
One might consider it to be a solved problem.
Thus Fashion-mnist was born, and it was designed to be a standard benchmark, just like Mnist, but
with the goalpost further ahead.
Just like Mnist.
It's a dataset of 28 by 28 grayscale images, and it even has the same number of samples.
The difference is that instead of simply handwritten digits, we have images of different types of clothing.
For example, t shirts, shoes, pants and so forth.
Your job is to classify these images into the correct category.
The Cifar ten dataset is older, but more difficult.
This dataset contains color images, so the data takes up more Ram and the images are also a bit bigger.
32 by 32.
It contains classes such as automobile, frog, horse, cat and dog.
You can already imagine that with such tiny images it might be very easy to mistake a cat for a dog
or a dog for a horse.
Step number two is to build a model as per the topic of this section, we're going to build a convolutional
neural network.
This is not much more complicated than building an a an.
All that we need to do is add convolution layers, which are simply a different type of layer object
and TensorFlow.
So again, it's just a matter of spelling it right and passing in the right arguments as per the API.
What's new in this section is that I'm going to teach you a new way to use the Keras API called the
Functional API.
I personally prefer this method of writing models in TensorFlow as it makes the code somewhat cleaner
and more compact.
It's also convenient for building more complex neural networks later on.
Step number three is to train the model.
Step number four is to evaluate the model.
And step number five is to use the model to make predictions.
Just like the A and N section.
Nothing here changes.
These steps are model agnostic as per my rule.
All machine learning interfaces are the same.
One new thing you'll learn about later on in this section is what to do if your dataset is too large
to fit into memory or if you want to augment your data.
For example, we know that a dog facing left is a dog, but a dog facing right is also a dog.
So should our CNN not be able to recognize both of these orientations as a dog?
The answer is yes.
We also know that deep learning loves data.
The more data, the better.
This is why deep learning has come to prominence in recent years.
So data augmentation is a method of virtually adding more data without actually having it.
We'll discuss this more later on.
But for our initial example, we're going to keep things simple.
So let's elaborate on step number one, loading in the data.
Luckily, both the datasets will be using for these examples are also included in TensorFlow.
It's generally going to follow the same pattern as before.
So Fashion-mnist is available at tf.keras dot datasets, dot fashion-mnist dot load data and Cifar ten
is located at Tf.keras dot datasets dot Cifar10 dot load data.
As with Mnist, both of these return two tuples where the first tuple is the train set and the second
tuple is the test set.
Each tuple is split up into two arrays.
The input image is stored as a numpy array and the labels also stored as a numpy array.
As mentioned, since Fashion-mnist is a drop in replacement for Mnist, it has the exact same format.
They are grayscale images and the x matrix is of size.
N by 28 by 28.
Each image contains only integers with the values 0 to 255.
As before.
Now, importantly, this is not the right shape for a CNN because remember, a CNN wants something in
the shape of N by height, by width, by color.
So in order to make a color channel, we have to add a fourth dimension to the x matrix of size one,
which means no data is being added.
We're just adding a superfluous dimension.
This is kind of like how a vector could be a 1D array of length N or a 2D array of length n by one.
For the Cifar ten dataset.
We have pretty much what you expect.
The data is n by 32, by 32 by three, and each value is an integer from 0 to 255.
The one slight inconvenience is that the labels are of shape n by one rather than just a 1D array of
length.
N.
Luckily, this is pretty trivial to deal with.
We can just flatten the labels using the numpy function flatten.
All right.
So that's the data.
Pretty straightforward in terms of the format.
Next, let's look at building the model.
As promised, we're going to start taking a different approach in this section, which is to use the
Keras functional API.
By the way, one common question I hear is what's the difference between Keras, the library and Keras,
the module inside the TensorFlow library?
The current way of thinking of this is that Keras is just an API specification.
That means you can build your own deep learning library, not using TensorFlow or PyTorch or any other
lower level library, but you would still conform to the Keras API.
In this way someone could use Keras with your library as a back end and this code should work the same
as it does when you use any other library like TensorFlow as a back end.
In terms of TensorFlow, it's kind of an interesting situation because the creator of the Keras library
actually went on to work at Google.
So the TensorFlow team has direct access to the person who actually invented Keras.
And thus it kind of makes sense that Keras would be directly included in TensorFlow.
Practically speaking, the Keras library is installed separately from the backend library, such as
TensorFlow or piano.
So if you have a version mismatch, then things might not work.
Whereas if you use the Keras module within TensorFlow, you can be sure that everything works with each
other because they are all part of the same library.
At the time I'm making this lecture.
The latest version of Keras does not work with TensorFlow 2.0, whereas the Keras module inside TensorFlow
2.0 obviously works with TensorFlow 2.0.
Anyway, back to the Keras functional API.
I think the best way to learn is by example.
So take the basic feedforward A&amp;M we had earlier.
The way we define an Ann is we create a list of layers, input, dense and dense.
And then we pass this list into the sequential object constructor.
This returns a model on which we can call fit, predict, and so forth.
The corresponding functional way of doing this is to create the layers and for each layer you pass in
the input and get its output as if it were a function.
In other words, these Keras layer objects have sort of a dual purpose.
They are objects, but they also act like functions.
Once we get the final output, we pass both the input and the output into the model constructor.
And this returns a model object.
Now, some of you might object and say this code is pretty ugly.
Why am I using this one letter?
Variable X?
And even worse, why am I using the same variable over and over again to represent different things?
Isn't this bad style?
My personal opinion is that this is quite nice, but there's a more important concept to pay attention
to.
One of the most important rules of software engineering is to conform to the same style guide as the
rest of your team.
For example, you can't join a company that uses two space indentation and then commit all your code
using four spaces.
You can't join a company that uses spaces for indentation and then commit all your code using tabs.
Now that would be very bad style.
In data science and machine learning using one letter, variable names is common.
In terms of math, it actually leads to less cognitive load because it means your code.
Has a 1 to 1 mapping to your equations if you start naming all your variables.
Big long names, then you have to translate the equations into the code and vice versa.
Which takes more effort and hence is kind of a waste of time.
That is to say, what you see here is the convention for how to write Keras code.
So don't go against the convention and try to invent your own style because that actually makes your
code more unreadable rather than more readable.
So sometimes people ask me, Why do you like the Keras functional API?
Here are a few reasons.
First, to me it looks cleaner.
I like the simplicity.
But second, and this is more practical.
It allows you to create branches.
In other words, one layer's output might be fed into two different layers in parallel.
The functional API makes this very easy.
Of course, you haven't seen any models like this yet, but I assure you they exist.
We may even encounter some later in this course.
Third, it allows you to define models that have multiple inputs and outputs.
If you noticed the names of the arguments into the model, objects are plural.
This is because, in general, a model can have more than one input and more than one output.
Again, the functional API makes this very easy.
Just as easy as creating a model with one input and one output.
All right.
So now that you understand the functional API, let's look at how to create a CNN using the functional
API.
So we're going to build the simplest thing possible here with just a few strided convolutions, a flatten
and a couple of dense layers.
The only new thing for you here is the Conv 2d layer.
By the way, it's 2D, because the color dimension of the image is not a spatial dimension.
It's also possible to have 1D convolutions and 3D convolutions using the conv 1D and Conv 3D layers
respectively.
So for example, if you're looking at a signal that only varies with time, that would be a one dimensional
convolution.
For example, speech.
If you're looking at a video, for example, that would be a 3D convolution.
That's because height and width are the first two spatial dimensions, and time is the third dimension.
It's also possible to have objects that have three spatial dimensions height, width and depth instead
of pixels.
We call these voxels.
You might encounter these if you look at something like medical imaging data.
By the way, you might be wondering where the word voxel comes from.
Well, it's pretty obvious that it's analogous to the word pixel.
But why?
Pixel is actually a short form of the phrase picture element, and thus voxel is the short form of the
phrase volume element.
In any case, that's just for the curious.
Back to our CNN.
There are a few arguments into the Conv 2d constructor that we need to know about.
Of course they relate to the theory that we just discussed.
First is the number of output feature maps.
Just like the dense layer, we don't need to specify the number of input features because Keras already
knows that when you pass in the input from the previous layer.
Second is the filter size for this.
It's not the size of the entire four dimensional weight tensor.
It's only the spatial dimensions.
So you're going to pass in something like the tuple three by 3 or 5 by five.
We also have the stride, which controls the speed of the filter as you pass it along the image.
Next we have the activation function, which as mentioned in the previous section, is normally just
a Relu or some variant thereof.
Finally, we have the padding argument for which we can pass in the strings valid, same or full.
Valid is the default.
So that's what you get if you don't pass in anything at all.
The rest of the CNN is pretty straightforward since you've already seen it.
We flatten the image, then do a dense, maybe a few more and then a final dense to output the probabilities
for each of the K classes.
Optionally, we can also add some dropout regularization before each of the dense layers.
One interesting discussion I've seen pop up here and there is whether or not you should use dropout
regularization in the convolutional layers.
While the Keras API lets you do this, it might not necessarily be a good idea.
Remember that convolution is pattern finding.
It's looking for a specific stroke or edge.
If you suddenly remove half the pixels from an image, is the stroke still visible?
Can it still be considered the stroke that we are looking for?
Personally, I lean more towards no, but I've seen a few people use dropout in their convolution layers.
Even if it doesn't hurt, if it also doesn't help, then there's no point in using it.
As always, I recommend experimentation over guessing.
So if you're interested in trying it, I would strongly suggest doing so.
