--- Data Augmentation ---

In this lecture, we are going to discuss how to improve our model using a process known as data augmentation.
Consider this picture of a cat.
You and I can agree that it's a cat.
Now consider this other picture of a cat.
It's pretty obvious that this is the same cat.
Unfortunately, while this is easy for us, it's not so easy for a computer.
Now, the neural network may be able to identify this because of convolution, but perhaps there's a
way to help the neural network learn this translational invariance using data augmentation.
As you may have heard, one of the reasons deep learning has been so successful these days is that we
have so much more data.
Deep learning continues to improve as it trains on more and more data.
While other machine learning algorithms seem to hit a plateau.
One disadvantage of tabular data is that you just have what you have.
If we're doing classification to see if you'll pass my math exam and I based that on how many hours
you study and how many hours you play video games, that can only depend on the data I collected through
my survey.
I can't just make up new data which would necessarily include my own biases about student performance.
I can't invent a new student who studied this much and played video games this much and then decide
if he passed or failed.
That wouldn't make any sense.
However, images are an important class of data.
We can see them.
We know that both these images are cats.
I know that if I rotate this cat a little bit, it's still a cat.
I know that if I flip a car upside down or left to right, it's still a car.
Although you should probably be worried if you encounter an upside down car.
In other words, what I'm trying to say is with images, it is okay to invent new data.
Now, here's one problem with this approach.
Data takes up space, so the more new data I invent, the more space it takes up.
And on top of that, there are an endless number of ways I can invent new data.
I can shift the image to the left one pixel, two pixels, three pixels, four pixels, five pixels
and so forth.
And I can also go in the other direction.
I can also rotate one degree, two degrees, three degrees, and so on.
Do I really want to store all these combinations on my hard drive?
Probably not.
Also, Google CoLab probably wouldn't have space for that anyway.
So what can you do?
Luckily, none of this work is work you have to do yourself.
Have you ever considered how to rotate an image?
Well, it's not as straightforward as it sounds.
Instead, we can use TensorFlow Keras API to do all this work for us.
To understand how it's going to work, we have to discuss the concept of generators and iterators.
For the purpose of this lecture, we won't really differentiate between the two.
Consider what you do when you want to write a for loop using indices zero up to, let's say, ten.
We would do.
For I in range ten.
Now in Python two you would not want to use range ten because that would return an actual list of numbers
from zero up to nine.
Instead, you would use x range, which does not create a list but rather generates those numbers on
the fly.
That's why in Python three, this is the default behavior for the range function.
If you print out range ten, you will not see a list of numbers from zero up to nine as you might expect.
Conceptually, that's what it is, but physically that's not what it is.
Instead, it just says Range zero ten, which is kind of not telling us much.
A range is a range.
Well, it appears to be some kind of object on which you can call instance methods and so forth.
What you might not have known is that you can actually write your own generators.
For example, suppose you wanted to write a loop where the loop variable was a randomly generated number.
So for x in my random generator would be a loop where on each iteration of the loop x would be a random
number.
Can we do this in Python?
The answer is yes.
We can accomplish exactly this using the yield command.
So here is how you would write my random generator function that generates ten random numbers on the
fly.
As you can see, the code to generate the entire list of values does not have to be computed beforehand.
And more importantly, the entire list of values does not have to be stored in memory.
Instead, this takes up a constant amount of memory because as soon as the variable for each iteration
is computed and used, it is no longer needed.
So in Keras, when we want to do data augmentation, it's a similar concept.
Instead of pre calculating all of our augmented images beforehand, we are going to create them on the
fly.
You can imagine that it looks something like this.
For each batch of data we augment x batch using some randomly generated values.
Then we yield x batch and Y batch.
In other words, only a small slice of x train is ever augmented on each iteration.
More importantly, it's done randomly.
So you get something different each time, which helps with generalization.
So how does this actually work in Keras?
First, we need to create an object of type image data generator from the module Tf.keras dot preprocessing
dot image.
The constructor for this image accepts a bunch of arguments for how to augment the image such as rotation
range width shift range height shift range.
Brightness range.
Shear range.
Zoom range.
Horizontal flip and vertical flip.
So there are things in here that we haven't even considered yet.
For example, a brighter cat or a darker cat is still a cat.
Shearing is sort of like rotation, but you can think of it as ripping apart the image.
There's also Zoom.
A big cat is still a cat and a small cat is still a cat.
One thing that's not always applicable is vertical flip.
For example, if you flip a nine upside down and left to right, you would get a six, which is not
what we want.
Or you would get the same effect if you rotated the image 180 degrees.
So you want to be sure that when you create this generator object, that you pass in the right range
of values.
The next step is to call the flow function where you pass in the actual data you want to augment.
Here you also pass in the batch size.
As a side note, if you want to learn more about what batch size means, you can check out the in-depth
section on gradient descent.
So this is what actually returns the generator.
Now, theoretically, you could loop through the generator yourself, but you don't have to.
Instead, there is a Keras model function that deals with this directly called very appropriately fit
generator.
For this function, instead of passing in x train and y train, you just pass in the generator you just
created.
Keras automatically knows to loop through the generator and process each batch of data as it arrives.
One thing we have to pass in here is the steps per epoch argument which we did not have to do before.
This is because a generator can potentially just go on forever.
Essentially, this is just n divided by the batch size.
For example, if you have 50 samples and your batch size is ten, then you'll want to iterate through
five batches because five times ten is equal to 50.
That's how many steps it would take to see every sample in your training set.
After that, everything is the same as usual.
The fit generator function, just like the fit function returns an object where we can look up the training
history so we can plot the loss per iteration and any other metric we ask the model to keep track of.

--- Batch Normalization ---

In this lecture, we are going to introduce a new type of layer which has been shown to be very helpful
in training convolutional neural networks.
Here's something to consider.
Recall that early on we looked at why it's important to normalize your data before passing it into a
model such as linear regression or logistic regression.
We do this by subtracting the mean and dividing by the standard deviation.
But because this is an operation on the data, we can only do it at the very first layer of the neural
network.
What happens if the data becomes not normalized at some later layer?
Then that layer has to deal with unnormalized data, which is not as good as normalized data.
So this begs the question how can we make the data at every layer normalized?
The answer is batch normalization.
If you recall, inside the TensorFlow fit function, we're actually doing batch gradient descent on
the outer loop.
We loop through each epoch and on the inner loop we look at a chunk of data on each step and do gradient
descent with respect to just this chunk of data.
This works because we know that a batch of data will have approximately the same statistics as the full
dataset.
So what if we did something like this?
We inject a layer that does normalization for us, for example, in an A and N instead of dense, dense,
dense.
We have batch norm dense batch, norm dense batch, norm dense.
At each of these layers, all it does is take the batch of data, subtract its mean and divide by its
standard deviation.
In fact, this is pretty much what batch normalization does.
But there's something extra that batch normalization does.
Here's another question to consider.
How do we know that normalization is actually good?
In fact, we don't.
Perhaps there's a better amount of shifting and a better amount of scaling that we can use that leads
to a more optimal model.
And so this is the full description of the batch normalization layer.
It first, Does normalization using the batch mean and the batch standard deviation?
Then it rescales the data and re shifts the data so that the final output has an optimal location and
an optimal scale.
Of course, these new parameters, gamma and beta are learned automatically using gradient descent.
One alternative perspective on batch normalization is that it acts as a regularizer.
As you know, we use regularization to prevent overfitting with methods such as dropout.
So how does batch normalization do regularization?
The idea is because each batch of data is slightly different, it's going to have a different mean and
standard deviation.
It's not the true mean and standard deviation of the entire dataset.
These differences are like noise, which slightly change what the neural network sees on each iteration.
In other words, because the neural network always has to learn noisy images, it becomes impervious
to the noise.
The final thing I want to mention in this lecture is that while we discussed batch Norm in the context
of a feedforward neural network batch, norm is not commonly applied in between dense layers.
As you recall, this is the CNN section of the course.
And so the reason we're talking about batch normalization now is because batch norm is mostly used with
convolutions.
Now, adding batch Norm also adds some hyper parameter choices.
In particular, you have to decide whether or not you should use batch norm.
And if you decide you should, then you have to decide where to put it.
Luckily we can just follow the conventions in this regard.
Batch norm layers are usually applied after convolution layers, well after or before.
It depends on which way you look at it.
So maybe a better way of looking at it is that they go in between convolution layers.
One common pattern would be like this convolution to batch norm, to convolution to batch norm, back
to convolution, back to batch norm and then flatten and then your dense layers.
So that's just to give you an idea of what it might look like.
You're always encouraged to try things on your own, but also to read about the most popular.
CNNs such as Vgg, Resnet and Inception to see how they're doing things.
This is the approach we're going to take in the next lecture when we improve our Cifar ten results.
By the way, if you want to read more about Batch Norm, you can check out the paper included in extra
reading dot txt.
The title is Batch Normalization Accelerating Deep network training by reducing internal covariate Shift.

In this lecture.

data augmentation and batch normalization.
This lecture is going to walk you through a prepared CoLab notebook, although a very good exercise,
which I always recommend, is once you know how this is done, to try and recreate it yourself with
as few references as possible.
As usual, you can look at the title of a notebook to determine what notebook we are currently looking
at.

--- Embeddings ---

In this section of the course, we are going to discuss how to deal with text, data and natural language
processing.
You may have heard that RNNs or recurrent neural networks are good for dealing with sequences, and
hence they are good for dealing with text.
Why is this?
Because text is also sequence data.
But there is a major difference between text sequences and the continuous valued sequences we were dealing
with in the previous section.
The difference is text is made of words, and words are categorical objects.
In other words, they are not continuous.
There is another idea I snuck in there, which is the idea that somehow sequences are related to images
which we looked at earlier.
In short, images are in fact like sequences, except they are two dimensional.
So you can imagine they have two time dimensions, and the value of the sequence is the pixel intensity
in the image.
We'll discuss this more in a later lecture.
For now, let's think about why sequences of text cannot be treated the same way as a continuous valued
sequence.
Suppose we're given a sequence of words.
The quick brown fox jumps over the lazy dog.
So x1 equals the x2 equals quick, and so on.
But here's the problem.
We know that in a neural network we are always doing something like a matrix multiplication, whether
that's an actual matrix multiply or a convolution.
In order to do any kind of multiplying, we must have numbers.
You can see that if x is a category, this is not possible.
You might think aha, I've seen this before.
When I have categories, all I need to do is one hot encode them.
So for example what you would do is create an array which has length equal to the size of our vocabulary.
In other words, the number of words in the English language.
Typically we denote that with the capital letter V.
So here's how we are going to one hot encode a word.
First we're going to create a vector of size big V containing all zeros.
Then we're going to create a mapping where we have for each word a corresponding integer starting from
one.
So for example the word a will get the index one.
The word a will get the index two.
The word activation might get the index 100.
And we do this for all other words.
So we get all the way up to zoo which maybe has the index 1 million.
Finally, once we have a map for each word to an integer, we simply set that corresponding index to
one.
So as you can see, all of these vectors are a bunch of zeros with only a single one at a unique position.
So thus our final one hot encoded word vector becomes a vector of mostly all zeros except with one one.
So what happens after we do this?
Well, now we can go back to our usual scenario.
If we have a sequence of t words and each of them becomes a vector of size v, then we have a t by v
matrix which represents our sequence.
This is the same shape as a generic t by d sequence.
We can then pass this into our recurrent neural network as normal.
So let's do a very simple example.
Suppose we have the sentence I like cats.
This is a sequence of length three because this sentence has three words.
This might turn into the sequence of vectors zero, zero, 0101001000.
For this simple example, we've assumed that our vocabulary only contains four words, which of course
is much smaller than a real English language dataset.
But there are some problems with this approach.
Some English language data sets have about 1 million possible tokens, or words that would lead to an
extremely large, one hot encoded feature vector, which means that your weight matrix would also be
very large.
So in other words, you have a t by V matrix, but V is a very big number.
In actuality, the English dictionary contains only about 200,000 words, so it really depends on how
you split up your words and where you obtain your vocabulary from.
But this is still a significantly large number.
Here's another problem with this approach.
Remember that we would like our input features to have some structure.
If you recall, one of the earliest rules we encountered was machine learning is nothing but a geometry
problem.
This rule relies on the fact that there is geometrical structure in our data.
Data vectors from the same class are probably close to each other.
But what do one hot encoded feature vectors look like?
Well, if we take any two one hot encoded feature vectors and we calculate the Euclidean distance,
we will always get the square root of one squared plus one squared, which is just the square root of
two.
It doesn't matter which two words we select because they are all one hot encoded.
For example, the distance between cat and feline would be square root of two, but the distance between
cat and airplane would also be square root of two.
In other words, this data has no useful geometrical structure.
So what can we do instead?
Well, it would be nice if for each word we could map them to a d dimensional vector.
Luckily we have just the tool for this.
It's called an embedding layer.
Now you might be might be wondering, how can we actually make these vectors have a useful structure?
We'll get back to that later, so stay tuned.
But before we discuss that, I want to show you a little coding trick.
So consider what happens if we multiply a one hot encoded vector with a weight matrix.
Let's suppose the vector is just three dimensional 100, and the weight matrix just contains the numbers
one up to nine.
You can verify for yourself that when we multiply this vector by this matrix, we get the vector one
two, three.
Now consider the one hot encoded vector 010.
What happens when you multiply this vector by the same weight matrix?
We get the vector 456.
Now consider the one hot encoded vector 001.
What happens when we multiply this vector by the same weight matrix?
We get the vector 789.
So what is the pattern we see?
If the index in the one hot encoded vector which was set to one was one, then we get the first row
of the weight matrix.
If the index was set to two, then we get the second row of the weight matrix.
If the index was set to three, then we get the third row of the weight matrix.
In other words, if we one hot encode the integer k and multiply it by the weight matrix.
All that's really doing is selecting the kth row of the weight matrix.
So here's a shortcut.
The old way of doing this took two steps.
First we had to create a one hot encoded vector and set the kth entry to one.
Second, we had to multiply this one hot encoded vector by the weight matrix.
The shortcut way of doing this takes only one step.
We simply index the weight matrix at the row k.
This is obviously much more efficient than creating a one hot vector and then doing matrix multiplication.
Think of it this way indexing an array is O of one.
That's constant time.
But how long does it take to create a one hot vector and then do matrix multiplication?
If your vector is of size v and the weight matrix is of size v times d, then this is o of v times d.
So this is exactly what the embedding layer in TensorFlow does.
Instead of doing all the work yourself to create one hot encoded vectors.
The only step you need to do is map your dataset with sequences of words into a dataset with sequences
of word indices.
Now that you have only integers, you can use an embedding layer to map each word's integer to a corresponding
word vector.
From there on out, your sequence becomes a t by d matrix, at which point you can use an RNN as you
normally would.
Conceptually, you can think of the process like this.
First we have some sentence a sequence of words.
Let's say for simplicity's sake, it's I like cats.
Then this sequence of words becomes a sequence of integers.
Obviously, it doesn't matter which integer we assign to each word as long as they are unique.
This is just like when we are doing classification.
It doesn't matter if we set dog to one and cat to zero or vice versa.
They are They are just arbitrary assignments.
Finally, we can use these integers to index an embedding matrix, which will convert each integer into
a word vector.
So in the end, what we have done is two steps.
First, we converted words to integers.
Second, we map those integers to vectors.
One question you might have at this point is.
We know intuitively that similar words should be closer together than dissimilar words.
In other words, these feature vectors should exist somewhere meaningful relative to each other.
For example, King should be close to queen and car should be close to automobile, but queen should
not be close to car, and king should not be close to automobile.
So how can we make sure that that's the case?
Luckily, this is the same story as with convolutional filters because they are just weights in a neural
network.
They all get trained automatically.
There's nothing fancy you need to do now.
There's one caveat to that, which is that what people often do is use pre-trained word vectors.
How this works is when they create an embedding layer, they set the weights to a set of pre-trained
vectors that were obtained through some other method.
Then they freeze this layer so that when you call model.fit, those weights are never changed.
Typically, these word vectors are found through algorithms such as word two, vec and glove.
Now that is a deep topic in itself.
So in this course we are going to avoid discussing that.
Instead, we will train embeddings like any other layer by calling Model.fit and letting gradient descent
do its magic.
If you want, you're encouraged to read the word two vec and glove papers, since they are generally
pretty accessible.
To summarize this lecture, here's what we discussed.
First, we recognize that it's not possible to multiply a word by a weight matrix.
There's no concept of multiplying words.
Therefore, we needed to somehow convert words into a numerical representation.
We first thought about one hot encoding each word, but we soon realized why that would not be a good
idea.
First of all, it would take up way too much space.
There are around 1 million possible tokens in the English language, so each word would take up 1 million
numbers to represent, which is quite silly.
Also, these one hot encoded vectors are actually meaningless in terms of being feature vectors.
This is because each word vector, if it's just a one hot encoded vector, would be equal distances
apart.
So cat is the same distance to feline as it is to airplane, so that's not very useful.
It doesn't allow us to really make use of the rule.
Machine learning is nothing but a geometry problem, and thus we need a better way to convert words
into vectors.
This method is to take each word, turn it into an integer, and then use that integer to index an embedding
matrix.
This is much faster than one hot encoding and then doing matrix multiplication.
In this way, an input sentence which consists of words then becomes a t by d matrix of numbers, which,
you know, a neural network can readily accept.
We saw that this layer's parameters are trained just like any other layer, so nothing other than calling
model.fit needs to be done.
Alternatively, if you are interested, you are welcome to read up on word two, vec and glove, which
provide different ways of initializing word embeddings so that you don't need to train them along with
the rest of the neural network.

--- Code Preparation (NLP) ---

In this lecture we are going to do some code preparation.
Previously we discussed embeddings and how that would help us convert a sequence of words into a t by
d matrix of numbers, which we can then pass into a neural network.
Now we know that this process isn't just instant like one line of code.
So this lecture will focus on the details of how this is done.
And hopefully by the end of this lecture, you'll be ready to tackle the following code examples.
We know that the first thing we have to do when converting a sequence of words into a sequence of vectors
is that we need to first convert that sequence of words into a sequence of integers.
Each word needs to have an integer representation, because that tells us which row of row of the word
embedding matrix to index.
So the question is how do we do this.
Well it really helps to write your own pseudocode even though that's not what you may end up using.
And you'll realize that this is actually not too difficult to do by yourself.
All you need to do is loop through each word in your data set and assign integer indices as you encounter
each new word.
What we really want to do is find a mapping or a dictionary that tells us for each word, what is the
corresponding integer value?
So here's some code that can help us do that.
We start by setting a variable called current index to one.
Then we initialize an empty dictionary called word to index.
I think this makes it clear what the dictionary is going to store.
The key to the dictionary will be a word, while the corresponding value will be the index.
Then we loop through each word in our data set.
Imagine that Imagine that it's just a bunch of documents concatenated together.
So we may encounter the same word more than once.
Then we check whether or not the word is in our word to index dictionary.
If it's not, then we assign this word to the current index.
And then we increment the current index to be used for the next word that we find.
Now you might look at this and wonder if it's in a computer.
Why do we start at index one rather than index zero.
And this is pretty important for TensorFlow in particular.
If you recall I said earlier that TensorFlow uses constant length time series.
So we'll always have an n by t by d array containing our data.
But t represents the maximum length sentence.
So any sentence shorter than that would need to contain a padding.
Therefore we need a special number to represent padding.
And that number is zero.
Therefore we do not use zero for word indices because it's already being used for padding.
More on padding later.
There is still more we need to discuss in terms of converting sequences of words into sequences of integers.
The previous pseudocode we looked at was for your intuition only.
In actuality, TensorFlow and Keras already have some built in functions to help us do this work.
The first thing we have to realize is that text, in and of itself, is not formatted as a sequence
of words.
Instead, text, when you load it in from a file, is just a string.
You'll have one single string containing possibly multiple sentences, and each sentence will, of course,
contain multiple words.
So it should be clear that the very first thing we need to do is separate each word from the other words.
What I mean by that is given a single string containing multiple words.
What we would really like to have is just a list of strings, where each string contains only a single
word.
This is a process known as tokenization because we're going to create a list of tokens.
So just to add a little more detail to this process, we've discovered that we actually need to do two
things here.
First, we need to convert a string which may contain multiple words into a list of individual words.
Second, this allows us to then loop through each word and then assign word indices.
Luckily, the TensorFlow tokenizer does all this work for us.
For example, suppose we're given three sentences in a list.
So we have.
I like eggs and ham.
I love chocolate and bunnies and I hate onions.
What the tokenizer will do is return a corresponding list of sequences of integers, where each sentence
has been converted into a list of Integers.
Each integer, of course, corresponds to a word.
So here's what it looks like in TensorFlow code.
Now you'll notice one more important thing, which is that the tokenizer accepts an argument called
num words.
This allows us to specify how many words we want our tokenizer to actually keep.
You can imagine that if you have a huge Google sized dataset, it's going to have about a million tokens,
most of which are extremely rare and probably useless for classification or whatever else you're trying
to do.
Also consider misspellings.
Thus, num words allows us to limit the total number of words in our vocabulary, and everything else
is just to sign the same token.
So you can think of that token as representing rare words or unknown words.
Other than that, it's just three lines of code.
First, you instantiate the tokenizer object.
Then you call the fit on text function where you pass in the sentences.
Your sentences will be an iterable, like a list or array containing sentences.
Each sentence is a string.
Finally, you call the function text to sequences to convert an iterable of sentences into a list of
corresponding sequences of integers.
The second preprocessing step we have to consider is this.
When we convert our sentences into sequences of integers through the tokenizer.
Those sentences all have different lengths.
That is a problem for us, because we know that in TensorFlow, neural networks do not accept sequences
of different lengths as we discussed previously.
The solution to this is padding.
Each sentence in the dataset will be padded, so that shorter sentences will all have the same length
as the longest sentence.
So how do we do this in TensorFlow?
First, just as a thought exercise, I always like to think about how I would do this myself if I did
not have these nice tools at my disposal.
And if you think about it, it's pretty easy.
First, just find the maximum sequence in your list of sequences.
Get the length of that.
Call that max size.
Then loop through each sequence.
Then for each sequence, append a list of zeros of size max size minus the length of the sequence.
In that way, all the sequences will become the same length, just max size.
And that's all there is to it.
Luckily, while you could also do this by yourself manually, you don't have to because there is a TensorFlow
function to do it for us.
Not unsurprisingly, it is called padsequences.
So all you need to do is pass in your jagged array of sequences into pad sequences, and you get back
a nice N by T matrix padded with zeros where necessary.
Now there are some arguments into this function that are important to be aware of.
First, there's the max Len argument.
This controls the size of big T.
I told you earlier that this would be the size of the maximum length sequence, which is true if you
use the default setting where max Len equals none.
But there's always the option to set it to a different number.
For example, if your data set contains some really long sentences, but you know that those sentences
contain mostly data that can be ignored, then you may want to simply truncate those sentences.
In other words, make them shorter by cutting off the beginning or the ending.
But if you're going to truncate a sentence, how do we know if it's going to cut off the beginning or
the end?
Well, you can control whether to cut off the beginning or the end by setting the argument truncating
to pre or post.
On the other hand, there's really no reason to set maxlen to be bigger than your maximum length sequence,
since that would just be a waste of space.
So in other words, every sentence will end up being padded with useless zeros.
The next important argument is the padding argument, which can also be pre or post.
This lets you control whether to pad the shorter sentences with zeros at the beginning of the sequence,
or at the end of the sequence.
This brings up the next question.
Is it also possible to control where the padding goes?
What if we want to put the padding at the beginning versus at the end?
Luckily we can control this too.
There are some instances where you would want the padding at the beginning, and some instances where
you would want the padding at the end, for example.
Suppose you're building a spam detection classifier that reads in an entire sequence and decides at
the final time step whether or not the email is spam.
Well, in that case, you would want to have padding at the beginning.
Why?
Because remember, RNNs have trouble with long distance memory.
So you wouldn't want to end your sequence with a bunch of zeros and a risk of the RNN forgetting what
it saw earlier.
Let's recap what we have so far to figure out where we need to go next.
First, we've seen that our data will be something like a list of strings, where each string is a sentence
or possibly multiple sentences.
Either way, each string will contain multiple words bunched up together in a single string.
Second, we use the tokenizer to convert those strings into a list of sequences of integers.
Those integers each correspond to a different word, which of course can later be used to index a word
embedding matrix.
Third, we pad those sequences so that they all come out the same length, and thus we end up with a
2D array of size n by t, and as usual is the number of samples, and t is the sequence length.
Each entry of this array is a word index, so the entry at x and t will tell you the word that appears
in the nth document at time step t.
The next step is to consider what do we do with this n by t matrix.
Well as we discussed earlier we pass this through an embedding layer.
Again this is all about keeping in mind the shapes.
Remember that an embedding layer is going to return a d sized word vector given a word index.
So when we pass it an array of size n by t what do we get.
We get an n by t by d array.
Now, what is this?
This should look familiar.
This is exactly what we need to pass this through the rest of a neural network.
So here's the whole neural network in one slide.
First, we declare an input of shape n by t.
Then we need to pass the output of the embedding layer, which is now shape n by t by d into neural
network layers like convolutions or LSTMs.
Note that for CNNs, you can optionally add batch normalization and pooling, as you've seen when we
work with images.
Furthermore, as with image based CNNs, we can have multiple rounds of convolutions and poolings.
However, note that these are all one dimensional layers instead of two dimensional, like when we work
with images.
Optionally, as you know, we can use global max pooling at the end if you like.
If you want the neural network to be able to handle sequences of varying length.
So I wanted to make a new note here, which is that in the following code lectures you'll see me using
a new layer called the text vectorization layer instead of the tokenizer, which is now deprecated in
TensorFlow and Keras, although it still works.
I've decided to make updates.
Now in order to be prepared.
Luckily, most of the functionality is the same, but some of it is different and I will make note of
the differences when we look at the code.
I've decided to leave the rest of this lecture up, since it conveys the important principles which
are independent of the syntax.
This goes along with my motto.
Learn the principles, not the syntax.
So all the stuff you just learned is still important, including the concept of splitting a string into
tokens, transforming the string by lowercasing and removing punctuation, converting the tokens into
integers, and keeping a mapping from token to integer and vice versa, as well as padding and truncation.
So you're still going to see all of these just with a different syntax.

Okay, so let's do this.
And you can see now how it also returns a list.
But it adds these zeros in front where we need padding.
So this is what you would have to do if you want padding at the front.
Okay.
So now why do we want padding in the front.
And the answer is so if you imagine a model like say a recurrent neural network that reads the input
from the beginning to the end, but as you may know, it has trouble remembering things that are too
far in the past.
And so if you had, for example, put the padding at the end.
And so also keep in mind that a typical way to use RNNs is to read the final hidden state, so it processes
all the data.
You have all the hidden states from the intermediate steps, but you only read the last one, meaning
you have the best view of the most recent And the worst view of the oldest tokens.
And so if the oldest tokens are the actual data and the most recent tokens are padding, then you're
going to lose information.
Then the neural network is going to find it harder to read the information in the actual input, because
you put it all the way at the beginning versus this way.
We're putting the padding at the front and the actual information at the end, so that the most recent
thing the neural network has seen is the actual sequence, rather than just the padding.
And so if you're using something like a recurrent neural network or an LSTM that has trouble going too
far back into the past, at least most recently, what you've seen is the actual data.
And you can do experiments yourself.
Like to try pre padding and post padding and seeing which type of input gives you the best performance.

--- CNNs for Text ---

In this lecture, we are going to discuss how we can use convolutional neural networks for sequences,
specifically text.
Although the type of CNN we are about to discuss will work just as well for generic sequences too.
You might be wondering how can this be if CNN's are for images, how is it possible for CNN's to also
work with sequences?
First, let's recall the basics of convolution.
The idea is this You have an image which is the big square.
You have a filter, which is the small square, and you're going to slide the filter along each possible
position in the big square.
And at each point you're going to multiply all the overlapping values and add them together just like
a dot product.
Now, I know this is obvious, but it's worth taking note of.
An image has two dimensions height and width.
There is also the feature dimension, but that's not an actual dimension which has correlation.
And recall what I mean by correlation.
If you have a picture of a red car and you find a pixel in the image which is red, probably the neighboring
pixels are also red.
In other words, pixels beside each other likely have similar values.
Now think of a sequence.
Unlike an image, a sequence has just one dimension that is not a feature time.
So it's time instead of space.
But notice one important detail.
We have the same type of correlation and sequences.
Data which are nearby.
Other data in time are also close in value.
That's why this appears like a smooth curve rather than just noise jumping around with random uncorrelated
values.
This suggests that convolution might be useful here as well.
Luckily, convolution in one dimension is actually much simpler than convolution in two dimensions instead
of a big square, we just have a big line and the filter is the smaller line.
Then we slide the small line across every position in the big line.
Multiply all the overlapping values and add them together.
Let's do a simple example for the sequence data.
We have.
One, two, three, two, one.
And for the filter we have simply plus one minus one.
As an exercise, you might want to try to figure out the answer first before we move on to the solution.
Okay, So here's what we get at the first position.
We have one times one plus two times minus one, which is minus one.
At the second position, we get two times one plus three times minus one, which is minus one.
At the third position, we get three times one plus two times minus one, which is one.
And at the fourth and final position, we get two times, one plus one times minus one, which is one.
So essentially exactly what we would expect.
As with images, it's possible to express this operation as an equation.
And remember that in deep learning, while we call this convolution, pure mathematicians and statisticians
would call this cross-correlation.
So just keep in mind that the sign is reversed.
As an exercise, you might want to try and confirm to yourself that this equation does in fact implement
the operation we just performed in the previous example.
And remember that another thing we can do is add on features.
So imagine X as a T by D array, where T is the number of time steps and D is the number of input features.
Then imagine the output as another two dimensional array of size.
T by M, where T is the number of time steps and M is the number of output features.
Then W must be a three dimensional array T by D by M, and then we would have the equation that we see
here.
This is just like convolution with images for a two dimensional convolution.
We have two spatial dimensions plus one dimension for the input features, plus one dimension for the
output features.
So that's four dimensions in total.
For a one dimensional convolution, we have one time dimension plus one dimension for the input features,
plus one dimension for the output features.
So that's three dimensions in total.
As usual.
While convolution might seem like an abstract concept, remember that there are multiple convenient
perspectives on it that make it seem like things we are already familiar with.
So one perspective is that it's just matrix multiplication.
Just like a regular feedforward neural network.
Except that we have shared weights in order to take advantage of the special structure and correlation
in the data.
Another intuitive perspective is that it's just a sliding dot product and the dot product is just a
correlation finder.
Correlation is just another name for pattern matching or similarity.
So really what we are doing is asking, is this part of the sequence similar to my filter and thus the
filter becomes a pattern matcher or a pattern finder?
All these concepts that you learn about convolution before still apply here.
All right.
So now that you know how convolution works with sequences, how do we apply these to text?
Well, luckily, when we use embeddings, that already gives us exactly what we need for one dimensional
convolution.
We need an input, which is a T by D sequence where T is the number of time steps and D is the number
of features.
And of course, this is exactly what we get after we pass our sentence through an embedding layer.
We go from a length t sequence of words to a length t sequence of integers to a length t sequence of
D length vectors.
Since we have t vectors each of length d, this makes up a T by d matrix when you stack them all together,
and thus we have exactly what we need to build a CNN for text.
So here's how it all looks in code.
First, we have our input.
That's a length t sequence of word indexes.
Then we have an embedding layer.
The output of that is a T by D sequence of word vectors.
Then we have a 1D convolution.
This is just the conv 1d class.
Then we follow the same typical CNN architecture where we have convolution followed by pooling and so
forth.
So the same pattern applies here that we had for images.
Generally speaking, the data shrinks in the time dimension but grows in the feature dimension.
So that's why you see the number of feature maps getting larger and larger.
Once we're done that, then we can do a flatten or we can do a global max pooling which will give us
a single vector of size.
M three.
Finally, we pass this through one or more dense layers to get a single scalar.
Assuming we are doing binary classification.
As you can see, this is no different from a CNN meant for images, except that all the convolutions
and pooling are one dimensional instead of two dimensional.

So the difference is that when we're working with RNNs, we don't have to have we don't have to have

Okay.
An RNN can deal with a sequence of any length.
It's just that when we have a batch of sequences, they all have to be the same length so that we can

So the issue with that.
So we can do that with CNN's as well.
So we could have a per batch constant size but every batch can be a different size.
The issue with that is that this won't quite work with CNN's when we're doing text classification,
because so we're doing all these convolutions and poolings, which, as you know, will change the size
of the data or the vectors as they go through the neural network.
And you might say, well, we can use global max pooling.
And that ignores the variable sizes because it collapses the time dimension.
The problem is that if you have, say, a set of inputs, input texts that are so short, so say 1 to
3 words, that when you do the convolutions by the second or third convolution, there's no more to
convolve.
So it will complain that the size of the output of the convolution goes negative.
Okay.
And you can try that yourself to see what I mean.
So although an RNN could handle that, a CNN could not because a CNN, it's going to explicitly change
the size of the data as it goes through the network, whereas an RNN does not do that.

Convolutional Neural Networks Deep Learning in Python Part three.
In this section, we're going to start looking at the idea of convolution.
Of course, in order to understand what a convolutional neural network is, you have to know two things
what a neural network is and what convolution is.
Since you already know what a neural network is and how to build one, the next thing you have to understand
is how convolution works.
The next step is to put them together.
In this lecture, I want to give you a zero math low tech introduction to convolution in the real world.
It may surprise you to learn that convolution is everywhere.

and so on.

The application of a filter to the input signal is an example of doing convolution.

We're not doing convolution to add special effects to an image or audio.
We're doing convolution because it's going to transform the data in a useful way such that it'll be
useful for computer vision tasks like image classification.
And since this is deep learning, keep in mind that human design filters won't do.

computer.
So the filters we just looked at are very intuitive to us humans because they produce aesthetically

--- Beginner's Guide to Convolution ---

Hey, everyone, and welcome back to this class.
Convolutional Neural Networks Deep Learning in Python Part three.
In this lecture, I want to give you a very basic beginner's guide to convolution.
The reason I want to do this is because this section on convolution is very long.
I go more in depth into convolution than any other resource I've come across.
And so it's up to you if you want to go that deep.
In this lecture, I'm going to show you everything you need to know so that if you want to skip the
rest of this section and jump straight into how convolution fits into neural networks, then you can
do so.
So what do you need to know in order to be able to do convolution?
Well, you need just two things.
Can you multiply?
Can you add?
If your answer to these two questions is yes, then you can do convolution.
I think the easiest way to learn convolution is just to jump straight into an example.
The convolution operator is denoted by a star or an asterisk.
So when you see I star K, that means the image I convolved with the filter.
K.
Note that in deep learning the filter is also called a kernel.
So what's happening here?
Well, we're taking an input image ie a filter k and we're trying to find the output image on the right.
In this particular example, we're highlighting how the Green Square is calculated.
As you can see, we get a value of four.
Where does this value come from?
Well, if you take the red square and the blue square and you multiply all those numbers together element
wise and then add up the results, you get four.
So let's try that.
The only values that are not zero are the top left corner, where we have one times one the center where
we have one times one, the bottom left where we have another one times one and the bottom right where
we have another one times one.
So we have one plus one plus one plus one, which is four.
So how are the rest of the entries on the right Calculated.
Well, in fact, they are calculated just by moving the red square around on the image, multiplying
that block with the filter, just like we did.
So everywhere you can possibly put the red square, I'm taking that red square, multiplying it by the
blue square and then summing up the results.
Now, if you notice, the input image is a seven by seven image and the output is five by five.
The filter is three by three.
This is because there are only five possible positions going from left to right and up and down that
the red box can fit into.
So here you see one, two, three, four, five.
Going this way.
And one, two, three, four, five, going that way.
So that's why the output is five by five.
As an exercise, I would recommend trying to calculate the rest of the output elements just to make
sure you understand how this is done.
Here is another example on an image which is a bit smaller, just five by five, so that the output
is three by three.
I like this one because you can actually see the filter move along each position in the original image.
Note how the filter's values show up in the shaded region at the bottom right hand corner.
Again, I recommend computing these outputs by hand so that you really understand how this works.
Here's another visualization I like for how convolution works.
As you can see, the output size can be directly calculated from the size of the filter and the size
of the image.
In general, if the image is of length n and the filter is of length K, then the output will be of
length, n minus k plus one.
So in this example, n equals four and K equals three.
So the output is of length four minus three plus one, which is two.
Now, you might be wondering if it's possible to make it so that the output length is the same as the
input length.
And so this is how you would do that.
Essentially, you pretend that the image extends outwards with zeros.
We call this padding.
So we imagine that the input image is padded all around with zeros.
Then you just do convolution as normal.
This is sometimes known as same padding or half padding.
Finally, we have one more way to pad the input image where we pad even more zeros so that the filter
can extend out even further.
Essentially we want it so that every part of the filter touches every part of the input image.
This yields an output that is bigger than the input.
This case is usually called full padding and the output size is now N plus K minus one.
So in this image, N equals five and K equals three.
So the output size is five plus three minus one, which is seven.
All right.
So that's your crash course on convolution.
I want to reiterate that the rest of this section will be spent on going very in depth into convolution.
We're going to write convolution from scratch and see how we can implement filters like the echo filter,
blur filter and edge detection.
We're going to look at different perspectives on convolution, like how it's related to matrix multiplication,
as well as the concept of cross correlation, which is a topic in the field of probability and statistics.
So if you think you know enough convolution for now, feel free to skip ahead and see how convolution
is used in a neural network.

--- Alternative Views on Convolution ---

Hey, everyone, and welcome back to this class.
Convolutional Neural Networks Deep Learning in Python Part three.
Many students are confused by the convolution operator.
So in this lecture, we are going to look at some different but equivalent perspectives on convolution
that will help you relate convolution to something you understand better.
If you really want to gain more intuition about convolution, you should really take a course on signal
processing.
Only by doing many examples will you really get a feel for what convolution is doing.
The first alternative perspective is that all we're doing is just matrix multiplication.
Let's say we want to convolve two signals X of T, which is the original signal and W of T, which is
the filter or kernel.
As is typical, the filter is much smaller than the signal we want to filter.
So let's say x of t is a signal of length ten and w of t is a filter of length three.
Then we can create a matrix that contains W of T repeated multiple times in this pattern.
Notice how it shows up along the columns and it's shifted up by one each time we go to the right.
This is the same thing that convolution does.
It shifts the filter along the signal and multiplies it at each point.
So this is one way of looking at convolution.
It's just matrix multiplication where the matrix is the filter repeated multiple times along the columns.
If you multiply W and X, you get an expression.
That's exactly the expression we get if we convolve x with the original w.
The next alternative perspective comes from probability and statistics.
Specifically, we'll look at the cross correlation function.
This is easily confused with the correlation coefficient, which is just a number.
The correlation coefficient, sometimes referred to as R tells us how predictable one random variable
is from another.
So for example, if we have y equals two x plus one, this would give us a correlation coefficient of
one because y is exactly predictable given x.
If we have Y equals minus two x plus one.
This would give us a correlation coefficient of minus one because y is exactly predictable given x,
but as x increases, y decreases.
When we're talking about the cross correlation function, the idea is similar.
It tells us how predictable one time series is in terms of another, but the result is another function,
not just the number.
They get confused because they're both referred to as just correlation, but they're in fact different
things.
The cross correlation function applies to sequences, but the correlation coefficient applies to time
independent random variables.
This will probably only help you if you've heard of the correlation function before.
If you haven't, it'll probably just end up confusing you more.
So feel free to skip it.
Before we look at cross correlation, it's helpful to look at Autocorrelation first.
As you might expect, the autocorrelation function just tells you how related a sequence is to itself.
The result of the autocorrelation is a function where the independent variable is called the lag, which
we usually represent with the symbol tau.
I'm showing you the formula for the correlation function here, but we'll be using a built in function
in scipy called correlate to compute it.
The main thing I want you to notice is that this looks a lot like convolution, except that instead
of a plus, we have a minus inside the integral.
A typical autocorrelation function looks like this.
There is usually a peak at the middle where tau is zero and then it quickly goes to zero as tau increases.
This makes sense because you'd expect, for example, the weather today to be highly correlated to the
weather yesterday, but not at all correlated to the weather 100 days ago.
In code, we'll create a sequence of random Gaussian variables with zero mean and unit variance.
We know that each sample is independent of the others.
Hence there should be zero correlation between any two variables in the sequence.
So for example, x zero is uncorrelated with X1X2 and so on.
X zero is only correlated with itself.
In other words, we expect that the correlation for a lag of zero is a big number, but the correlation
for any other lag is zero.
You are encouraged to try this?
Encode yourself.
The result if you plot the autocorrelation, is that we see a large spike where the lag is zero and
near zero values for any other lag.
Of course, it's not perfectly zero due to random variation, but we can see that the values for any
non zero lag are significantly less than the center value.
Now let's create a signal y, which we know is correlated with X specifically at a lag of 100.
In other words, y zero can be predicted from x 100 y one can be calculated from x, 101 and so on.
The result is that we see a spike at a lag of 100 and near zero values for any other lag.
So what's the lesson here?
Well, now we know what cross correlation does.
It gives you spikes anywhere.
The two signals are highly correlated given a certain displacement or lag.
And we also know that the cross correlation function is just the convolution of the two signals, but
flipping one of the signals first.
And hence we can think of convolution as just the cross correlation of a signal and the reverse filter.
In other words, the filter can be a signal close to some pattern we're looking for.
And then after doing a convolution, we'll see spikes with a pattern occurred.
So that's one way of thinking about convolution and filters.
Filters are the pattern we're looking for and doing convolution with the filter will give us spikes
where the pattern was found.

--- Convolution on 3-D Images ---

Hey, everyone, and welcome back to this class.
Convolutional Neural Networks Deep Learning in Python Part three.
In this lecture, we're going to go over an important detail about how convolution is implemented in
neural networks in particular.
For the examples we did previously, we looked at one dimensional signals like audio and two dimensional
signals like black and white images.
But we know that images in the real world can be in color.
And when we have color, we have a three dimensional object.
Depending on whether you're using Theano or TensorFlow, an image can be shaped color by height, by
width or height, by width by color.
In fact, in convolutional neural networks, it is always assumed that we're working with 3D images.
In this lecture you'll see why that is and also how that works.
So just to review, let's look at what convolution does.
Again, we have an image which we assume is the bigger blue box and we have a filter which we assume
is the smaller, darker box.
Then we slide this filter over every possible position along the image.
And each time we do that, we multiply every overlapping point element wise, then add them all together
to get the output image.
So when we do convolution, our input is a 2D image and our output is also a 2D image.
Let's build on this idea now.
We know that to do edge detection, we actually need two filters.
We have the vertical edge finder and the horizontal edge finder.
These are both represented as matrices, of course.
Well, suppose I want to add more filters, like a downward diagonal edge filter and upward diagonal
edge filter and so on.
I can of course do that too.
In fact, I can add all sorts of filters that you can't even think of.
The question is, after I do say these two convolutions, I'm going to have to output images.
If I have two images, what do I do with them?
How can I input two images into the next layer of the neural network?
So in edge detection, we saw that we just took the magnitude of these sum of squares of the outputs.
But we can do something more general here.
The solution is to simply stack the output images together because all the filters are the same size
and all the output images are the same size.
Stacking them together is allowed.
So suppose my input image is of size height by width.
My filter is of size, k by k, and I have two of them.
For convenience, let's just assume that each filter is a square with equal height and equal width.
Then I will have two output images, both of size height by width.
Again, for convenience, assume we're doing convolution in same mode so the input has the same dimensionality
as the output.
I can then stack these images to get a 3D object of size height by width by two.
Also note that since I have two K by K filters, I can also just combine these into one object and say
it's a k by k by two filter.
This brings us one step closer, but we're not done yet.
Now we're ready to add another piece to this puzzle.
Originally, we assume that our input image was of size height by width, but we know that in a CNN
we're going to have multiple layers of convolutions.
That means if the output of a convolution is 3D like we just showed and that's being fed into another
convolution in the next step, then we're going to have to generalize this concept to work with not
just images of size, height by width, but also 3D images.
We can say that they're of size, height by width by C.
Of course, I chose the letter C because this is also how we're going to represent color images.
Recall that color images are usually represented with separate red, green and blue color channels.
So an input image, if it has color, will always be of shape, height by width by three.
And we can generalize black and white images by saying its height, by width by one rather than just
height by width.
Now the question is how do we do convolution on a color image?
The answer is actually not that intuitive, but it's simple once you know it.
So instead of multiplying 2D blocks element wise and summing them, we multiply 3D blocks element wise
and sum them.
So that means for a 3D image, we have a 3D filter and the process of convolution here is exactly the
same.
We slide the filter along the height and width and in each position we multiply everything element wise
and add them all together to get the output.
So we see that this purple dot at the output is a result of multiplying element wise.
All the numbers between the filter on the left with the image block on the left and adding them all
together.
On this slide, I've broken up the 3D image into three distinct color channels so you can see each 2D
part separately.
So you can see we've sort of unstacked the 3D image.
So you can see that for every spot on the image, we're going to multiply by the corresponding part
of the filter.
The result is just all these element wise products sum together, which is of course a scalar.
In the next step, we slide the filter over, multiply everything element wise in the block again,
add them all together again, and this gives us the blue dot at the output and so on.
So notice how the depth channel or the color channel goes away similar to a dot product.
When you take the dot product between two vectors, you get a scalar because one dimension goes away.
Similarly, when you multiply two matrices together, the inner dimension goes away because it gets
some doubt.
Same thing happens here.
But now you notice we have kind of the opposite problem as before.
So before our input was 2D and our output by using multiple filters was 3D.
Now our input is 3D, but our output is 2D.
So how can we reconcile this?
Well, you'll notice that for the filter we looked at on the previous slide, I very strategically placed
the color channel at the front of the filter.
So that filter was of shape C by K by K.
Of course, from here we can apply the same concept as before.
So instead of having just one filter, we could have multiple different filters which are of course
going to find multiple different features.
We can have a color vertical edge finder and a color horizontal edge finder and so on.
So instead of just one filter of size, C by K by K, I have two filters of size, C by K by k.
Or if I concatenate them together, one filter with an extra dimension of size.
C by k by k by two.
Now to avoid having to pick another letter to use.
I'm going to say the number of color channels in the input is C1 and the number of different output
images is C2.
Of course, you can now see the convergence of these two concepts.
On the one hand, we can think of this third dimension as different color channels like RGB.
On the other hand, we can think of this third dimension as the concatenation of different output images
After doing convolution, just like with feedforward neural networks, we call these features or in
a CNN we call them feature maps.
So we can say that the number of output feature maps is C two.
So concatenating everything together.
Our input is of shape, height by width by C1, Our filter is of shape C one by K, by K by C two,
which you can also think of as just C two different color filters.
And our output image is of shape height by width by C two.
So now our input and output images are both 3D images and that is consistent with the idea of having
multiple layers of convolutions one after the other.
Each input, output and filter have consistent dimensionalities.
So one final note I want to make while we discussed this lecture in the context of edge detection and
we had to output feature maps, this is, of course, for example, only in the real world we're going
to have hundreds or thousands of feature maps.
The number of feature maps is considered a hyper parameter and we can optimize it using techniques we
learned in the prerequisites, such as random search.
It's also important to note that while we used edge detection filters as an example, there should be
no guarantee nor expectation that our neural network is going to find features that do edge detection.
The neural network is going to optimize the filters using gradient descent on the cost function, which
is the main principle behind machine learning.
We don't want to manually decide what features should be found, since that will be suboptimal compared
to what a computer can find automatically.
And one more final, final note.
The edge detection example is actually a great way to reason about the Lynette architecture, where
we have a series of convolutions followed by a series of fully connected layers.
Recall that a series of fully connected layers in a neural network can be thought of as a universal
function.
Approximator.
So let's say it's trying to find some function F of x.
Now let's go back to what our edge detection algorithm was doing.
We took the original image, we passed it through the vertical edge filter and got out one image.
We passed it through the horizontal edge filter and got out another image.
Then we squared these images, added them together, and then took the square root.
We can think of this as just an arbitrary function.
So you see that a convolutional neural network is just a generalization of this idea.
We do multiple convolutions and then find some useful function out of the features that were found by
those convolutions.

--- Tracking Shapes in a CNN ---

Hey, everyone, and welcome back to this class.
Convolutional Neural Networks Deep Learning in Python Part three.
In this lecture, we are going to look more in depth at how data passes through a CNN.
This is going to give you a better idea of what everything looks like and you're going to get to know
the exact shapes of every object in a CNN.
This is really going to help you a lot with understanding the implementation.
So it's worth going through very slowly.
First, let's talk about what we know so far.
We know that a typical lean architecture is as follows.
We do a convolution, then a maxpool, then another convolution and another maxpool.
Then we flatten the data and pass it through a couple dense layers.
These are just the same kinds of layers you see in a feedforward neural network.
In this lecture, we're going to look at the precise shape of every object as it passes through the
CNN.
To motivate this discussion.
Let's remember how we would do this in a regular feedforward neural network.
To start, we have some input vector.
Let's say it's of size 784.
We know that each layer consists of a weight matrix and a bias vector.
In general, the size of the weight matrix is M one by M two, where M one is the size of the input
vector and M two is the size of the output vector.
Because of this.
The size of the bias vector is also of length M two.
So in our example, the first weight matrix is of size 784 by 1000, and the second weight matrix is
of size 1000 by ten.
From this, you should immediately be able to tell what the size of the bias vectors are.
So we'll ignore them for the remainder of this example.
So let's go back to the input vector.
It's of size 784.
What happens after we pass it through the first weight matrix?
That vector gets transformed into a size 1000 vector.
This is just due to the rules of matrix multiplication.
Next we would add the bias term and apply the activation function such as a Relu.
But importantly, we know that this does not change the shape.
Next we pass this 1000 length vector into the second layer, which means we multiply it by a 1000 by
ten matrix.
And of course, this gives us a length ten vector.
Typically our output activation would be a softmax, which again does not change the shape.
So our vector goes from size 784 to size, 1000 to size ten.
Pretty simple.
Before we go on to a full CNN, let's recall some nuances regarding convolution.
When you do a convolution, the output shape is always approximately the same size as the input shape,
but not quite.
It depends on what mode you use.
So there's valid mode.
Same mode and full mode.
So let's review these first.
Invalid mode.
Your filter never touches the outside of the input.
So if you let your input length be n and your filter length be K, then your output will always be of
length n minus k plus one.
Full mode is kind of the opposite of valid mode.
Instead of sliding the filter through the inside of the input, we're allowed to go outside.
We pretend that the outside is just full of zeros so we can go as far as we can to get any values that
are possibly non-zero.
Recall that we did this calculation in the previous section.
Another way to think of this is that we have padding on both sides of size.
K minus one.
What this does is on the extreme left or the extreme right, it always gives us one box to multiply
to get the output.
You can easily verify that the output is going to be of size N plus K minus one.
Next.
We have same mode.
Same mode is a little awkward because instead of having infinite padding or a preset amount of padding,
the amount of padding is set so that the output is the same size as the input, hence the name, same
mode.
As an example, if we have N equals five and K equals three, then we would add a padding of size one
on both sides and therefore the output becomes size five, which is the same size as the input.
Note that in piano this is called half padding rather than same mode due to the fact that the amount
of padding is approximately half of the filter length.
But if N equals seven and K equals five, then we would add padding of size two on both sides.
And due to symmetry, this is why you usually see filters with an odd number as the length.
This makes it easy to break up the padding so that both sides of the input have the same amount of padding.
Now that we've reviewed the different kinds of padding, let's do a simple example with just one convolution.
Remember that images are 3D.
So let's suppose my input image is of size 32 by 32 by three.
My filter or kernel is of size.
Three by five by five by 16.
What is the output image size going to be?
It's important to remember that different libraries use different ordering conventions.
In this particular lecture, I put the input feature maps at the front of the filter and the output
feature maps at the end of the filter.
This makes it easy to see which is the input and which is the output similar to a weight matrix which
is of size.
M one by M two.
In this lecture it's always going to be C one by K by K by C two.
Assuming that the filter is a square of length k.
But don't be surprised if I use some other ordering in a different lecture.
And also remember that each deep learning library uses something different.
In fact, some libraries don't even use the RGB coloring convention.
So ultimately, it's important to just read the documentation.
So for this example, let's assume we're using convolution in valid mode.
Luckily, we've done most of these calculations in the previous lecture.
So we know that the length of the image becomes 32 minus five plus one, which is 28.
And we know that the number of output feature maps is 16.
So our output image is of size 28 by 28 by 16.
And one really important thing to keep in mind is that convolution always works no matter what your
input image size is.
So we could have taken the same filter three by five, by five by 16 and applied it to an image of size,
64 by 64 by three.
Assuming again that we're using valid mode, the output is going to be of size 60 by 60 by 16.
So that's a really interesting fact about convolution.
It works for any size image.
Note that this is not the case for a dense layer weight matrix.
However, if you have a 784 by 1000 weight matrix, then the input vector must be of size 784.
Otherwise you can't multiply it by that weight matrix.
Let's look at the pooling operation next.
We know that for max pooling, if we have a pool size of two by two, we're going to look at every two
by two block in an image and we take the max of those four pixels.
So in this image, we can see that the biggest number in the Red Square is six.
So the output for that portion becomes six.
We can see that the maximum for the green portion is eight.
The maximum for the yellow portion is three and the maximum for the blue portion is four.
Importantly, notice how the output size is half of the input size.
So the input length is four, but the output length is two.
Four divided by two is two.
So because of this for a pool size of two.
The output length is always going to be half the length of the input.
So if we take our 28 by 28 by 16 image, the output is going to be 14 by 14 by 16.
Keep in mind that we don't pull over features.
We only pull over the spatial dimensions.
And now that we have both convolution and pooling, we're ready to track the shapes through a full CNN.
So let's start with the following architecture.
We have convolution pooling, convolution pooling, flatten dense, and another dense.
We'll be looking at the sizes of each filter and weight matrix as we go along.
Since, as you'll see, it's going to be dependent on the data itself.
So first, let's assume our input image is of size 32 by 32 by three.
Using the same example as before, our filter is going to be of size three by five by five by 16.
Therefore, our output image size as discussed, will be of size 28 by 28 by 16.
Next we do a max pooling with a pool size of two by two, which is the same example we had before.
So the output image from there will be 14 by 14 by 16.
Next.
Our second convolutional filter will be of size 16 by five, by five by 32.
Passing our image through this, we get ten by ten by 32.
And then we do another max pool with a pool size of two by two.
This gives us five.
By five by 32.
Next.
We have a couple dense layers, but recall that dense layers must take as input.
A vector.
A vector is a 1D object and we have an image which is a 3D object.
So that means we have to flatten the image first before passing it through the dense layer.
And because a dense layers weight matrix shape depends on the input size.
That means we must first know what the size of our input vector will be.
To get this value, we just multiply five times, five times 32, which is 800.
So let's oppose our weight matrix is of size 800 by 1000.
Then after passing our data through this dense layer, our data vector is now of size 1000.
One important thing to keep in mind is something I mentioned earlier that convolution works on any size
image.
So if the input image size changes, then the output image size also changes and therefore the size
of the dense layers.
Weight matrix is dependent on the size of the input images in your dataset.
Because of this limitation.
It means all your images must be of the same dimensionality.
This is not a problem most of the time because many datasets contain images of constant size.
In a more advanced course in the future.
We'll look at how to handle images of variable size.
In the last part is easy since we've already done this.
Let's say we have ten output classes.
Our final weight matrix is therefore of size 1000 by ten, and our output vector is of size ten.
Now that we've done a full run of passing data through a CNN, let's talk about a few more things that
are going to help you in your implementation.
First.
We only considered one image just now, but we know that in deep learning we like to do batch gradient
descent and process multiple images all at once.
To do this?
Nothing.
We just did changes.
If we assume our batch size is n.
Then at each layer the data simply has n prepended to its shape.
So our input size is n by 32.
By 32 by three.
After the first convolution, it's n by 28.
By 28.
By 16.
After pooling, it's n by 14.
By 14, by 16.
And at the end it's n by ten, which is exactly the right shape for the output of a neural network with
ten classes.
This makes sense because if you have n input images, then you must have n output predictions.
Another thing we want to discuss is how do we choose the size of the filter in a CNN?
We know that this is a hyper parameter, so generally it must be chosen by experimentation.
But is there some kind of pattern we can follow?
This is a question I get a lot, so it's worth answering here.
The general pattern you may have noticed is this As we do, each convolution, two things happen.
Number one, the image shrinks.
This is due to pooling.
Number two, the number of feature maps increases.
This is not due to anything, just that we choose the feature maps such that they increase with each
convolution.
After you get to a big enough number, typically it stays constant.
You can think of the number of feature maps like the features in a feedforward neural network layer.
One good way to get a feel for what typical CNNs look like is to look at some famous architectures and
see what they did.
Read the literature, too, so you get a sense of who's doing what.
What's the most common, and so on.
We'll discuss famous CNN architectures more in depth in a later lecture.
The last thing I want to discuss in this lecture is how to think about what a CNN has learned.
CNN's are interesting because they work on images.
Now, I know I'm stating the obvious for most of you, but images are things we can see because we can
see images.
It's relatively simple to visualize what a CNN has learned in its filters.
This is a great triumph for deep learning because people often say you can't interpret what a neural
network is learning.
Well, actually, you can.
You can literally see it.
So a pattern that is often found by researchers is that each subsequent layer of convolution finds a
more and more complex feature representation of the input.
For example, at the first layer you might see some basic edges at different angles.
At the next layer, you might begin to see some shapes.
At a later layer.
You might see some macro structures like full faces or other objects.
The reason why this is is quite intuitive.
As we discussed on the last slide, as we go through each layer, the image itself is shrinking due
to pooling.
So the image starts out as 32, then it goes down to 14, then it goes down to five.
But the filters generally stay the same size around a three or 5 or 7.
So if the filter takes up the same space but the image is shrinking, then the filter is effectively
finding larger and larger patterns relative to the size of the image.
And these big patterns are made up of weighted linear combinations of smaller patterns from previous
layers.
So it's almost like you're building up full objects from basic edges one layer at a time.

--- Advanced CNNs and how to Design your Own ---

Hey, everyone, and welcome back to this class.
Convolutional Neural Networks Deep Learning in Python Part three.
In this lecture, we are going to look at some common design choices when it comes to convolutional
neural networks.
It might seem at first that there are way too many choices to make.
However, you'll see that most CNNs follow the same typical pattern.
So why is this important?
Well, you may have noticed that with each new thing we learn about in deep learning, the more choices
we have.
These choices are called hyper parameters.
