And as we discussed in deep learning, Part two, when you continue to increase the number of hyper
parameters in your model, you encounter the curse of dimensionality.
The space of possible choices increases geometrically.
So it's good to be able to narrow down our choices by looking at some common patterns and following
those rather than doing things completely randomly.
So what are these patterns?
Well, here's the first one.
If we look at the net, we notice that it consists of two parts.
The first part is a series of convolutions.
The second part is a series of fully connected layers.
This is pretty much always the case in a convolutional neural network, whether that's the lenet or
even very recent CNNs that have come out in the past few years.
So you wouldn't do something like alternate convolutions and fully connected layers or put the fully
connected layers first?
Convolutional layers always come first.
There is one exception to this that you will see in the future, and that's when we look at neural networks
that can generate images.
So a more general way of thinking about this rule is this for the neural networks we looked at in this
course, we were doing classification at the input.
We have an image that's where the convolutions go.
At the output, we have a vector.
We know that it represents probabilities, but to look at it in a more general way is just a vector
of numbers.
Now, this next part may be a little confusing to you if you've never encountered unsupervised learning
before, So feel free to ignore this part if you want.
In unsupervised learning, we have the concept of a latent vectors.
Instead of using the letter Y, we usually represent them with the letter Z.
The idea is we can learn how to generate images from a vector.
So we do the reverse from what we do in classification.
Here we go from vector to image.
So naturally in this architecture we have the fully connected layers first and then convolutional layers
second.
So to generalize this concept.
Fully connected layers go on the side of the neural network that has a vector convolutional layers go
on the side of the neural network that has an image.
You may have also noticed another pattern in the Lynette specifically regarding the convolutional layers.
We can think of the data at each layer as an image and we are doing a new convolution over a new image
at each layer.
This requires you to think of an image in a really general way.
First, we see that each image is a 3D object that has height and width, and these are the directions
in which we do the convolution.
There's also depth which in a real image we refer to as the color channels, which are typically RGB,
red, green and blue.
In the later layers, the image still has depth, but they are not colors per se.
They are feature maps or if you prefer, just features.
Or you could even call them feature channels.
Okay, so at every layer we have an image.
It is 3D, it has height, width and depth.
So what's the pattern we see?
Well, we see that in each layer, the height and width decrease, but the depth increases.
This is typically always the case.
Sometimes they'll stay the same size, but it will never go in the opposite direction.
You typically won't end up with less depth and a bigger height and width.
So this is one pattern you can always follow when you're choosing hyper parameters In a CNN, the image
dimensions usually decrease and the depth usually increases.
Of course, there's again one exception to this, and that's when you're generating images, when you
generate an image, the thing at the end of the neural network must have three color channels.
If it's a color image or one color channel, if it's a black and white image, that pretty much just
comes from the format of images.
We don't know how to display an image with ten color channels, for example.
It just doesn't exist.
So you must end up with either 3 or 1 color channels.
And so with generative neural networks, we again go in the opposite direction from the feature vector.
We start with a large depth and a small height and width.
And at each layer we decrease the depth, but we increase the height and width.
The final pattern we want to discuss is the fully connected layers.
This generally doesn't follow as strict of a pattern as you can find many different architectures out
in the wild.
I think the most typical case is either you keep a constant hidden layer size or you decrease the number
of hidden units at each layer.
Keeping it constant doesn't hurt the neural network, so it may be a good approach.
If you want to limit the number of choices you have to make.
Then all you have to do is pick the number of hidden units for one layer and apply that to all the hidden
layers and then pick the number of hidden layers.
So just two choices.
Number of hidden units and number of hidden layers.
But each layer has the same number of hidden units.
Now that we understand the basic pattern, let's look at some architectures that have implemented these
patterns.
I think these are really important to look at because what you'll see is that while they may seem really
fancy by name, they don't really involve any new ideas.
Everything you need to understand them.
You already learned in this course.
That's the power of understanding the fundamentals.
Most of these architectures are famous because they perform very well on the ImageNet competition.
The ImageNet dataset is a famous benchmark of millions of images that deep learning and computer vision
researchers around the world use to compare their algorithms.
The first example I'm going to show you is a very popular model called Vgg.
There are actually different types of architectures that fall under Vgg with just a different number
of layers.
So let's look at Vgg 16 as an example.
This does two convolutions Maxpool.
Two more convolutions Maxpool.
Three more convolutions Maxpool.
Three more convolutions Maxpool and three final convolutions and a final maxpool.
Then we have three fully connected layers, so that's 16 layers in total.
Hence the name Vgg 16.
So you can see that this is really no different from what we studied in this course, except it just
has more layers.
That's pretty cool.
If you want to read more about VG, I've added the original paper to extra reading dot txt.
In the course repo, the title is very deep Convolutional networks for large scale image recognition.
They appeared in the 2014 Image Net Competition.
And by the way, VG stands for Visual Geometry Group, which is the name of the research group that
invented the architecture.
The next example I'm going to show you is Alexnet.
Geoff Hinton also appears as an author on the Alexnet paper, which is pretty cool.
Not surprisingly, Alexnet also consists of a series of convolutions, followed by a series of fully
connected layers.
In particular, it is five convolutional layers and three fully connected layers.
Eight layers in total.
You may be wondering why it is smaller than Vgg.
The reason is Alexnet appeared in the 2012 ImageNet competition two years before Vgg.
So the general pattern in deep learning has been to just go deeper and deeper.
Over the years, more and more powerful GPUs have made this possible.
There is some slides on Alexnet in extra reading dot txt under the title ImageNet classification with
deep convolutional neural networks if you want to check it out.
The final architecture we're going to talk about is googlenet.
As you can see, this even has Lynette in its name.
Googlenet has 22 layers and is again a combination of convolutional layers followed by fully connected
layers.
The main novel idea in Googlenet is the inception model.
I won't go into the details in this course, but the main principle is simple.
So the main principle behind Inception is that instead of having to choose the filter size for each
convolution, we instead do multiple convolutions of different sizes and concatenate the results together
along the depth dimension.
Since depth in an image is analogous to features, what we're doing is taking all the features found
by the convolutional filters of different sizes and using all of those features.
We also take the features found by simply average pooling.
Backpropagation will automatically pick the best features, whatever they may be.
If you want to read more about Googlenet, check out Going deeper with convolutions in extra reading
dot txt in the course repo.
So to summarize, I hope this lecture convinced you that modern convolutional neural networks are nothing
but variations on a theme, and you pretty much already have all the tools you need to implement them.
Of course, practicality is also important to consider.
You already saw how long it takes to train a CNN in this class.
Now imagine doubling, tripling or even quadrupling the size of these tiny CNNs.
And you get an idea of how much computational power it requires.
In future courses, we'll look at how we can make use of large neural networks like these without having
to train them from scratch.
But I hope that you'll be able to use the ideas in this lecture to invent your own convolutional neural
networks.
You've seen that some pretty famous inventions involve no more than simply adding more layers.

--- Mean Squared Error ---

In this lecture we are going to discuss the mean squared error in depth.
The goal of this lecture is to ensure that you understand the mean squared error from a probabilistic
perspective.
This helps to give some theoretical backing for the mean squared error and it will help prepare us to
discuss the cross entropy loss as well.
As a side note, I just want to remind you that there are multiple names that we use interchangeably.
So if you hear error or loss or cost or objective, these are all generally referring to the same thing.
So we might say cross entropy error, but we also might say cross entropy loss.
They mean exactly the same thing.
First, let's just make sure the mean squared error makes sense in the first place.
Why is it squared?
Well, the idea is that we would like the error to always be positive.
Imagine this scenario.
One of your predictions has error plus two.
The other prediction you've made has error minus two.
If you add these two errors together, you would get plus two minus two, which is just zero.
Of course, this is not zero error because both of your predictions are off by two.
They should not cancel each other out.
So squaring the error is a nice way to make it positive.
But this still doesn't really explain why we want the square.
Why not the absolute value?
Why not some other method of making the difference positive.
In fact, it's perfectly okay to use the mean absolute error.
We just don't normally refer to that as linear regression.
So when we say linear regression, usually we are talking about a linear model using the mean squared
error.
Okay, so let's dig a little deeper on why we would want to square the error.
To understand this discussion, you have to be familiar with maximum likelihood estimation what you
would normally learn in an introductory probability course.
Let's say, for example, we would like to measure the heights of all the students in our class and
we decide to model this as a Gaussian distribution.
As you recall, the Gaussian distribution is the typical bell curve.
It has the probability density function that you see here, which will become important momentarily.
We know intuitively that the best way to estimate mu let's call that mu hat is the sample mean of all
the heights we have measured.
The question is why is this so?
Here's how we solve this problem.
There is actually a method to find this mu hat.
This method is called maximum likelihood estimation, as I mentioned earlier.
So what are the steps?
First, we create the likelihood function.
Let's call that L.
Unfortunately, we use the letter L for a lot of things in deep learning.
So please don't confuse this L with anything else we've discussed.
The likelihood is the multiplication of the probability density function at each of the x values we've
collected.
We consider this to be a function of mu.
So here Mu is the variable and the x's are constants because they are just numbers.
They are the heights of the students that we have measured in our class.
The next thing we would like to do is maximize L with respect to Mu.
Let's think about why we would want to do this.
Just for intuition.
Imagine that you measure someone's height x.
Now imagine Mu is very far away from x.
In this case, the probability of x is very tiny.
We might think that x is not from this probability distribution because p of x yields a very small value.
But what if Mu is equal to x?
In this case, p of x yields a very large value.
Thus we want p of x to be big if it is to be representative of the data we've collected.
So it's the same idea for Elle.
We want Elle to be big so that Mu is close to all of the data points x that we have collected.
So how do we maximize Elle with respect to Mu?
Luckily we can just ask our old friend calculus.
Normally the process would be take the derivative of L with respect to Mu and then set this derivative
to zero and solve for mu.
But for probabilities, taking the log of l first usually leads to a more easily solvable derivative.
The reason why this is allowed is because the log is a monotonically increasing function.
Maximizing L is the same as maximizing the log of L.
The value of mu that maximizes l is the same as the value of mu that maximizes log l.
You can prove this to yourself intuitively by taking any two numbers A and B, if A is bigger than B,
It is also the case that log A is bigger than log B.
Okay.
So I'm not going to bore you with the calculation, but as always, you are welcome to do it yourself
on paper using your trusty calculus skills.
You should end up with the derivative we see here.
So if you want, please pause the video and make sure you can take this derivative yourself.
Okay, so what do we get if we set this to zero and solve for mu?
Well, you can see that we get back our original answer that mu is the sample mean of x.
This is called the maximum likelihood estimate of Mu because in order to find it, we maximize the likelihood.
Here's what's important to notice about the log likelihood.
If you notice, the log likelihood has the same form as this expression where I've replaced the constant
terms with C one and C two.
You'll realize that it actually doesn't matter what value C one and C two have.
They don't affect the answer we get eventually that mu is the sample mean of x, although we do recognize
that c two is positive.
C1 is also positive given our old expression, but it actually doesn't matter what it is at all.
In other words, they are just superfluous constants.
So what happens if we set these constants to convenient values such as zero and one over n?
Well, we get an even more interesting expression, little El.
The thing we want to maximize is the negative sum of x i minus mu squared.
One thing you may recall from your study of calculus is that maximizing something is the same as minimizing
the negative of that thing.
In other words, if I want to maximize negative X squared, I should get the same answer as minimizing
x squared.
The value x equals zero is the answer in both cases.
Hopefully you can see right away that this is obvious.
At this point, you probably get the gist of what I'm trying to say.
Maximizing the likelihood is the same as minimizing the mean squared error.
Where the error is the difference between x I and mu.
So how can we relate this back to problems such as linear regression?
Take a look at these two error functions.
The first error function comes from our earlier problem finding the best Mu to model the heights of
the students in our class as a Gaussian.
The second error comes from linear regression.
Make the Y hats close to the Y's.
How can we relate the second problem to the first problem?
Well, if we say that the y i's are samples drawn from a Gaussian distribution with a mean is y hat,
I then minimizing this mean squared error is the same as minimizing the negative log likelihood.
And that of course is the same as maximizing the log likelihood, which is the same as maximizing the
likelihood.
Finally, one thing we can do is instead of saying that the mean of the distribution is y hat, we can
simply say that y minus y hat is a Gaussian centered at zero.
Equivalently, this is saying that when we use the squared error, we are making the assumption that
the error of our model with respect to the data is Gaussian distributed with mean zero.
This formulation helps us to put a probabilistic backing on the error function and it will also help
us make sense of the cross entropy loss for classification.

--- Binary Cross Entropy ---

In this lecture, we are going to discuss the binary cross entropy loss, which is the correct loss
function to use when we are doing binary classification.
Let's recap the actual loss function and then we will use the rest of this lecture to determine how
to arrive at this loss function.
Since we know that this is going to be based in probability, let's think about the associated probability
problem.
We know that the outcomes, the Y's must be binary.
That means the targets in a binary classification problem must be 0 or 1.
Spam or not.
Spam, fraud or not fraud.
ET cetera.
The next question is what distribution is used for binary events?
The answer is the Bernoulli distribution.
Probably the most typical example of the Bernoulli distribution is the coin toss.
So imagine if you tossed a coin a bunch of times and you want to calculate the probability of heads.
Call that mu.
Intuitively, we know that Mu is the number of heads divided by the total number of coin tosses.
Again, we are going to go through the process of maximum likelihood estimation to see how we can arrive
at this answer.
All right.
So let's say we've tossed our coin a bunch of times.
Let's call the results X1X2 all the way up to x n.
In this case, the X's can only take on two values 0 or 1.
Let's say one means heads and zero means tails.
In order to calculate the likelihood, we need the Bernoulli PMF, which is the equation you see here.
Now, there's one small difference between this example and the previous example on the squared error.
The difference is that for the previous example, we were working with heights which are continuous
valued.
Now we are working with coin tosses which are discrete.
A coin toss can only give you 0 or 1.
It can't give you say 0.5 or 0.9.
So we know from our study of probability that for continuous distributions we use the probability density
function.
But for discrete distributions we use the probability mass function or PMF.
So this is a PMF and it returns a probability rather than a probability density.
Since you already know the steps, let's get right to it.
We create our likelihood function, which is the product of the pmfs for each of the X's we've collected.
We know that we want to maximize the likelihood, but the step we have to do before taking the derivative
is that we want to find the log likelihood.
At this point, this equation should already look very familiar.
It's in the exact same form as the binary cross entropy except negated.
As usual, if you were to take the derivative of the log likelihood, set it to zero and solve for mu.
This is what you would get.
Curiously, we arrive at the same answer as the Gaussian case.
The sample mean of X.
How can this be?
Why isn't it number of heads divided by n?
In actuality, it is.
Remember that X can only take on the values one and zero.
So when x is zero, that's tails.
So they don't contribute anything to the sum of x.
The sum of x then, is just the sum of a bunch of ones, which is the number of times we flipped heads.
So in fact, this is exactly the same as number of heads divided by n.
As before, if we take the negative log likelihood and we put it side by side with the binary cross
entropy, we can see the similarities.
We can conclude that what the binary cross entropy is really saying is that y i is the result of a coin
toss where the probability of heads for that coin toss is given by y hat i.
Analogously Z is the result of a coin toss where Mu is the probability of heads for that coin toss.
As a side note, you'll want to keep in mind that we also usually divide the sum of the errors by N
so that we get the average binary cross entropy per data point.
This makes it just like the mean squared error in that the value is invariant to the number of samples
we have.
You can imagine that if we have lots of samples and we only use the sum, the error would be very large,
only due to the fact that we have a large number of samples.
In order to make the value of the error more meaningful, we can take the average and that makes our
error more interpretable.
So to conclude, what have we done in this lecture?
We have shown that just like the mean squared error, the binary cross entropy loss function is based
in probability.
What it says is that for the regression case where we use the mean squared error, it arises from the
Gaussian distribution for the binary classification case where we use the binary cross entropy.
It arises from the Bernoulli distribution.
Importantly, the common pattern between the two is that in both cases the error function is really
just the negative log likelihood and therefore the solution we end up finding in the end is called the
maximum likelihood solution.

--- Categorical Cross Entropy ---

In this lecture, we are going to derive the categorical cross entropy loss function, which is what
we use when we are doing multi-class classification.
At this point, you probably already know that the categorical cross entropy is the negative log likelihood
for some distribution.
So let's jump right in and try to figure out what this distribution is.
In the case where we only have binary outcomes, the correct discrete distribution is the Bernoulli
distribution.
So what is the correct distribution for multiple categorical outcomes?
Well, unlike the Gaussian and Bernoulli distributions, this distribution is not named after a person.
It is simply called, quite appropriately, the categorical distribution.
The usual analogy for the categorical distribution is a die roll.
So when you roll a die, there are six possible outcomes, typically one, two, three, all the way
up to six.
Of course, there are different kinds of dice where you may have even more possible outcomes.
In general, we will refer to the number of possible outcomes as K.
So what does the categorical distribution look like?
Well, it looks a little strange.
As you can see, it is a product.
Each of the Mu K's is a parameter telling us the probability that the result of the die roll was K.
In other words, mu k is equal to the probability that x is equal to K.
So what does this mean about the won function in the exponent?
This is what is called an indicator function.
It returns one when its argument is true and zero otherwise.
Knowing this, it's easy to confirm that if we plug in X equals one, we should get that the probability
that x is equal to one equals Mu one.
The probability that x is equal to two is mu two and so forth.
In other words, we can confirm that this is indeed the PMF for the categorical distribution.
As usual, we are going to pretend we've collected n data points and we are going to do maximum likelihood
estimation to find all the muse.
What is interesting about this likelihood is that because the likelihood is the product of Pmfs and
the PMF already has a product, we end up with two products in the likelihood.
Also notice that there are two indices in the exponent here.
There's the index I and also the index K, So that should remind you of a matrix because a matrix also
has two indices, the row and the column.
After taking the log.
We know that we get the log likelihood and we already know that the negative log likelihood is our categorical
cross entropy.
So if we take the negative of this expression, we will get back something that looks exactly like the
categorical cross entropy.
And thus we can relate this back to our categorical cross entropy loss function.
Notice here that it's a tiny bit different from our log likelihood and that we don't have an indicator
function anymore.
This is because an equivalent way of writing this is double indexing.
Y.
I k y.
I k is called a one hot encoded value because it can only take on the values 1 or 0.
Which row can only have one one.
Remember that I represents the sample index and K represents the class label.
The class label can only be one value for each sample.
So, for instance, if I roll a die, I can only get one number.
It must be 6 or 5 or 4 or 3.
It can't be six and three at the same time.
So as another example, if I have a picture and I'm classifying what type of animal it is, it can only
be one type of animal.
If it's a picture of a cat, it cannot simultaneously be a picture of a dog.
Therefore, only one of the K values can have a one representing the target.
The rest of the values must be zero.
You'll notice that one hot encoding the targets like this can be quite inefficient.
Let's do a simple example.
Suppose we have four samples, so N is four and three classes.
Cat, dog or bird?
Suppose the targets are cat, dog, bird and cat.
So numerically we can represent this with the vector.
One, two, three, one.
But in order to one hot encode this vector, we must turn it into a four by three matrix or an N by
k matrix containing only zeros and ones.
So the equivalent one hot encoding is 100010001100.
Now suppose my prediction probabilities also an N by k matrix are as follows.
So for each sample and each class, I have an associated probability.
It's the probability that I think the input belongs to that class.
After applying the categorical cross entropy formula, we get this value for the cost.
Now, you might be thinking that is a whole lot of multiplying by zero.
We already know that anything multiplied by zero is zero, so those values don't actually contribute
anything to the cost.
The only values that do matter are the ones which match the target.
Remember, the original target is the vector.
One, two, three, one.
What I would like to do ideally is to only take the log of these highlighted values and then add them
all together.
The question is, can I do that without one hot encoding the target first?
And indeed this is possible in numpy.
We can use the double indexing trick.
NumPy arrays allow you to index using iterables such as lists and other arrays instead of just single
integers.
So if I wanted to take my predictions and index only the relevant values, here's what I would do.
I would take y hat at I equals one and k equals one.
I would take y hat at I equals two and k equals two.
I would take y hat at I equals three and k equals three and I would take y hat at I equals four and
K equals one.
Using the NumPy double indexing method.
I can actually index all of these values at once.
I simply pass in all the row indices and all the corresponding column indices at the same time.
Importantly, note that by doing this I never have to one hot encode the targets.
I can pass them directly into the square brackets.
As an exercise.
You should try this in actual numpy code to verify that it really works.
Just note that in code we index starting at zero instead of one.
And so this is the idea behind the sparse categorical cross entropy.
So when you are in TensorFlow 2.0, if you use the regular categorical cross entropy, you are using
the one hot encoded target which requires n times K multiplications and additions.
If you use the sparse categorical cross entropy, you are using the original target which requires only
n multiplications and additions.
Therefore, it's usually better to use the original target and apply the sparse categorical cross entropy
loss.

--- Gradient Descent ---

In this lecture we are going to discuss gradient descent.
Gradient descent is extremely important because it's the algorithm that we use to train all of our models
from simple neurons to neural networks to convolutional neural networks and recurrent neural networks.
This one basic idea is the backbone of deep learning, and in fact, it can be used for other machine
learning models as well, such as K-means clustering hidden Markov models and matrix factorization.
Basically it is a general solution with wide applicability.
This lecture will discuss the basic intuitions about gradient descent, but if you are an expert in
mathematics, you might want to check out a rigorous proof that it works in a document.
I have attached an extra reading txt.
It's called Gradient Descent Convergence Analysis.
First, let's ask ourselves why do we need gradient descent in the first place?
What is the problem we are trying to solve?
Recall that for problems such as linear regression, we are going to come up with an error function
also known as a cost function or loss function.
Our goal is to minimize this error with respect to the model parameters.
Call that W.
So how do we minimize L with respect to W?
If you think back to your high school calculus studies, you'll realize that you've already encountered
this.
Whenever we have a function and we want to minimize it or maximize it with respect to its arguments,
what do we do?
We take its derivative and set it to zero.
Then we solve for the parameter in question.
So, for example, if you wanted to minimize five x squared plus three x plus one with respect to x,
you would find the derivative with respect to x, set it to zero and solve for x.
The reason why this works is because the derivative or the slope at the minimum and maximum of a function
is zero.
In other words, I can draw a line at this point tangent to the curve and it should be a horizontal
line.
Now, you might ask, how do we know if it's a maximum or minimum?
There are ways to determine that, but in machine learning, we generally do not do that analysis.
We pretty much already know that the loss functions we are working with have minimums and not maximums.
Sometimes we may worry about saddle points or local minima, but in general this is still not a problem
for the latest deep learning methods.
The reason why is outside the scope of this course.
But you are welcome to ask me about it on the Q&amp;A.
Okay.
This still doesn't answer the question.
Why do we need gradient descent?
Well, here's the problem.
Sometimes we can take the derivative and try to set it to zero and solve for the parameter.
But sometimes this equation is not solvable.
By the way, I've maybe once or twice had someone ask me why some equations are not solvable.
Well, my answer is if you think you can solve it, then do it and show me the answer.
You can use your intuition about other equations we do not know how to solve.
For example, we can't solve, let's say, the roots of a degree 100 polynomial.
It just simply isn't possible.
Okay, so what is the general strategy we use in mathematics when we cannot solve an equation?
The answer is we use numerical approximations.
One such example is finding integrals.
Sometimes you can't solve for an integral by hand.
I think anyone who has taken calculus knows that solving some integrals can be quite challenging and
for others, literally impossible.
So what do we do?
Well, instead of trying to solve the equation by hand, we just draw a little trapezoids underneath
the curve and we find the area of those trapezoids.
That will give us an approximation for the area under the curve.
Okay, so that's the analogy.
When we can't solve something analytically or by hand, we resort to numerical approximation methods.
So what's the idea behind gradient descent?
The idea is if I take the gradient of the function with respect to the parameter W and I take small
steps in that direction, the value of the function will decrease provided that my step size is small
enough.
Eventually, as I keep taking these very small steps, I'm going to get closer and closer to the bottom
of the curve and eventually I will converge to an answer.
By converge, I mean that when you get near the bottom, your steps will become so small that they will
become insignificant.
So W doesn't change as you take more steps.
At that point you have found the value of W that minimizes your function.
So what does this look like in code?
Basically, it's just a for loop.
Before the loop we initialize W to some random value.
Then inside the loop we continually update w using the gradient descent formula.
So here's an example of gradient descent at work.
Let's say we want to minimize W squared.
So that's our loss function.
The derivative is two w, so inside the loop we iteratively apply the equation W equals W minus eta
times two times w.
Now let's look at some code I've pasted that does this.
As you can see, I've initialized W to five.
I created an empty list called L where I'm going to store the loss at each iteration so that I can plot
it later.
Now in a loop, I run my gradient descent update 100 times and at each time I append W square to L.
Finally, I plot l.
We can see that eventually the value of the function goes down to zero.
Its minimum value.
The value of W that we find at the end is also zero because this is the value of W that makes the original
function zero.
Now you will notice that there are some hyper parameters that can't quite be ignored.
In particular, how many iterations should we run the loop for?
This is known as the number of epochs.
Of course we must have a number of epochs high enough so that the cost function converges, but it must
not be too high.
Otherwise we'll waste a lot of time where w simply doesn't change.
A How small should our step size be?
I've used the Greek symbol ETA for this, and we call it the learning rate.
The learning rate must be small enough so that the cost function doesn't explode, but it also must
be large enough so that the learning process doesn't take unnecessarily long.
Both of these hyper parameters are values that we must choose using trial and error.
There are a few more advanced methods such as Bayesian optimization that you could use also, but they
are outside the scope of this course.
Normally for students of machine learning, doing experiments manually and gaining intuition through
practice is the best approach.

--- Stochastic Gradient Descent ---

In this lecture we are going to discuss stochastic gradient descent.
Previously we looked at regular gradient descent and how it can be used to optimize a function.
But as you may know, in TensorFlow 2.0, the gradient descent option is called SGD, which is short
for stochastic gradient descent.
So what makes it stochastic and how is it different from non stochastic gradient descent?
To understand this, let's first think of a simpler problem.
Let's suppose we want to measure the average height of all humans in the entire world.
Now, that would be quite a hefty task.
There are 7 billion people in the world and it would take a very long time to ask each person individually.
But what if instead you took just 1000 random people and took the average height of these 1000 people?
Would you expect the average height of these 1000 to be similar to the average height of the entire
population?
The answer is yes.
It should be clear that the main advantage of asking only 1000 people instead of 7 billion people is
that it would take much less time.
Okay, so how is this relevant for deep learning?
If you recall, in deep learning, we have to calculate the cost, and from this cost we have to calculate
its gradient so that we can do gradient descent.
The problem is this cost depends on the number of training samples in our dataset.
So imagine you are training on the famous ImageNet dataset, which has about 1 million images.
This means that for each iteration of your training loop, you have to add up all the errors for all
1 million samples.
That's going to take an extremely long time.
But what if instead I took the average error over just 1000 images?
Of course they must be chosen randomly.
The same idea applies.
This average error is probably close to the average error over the entire dataset.
And so if we do gradient descent using only these 1000 images, we will probably end up taking a very
similar step just with 1000 times less computation.
In actuality, we typically use smaller batch sizes like 32, 64 or 128.
The basic algorithm looks like this.
Instead of just one loop over the epochs, we have two nested loops, one over the epochs and one over
the training set.
The idea is we are going to split up our data into batches and for each batch we are going to take one
step of gradient descent.
As you can see in this pseudocode, we are using a batch size of 32, So the number of iterations of
the inner loop is n divided by 32.
We can specify the start index as I times 32 and the end index as I plus one times 32.
So this will count from 0 to 32, 32 to 64, 64 to 96 and so forth.
On each epoch, we will make one pass through the entire dataset.
It's important to randomize the data on each epoch because running through the same samples in the same
order each time could lead to undesirable patterns being learned.
As an exercise.
You may want to try this out for yourself to prove that it is faster to set up your experiment.
Here's what you should do in the TensorFlow 2.0 fit function, you have an argument called batch size.
You can take any of the examples from this course and explicitly set the batch size to a small value
such as 32 and explicitly set it to the size of the entire dataset, whatever that may be.
From there, compare the time it takes for each method to converge to a minimum.
You should find that with batch gradient descent, the training process converges much faster.
And in fact, sometimes your data set may be so large that doing one step of gradient descent cannot
even fit into memory.

--- Momentum ---

Hey, everyone, and welcome back to this class.
Modern, deep Learning in Python.
Deep Learning in Python Part two.
In this lecture, we are going to look at what I consider to be one of the most effective improvements
over plane gradient descent called momentum.
Personally, I found it to be the 80% factor that can improve your learning procedure.
So how does momentum work?
I like the analogy of a ball rolling down a hill, which is nice because during gradient descent we
imagine the error surface as the bottom of a hill.
But I think today an even better analogy is moving something on a frictionless surface like ice.
So if you've ever gone skating before, you know that you can easily glide along the ice very quickly
and without much force because the momentum just carries you the way you were going before.
You can imagine pushing a box on ice and the box will move from one point to another quite easily because
you can push it once and it's going to slide.
That's exactly what happens in physics.
If there is no friction, then an object will just continue to go in the direction it was already going.
Of course, I still does have some friction, so eventually the box will slow down and eventually stop,
as you'll see.
Momentum in gradient descent also slows down after a while to.
To continue with this analogy, let's try to think of a situation without momentum.
Now keep in mind I'm talking about gradient descent momentum here, not physics momentum, since the
definition of momentum in physics is a little different.
So imagine now that instead of ice, you're trying to push a box in sand.
Now you can imagine that this is going to be very difficult because sand has lots of friction.
So if you push a box in sand, it's going to go as far as you push it and no more.
If you want the box to move again, you have to push it again.
That's like gradient descent without momentum.
Each time, if we want to move, there has to be a gradient so that we can move in the direction of
the gradient.
Now let's try to put these ideas into math.
Let's start with regular gradient descent without momentum.
So we have theta at time.
T is equal to theta at time T minus one minus the learning rate times the gradient g.
And from this we can see that if the gradient is zero, nothing is going to happen to theta.
It just gets updated to its old value and doesn't change.
Now let's look at gradient descent with momentum.
We use the term momentum very loosely here, since it has nothing to do with actual physical momentum.
So we create a new variable called V, which stands for the Velocity.
It's equal to Mu times, its old velocity minus the learning rate times the gradient.
And this is exactly what we talked about earlier when we imagined pushing a box on ice.
The G term is the effect of our pushing the box, but the mu times v of T minus one term is the effect
of continuing to move in the same direction as we were going before.
Now, we talked about how when you push a box on ice, even though there's less friction and the box
can slide, it's still going to stop eventually.
So we want the movie to take on only a fraction of the old V, And so a typical value for Mu, which
we also call the momentum term, is 0.9 or 0.9 5 or 0.99, somewhere in that area.
One thing you can immediately see is that if we plug in the expression for V of T, we can get the update
for theta of T in terms of only the old theta, the old V and the gradient g.
From here it's easy to see that if we set Mu to zero we just get back regular old gradient descent.
So what is the effect of using momentum?
Well, as you know, in all of our scripts, we like to plot the cost per iteration.
And so what we usually see is that the cost converges to its minimum value much faster than if we had
not used momentum.
So using momentum is pretty great because it significantly speeds up training.
Now I want to discuss one more perspective on momentum before we move on.
This is what is typically taught in deep learning courses these days.
So in this perspective, the problem that momentum is solving is if we have unequal gradients in different
directions.
So for visualization purposes, let's assume we have two parameters to optimize the vertical parameter
and the horizontal parameter.
So the gradient in one direction is very steep and the gradient in the other direction is very shallow.
The idea is if you don't have momentum, then you rely purely on the gradient, which points more in
the steep direction than in the shallow direction.
And this is just a property of the gradient.
It's the direction of steepest descent.
So since this gradient vector points more in the steep direction, then the result is we're going to
zigzag back and forth across this valley.
And that's a really inefficient way of reaching the minimum.
So what happens when we add momentum to this situation?
Well, since in the shallow direction, we move in the same direction every time those velocities are
going to accumulate.
So we'll have a portion of our old velocity added to our new velocity to help us along in that direction.
The result is that we get there faster by taking bigger steps in the direction of the shallow gradient.

--- Variable and Adaptive Learning Rates ---

Hey, everyone, and welcome back to this class.
Modern, deep Learning in Python.
Deep Learning in Python Part two.
In this lecture we are going to discuss variable and adaptive learning rates.
So far we've looked at momentum as a modification to vanilla backpropagation that can greatly speed
up training.
And from my perspective, I find momentum to be the most impactful.
If you compare the loss per iteration for standard gradient descent versus gradient descent with momentum,
the difference is huge.
At the same time, momentum is nice because you don't really have to play with the hyper parameters
that much.
Just go with 0.9 and it's probably fine.
So momentum is often my go to.
At the same time, some of these adaptive learning rate techniques are very powerful.
So let's have a look.
The first thing we'll talk about is variable learning rates or in other words, learning rates as a
function of iteration or time.
Sometimes people call this learning rate scheduling.
They are very simple, but they give you a lot to play with.
So the first one we're going to look at is called step decay.
Essentially what we do is periodically say every 100 steps we reduce the learning rate by a constant
factor.
So, for example, we divide by two or have it each time.
If we plot it, you can see that it kind of looks like a staircase.
The second method is called exponential decay.
In this method, the learning rate follows an exponential curve.
The third method is for the learning rate to decay proportionally to one over T.
You can control how fast or slow the learning rate decays by changing the proportionality constant.
Notice how the drop off is slower than exponential decay.
So what do all of these have in common?
Well, you can see that they all decrease the learning rate with time.
Why would we want to do that?
Well, generally speaking, when we initialize the weights of a neural network randomly, they're going
to be very far from the optimal weights.
So it's good to start with a large learning rate so that we can take bigger steps towards the goal.
This is the motivation behind momentum as well.
We want to pick up speed by accumulating past gradients because we know that if we are very far away
from our goal, then those gradients should be large.
But when we get close to the goal, the gradient is going to shrink.
In fact, by definition the minimum of a function necessitates a gradient of zero.
That's how you solve for a minimum of a function and calculus.
If you recall, you find the derivative and then you set it to zero and then you solve for the parameter
in question.
So why might we want to slow down when we get close to the minimum?
Well, when you're close to the minimum and you take too big of a step, you're going to overshoot.
And so what ends up happening is you just end up bouncing back and forth.
In fact, if your learning rate is too large, you'll just bounce right out of the valley and your loss
might actually increase.
So in order to reduce all this bouncing around, we would like to take smaller steps.
Here is another technique that people use which is related to these variable learning rate techniques.
Sometimes researchers and machine learning practitioners will actually sit there and babysit the neural
network training process.
So they'll do a couple of epochs and see how it goes.
If learning is happening too slowly, they might increase the learning rate.
If learning is starting to even out, they might decrease the learning rate.
But you have to be careful because you're not guaranteed to have a nice monotonically decreasing curve.
Sometimes you might get stuck in a relatively flat portion of the error surface, but this could just
be temporary.
And if you're patient enough, you might end up getting a steep drop.
But at the same time, this is not guaranteed.
As with most things in machine learning, the answer is that behavior is data dependent.
Waiting longer is desirable if you have the resources because then you get a full picture of the learning
process.
So manual learning rate scheduling is also an option for you to choose from.
Now, also important to think about what else do all these methods have in common?
Well, all of these methods add new hyper parameters to your list of things to optimize.
In fact, choosing between each of these methods can also be seen as a hyper parameter optimization.
So in one way, knowing about these techniques can help you, but from another perspective, it adds
more work to your plate.
The next couple techniques I want to talk about are what I like to call adaptive learning rate techniques,
because they adapt to the training data that you've seen so far.
The first one I want to discuss is called Adagrad.
The basic idea is this.
We can't expect the dependence of the cost on each of the parameters to be the same.
In other words, in one direction the gradient might be really steep, but in another direction the
gradient might be really flat.
So perhaps it may be beneficial to adapt the learning rate for each parameter individually based on
how much it has changed in the past.
So in Adagrad, what we do is we introduce a variable called the cache.
Each parameter of the neural network has its own cache.
So for example, if you have one weight matrix of size three by four, then you'll also have a cache
matrix of size three by four.
And the same thing goes for your bias vectors.
The idea behind the cash is that it's going to accumulate the squared gradients because we're squaring
the gradients.
The cash is always going to be positive and because each parameter has its own cash, then if one parameter
has had a lot of large gradients in the past, then its cash will be very large and its effective learning
rate will be very small.
So it will change more slowly in the future.
On the other hand, if a parameter has had a lot of small gradients in the past, then its cash will
be small, so its effective learning rate will remain large and it will have more opportunity to change
in the future.
One minor detail is that we usually add a small number epsilon to the denominator to avoid dividing
by zero.
Typically, this is set to ten to the -8 or 10 to the minus ten.
One important point to stress about Adagrad is that everything we're doing is an element wise operation,
so each scalar parameter is effectively updated independently of the others, which makes sense.
So you can look at the formulas we presented as scalar updates which apply to all the parameters.
Or you can think of one huge parameter vector that contains all the neural network parameters and then
each of the operations is an element wise operation.
This next technique builds on the fact that researchers observed that Adagrad decreases the learning
rate too aggressively.
So the learning rate would approach zero too quickly when in fact there was still more learning to be
done.
This technique is called Rmsprop and was invented by Jeff Hinton and his team.
So the way it works is this.
The reason Adagrad decreases the learning rate too quickly is because the cache is growing too fast.
So in order to make the cache grow less fast, we actually decrease it each time we update it.
We do this by taking a weighted average of the old cache and the new squared gradient.
We call this weight the decay rate, and you can see that the two weights add up to one.
We'll be looking at this form again and again throughout the course.
So don't worry about knowing all there is to know right now.
Just know that we'll be taking part of the old cash and part of the new squared gradient and adding
them together to get the new cash.
Typical values for the decay rate are 0.99 or 0.99 9 or 0.9999.
And so on.
Again, the intuition for these choices will be discussed in later lectures.
So by doing this, we say that we're making the cache leaky.
And the reason it's leaking is because you can imagine that if we had zero gradient for a long time,
eventually the cache would shrink back down to zero because it would be decreased by the decay rate
on each round.
One obstacle I realized some time after this course was originally released is that there is actually
some ambiguity in the RMS prop and Adagrad algorithms.
Specifically, I discovered this in the context of Rmsprop.
So one thing that's not specified in the RMS prop algorithm is the initial value of the cache.
Now, one might automatically just assume that zero is a good value and I did this as well.
But this actually has a very strange effect.
Let's suppose you choose a decay rate of 0.999.
Then your initial update for the cache will be 0.001 times g squared.
So you're taking 1/1000 of the squared gradient.
So disregarding Epsilon for the time being, your effective learning rate actually becomes very large
due to the fact that the denominator is very small.
What you'd have to do is compensate by making your initial learning rate smaller than usual.
One solution to this is to set the cash to one instead by manipulating the prop update a little bit.
We can show that this gives us approximately what the update would be if we weren't doing prop at all.
Or in other words, the effective learning rate is approximately equal to the initial learning rate
divided by one.
Now, you might ask, well, which one is the correct way?
And we can't really say because it was never specified.
What I can tell you is that there are some very major packages that have implemented them both ways.
So it's not as if one is more correct than the other.
For example, in TensorFlow, the cache is initialized to one.
In Keras, the cache is initialized to zero.
In fact, it's a great exercise to try and implement these adaptive learning rate techniques.
Then compare your implementation to these official implementations to check if your implementation is
correct.
If it is correct, then your loss per iteration should match what the library gives you.
And so I've done this in the file Rmsprop Test.py in case you want to check it out.

--- Adam (pt 1) ---

In this lecture, we'll be looking at another way to modify gradient descent using a technique called
adaptive moment estimation or Adam for short.
Adam is a technique that deserves its own lecture because it is the go to method for optimizing neural
networks today.
That is, people most often use Adam as the default choice without really considering other options.
The reason for this is simple.
Adam tends to work well and it tends to work well with the default settings for the learning rate momentum
parameter and so on.
In other words, Adam is robust.
You can use it and not have to worry about tweaking the knobs to find values, which are exactly right,
since what you have by default is probably okay.
In addition, Adam is somewhat of a successor to Rmsprop.
Both were developed at the University of Toronto.
Rmsprop was developed by Geoffrey Hinton while Adam was developed by Jimmy Barr, who was a PhD student
of Hinton's at the time.
In fact, Jimmy Barr had three PhD supervisors, all of whom are prominent machine learning researchers,
and he is now a professor himself.
Now, one way to think of Atom is that it's rmsprop with momentum.
This is because it takes the same idea of having an adaptive learning rate, but it also adds momentum.
This might be a little confusing since libraries like TensorFlow also provide you with an option to
add momentum to RMS prop.
However, these are slightly different.
So the key point is Adam is like rmsprop with momentum, but it's not exactly the same.
Let's start by reviewing what we have seen so far.
Specifically, let's consider two helpful ways that we can improve vanilla gradient descent.
Method number one is to use momentum.
An object in motion stays in motion.
Method number two is to use adaptive learning rate methods like RMS prop.
Note that I'm using slightly different symbols than what you are probably used to.
This is just to set us up to learn Adam, which uses different symbols by convention.
The parameter update for momentum proceeds in two steps.
First, we update the momentum value, which I'm going to call M in this lecture.
It's equal to some proportion of the previous momentum minus the learning rate times the current gradient.
Then we update the parameters themselves, which we call theta.
The update is just the old value of theta plus the new momentum.
For this lecture, it's helpful to present momentum in a slightly different way.
Specifically, we now treat the momentum value as kind of the negative of what it was before.
The update for M of T is now the weighted sum of the previous M of T minus one and the current gradient
g of T to update theta.
We now subtract m of t times the learning rate eta.
Now, since all these new symbols are just constants, you can show that the two methods of presenting
momentum are equivalent.
Let's also review Rmsprop.
Rmsprop also proceeds in two steps.
First, we update our cache, which is now renamed V.
Notice how similar this is to our modified version of momentum.
But instead of being the weighted sum of the gradients, it's the weighted sum of the gradient squared.
The second step is to update the parameter theta as usual, except instead of having the same learning
rate for all time, we divide by the square root of the cache.
Okay.
So for the next phase of this lecture, we're going to take a small digression and talk about moving
averages.
By doing this, we'll begin to make sense of why momentum and RMS prop take the forms that they do.
To understand the moving average, a nice place to begin is just the regular average.
Suppose I have a set of data points X1X2 all the way up to x t.
The average of these data points is exactly what you expect.
Sum up all the x's and divide by the total number of data points, which is t.
Okay.
So pretty simple so far.
The next step is to consider what if I have so much data that I cannot fit it all into memory at once?
For example, the data lives in a file that's one terabyte in size.
Unfortunately, your computer is not capable of storing one terabyte of memory.
Or consider another scenario where you have a robot interacting with the world.
In this case, each data point arrives at the robot sensors at each instant in time.
I can't see any of the future data points, so my average can only consist of the past data points and
the current data point.
If I want the average of all the data points I have seen so far, let's assume that the current time
is T, then sure, I can add them all up and divide by T, but that seems wasteful.
You can see that as t increases the number of things I have to add together also increases.
Therefore, on each step my robot will have to do more and more computation in order to compute this
average.
We say that this computation is o of T because the number of steps to perform the computation is proportional
to T.
My claim is that if we have a robot streaming in data one point at a time, we can actually make this
computation o of one.
In other words, our computation is constant.
No matter how much data we've collected specifically, that we can compute the current average by somehow
making use of the previous computations we've done, and even more specifically, the previous average.
To understand how we can do this.
Let's manipulate the equation for the sample mean.
Firstly, notice that our notation for the mean at time t is x bar subscript t.
Let's begin by splitting our sum.
By taking out only the final data point X of T.
The next step is to multiply out the one over T term.
The next step is to replace the sum from little T equals one up to big T minus one.
Specifically we can replace this with the sample mean at time t minus one.
As you recall, the sample mean at time at t minus one is just the sum divided by t minus one.
Okay, so now what do we have?
We have exactly what we discussed previously.
We have an equation that allows us to compute the current average x bar T in constant time.
As you can see, it only involves summing two terms.
The previous average x bar t minus one and the current sample X of T.
The next step is to manipulate our expression.
Even more specifically, let's take the first constant T minus one over T and divide everything by T.
Then we get one minus one over T.
This is helpful because now the one over t term appears twice.
The next step is to ask the question What if we replace one over T with a constant.
Let's call it alpha.
In this case, we are changing the answer, but we are doing so in a way that might be helpful later
on when this was equal to one over t we saw that this gave us the regular sample mean.
As you can see, the function one over t decreases as t increases.
However, it turns out that this will weight all of the x's justrillionight so that every x we have
considered so far is weighted by one over T.
Note that we have just proven this and as you know it leads exactly to the sample mean.
Importantly, this means that if we replace one over t with a constant we will no longer have the sample
mean.
In fact, when we have a constant, this gives us the exponentially weighted moving average.
As an exercise, you might want to try manipulating the equation for constant alpha and put it back
into a summation so that it depends only on the X's and not any previous averages.
What you should see is that the weights decay exponentially as you go further and further back in time.
This is opposed to the regular average where all the weights are equal.
Basically, this means that for the exponentially weighted moving average data points which are more
recent are more highly weighted.
Now, just by convention, we can replace Alpha with a new symbol.
Specifically, let's introduce beta, which is equal to one minus alpha.
We'll call this the decay.
Note that this doesn't change anything.
Only the symbols are different, but the functionality of the equation is still exactly the same.
At this point, you should recognize that this is exactly the same as the cash update for Rmsprop.
It is also the same as our modified momentum update.
So in our mess prop, where do we use this exponentially weighted moving average?
In fact, we use it on the squared gradients.
So it's worth considering what is this the average of?
What are we estimating now?
Trivially?
We are estimating the mean of the gradient squared.
So in Rmsprop, because we eventually end up using the square root of the cache, now the name Rmsprop
makes sense.
Rmse stands for Root Mean Square.
Specifically, we are estimating the square root of the mean of the square of the gradient.
However, we can go further than this.
What is the sample mean?
You may recall from statistics that the sample mean is used to estimate expected values.
Now, what is the expected value of the square of a random variable?
Well, it is related to the variance.
Specifically, the variance is equal to the expected value of the random variable squared minus the
true mean squared.
However, when we just have the expected value of the square, we often call this the second moment.
In general, the expected value of X to the power n is called The Nth Moment.
In statistics, it's often the case that what we care about are the first and second moments.
There are some practical and theoretical reasons behind this, but they are outside the scope of this
lecture.
Intuitively, we know that what we often care about when it comes to something random is the mean and
the variance.
We know that the second moment is something like variance.
Similarly, the first moment is simply the mean that is the expected value of X to the one or simply
the expected value of x is the mean.
At this point, we can recognize how this relates to momentum and our RMS prop momentum essentially
estimates the first moment of the gradient using an exponential moving average.
Similarly, RMS prop essentially estimates the second moment of the gradient using an exponential moving
average.
Now, since we might want to tune these two values separately, we will give each of them a possibly
different value of beta.
We'll call the beta for the first moment, beta one, and we'll call the beta for the second moment
beta two.
The last step is to essentially put these two ideas together and that gives us Adam.
In order to make sense of how to put these two ideas together, let's remind ourselves what momentum
and RMS prop look like when they are apart.
For momentum, we subtract the learning rate and multiply by M.
For our RMS prop, we subtract the learning rate, multiply by the gradient and divide by the square
root of V plus some tiny value.
Thus for Adam, we simply combine these two together.
That is to say, we subtract the learning rate, multiply it by m and divide by the square root of V
plus the usual tiny value to avoid dividing by zero.
Now this is the essence of the Adam Optimizer.
But note that there is still one more step before we are done.
Since this lecture has been quite long so far and this is a different topic, we will save it for the
next lecture.

--- Adam (pt 2) ---

In this lecture, we'll learn about one more step to finalize our Atom Optimizer equations.
To understand why we need to do this, we have to understand what the exponentially weighted moving
average can be used for.
One way to look at it is that it's just another way of computing the average.
Specifically, it's useful when our data is non-stationary.
That is, the true expected value might be changing over time.
Therefore, more recent values are more useful than older values.
Another way to look at the exponential moving average is this Suppose that you have some time series
signal, such as a stock price.
Clearly this is a non-stationary signal, so it's a good candidate for using this kind of average.
Well, what happens if we apply our moving average to this time series signal?
Let's call the input time series X of T and let's call the output time series Y of T.
Well, what you get is you get a smoothed version of the input signal.
That is, it generally follows the same trend, but in a much smoother manner without lots of random
jumps up and down.
This is useful in finance, for example, when you want to know the trend of a stock price, but you
don't care about minor fluctuations.
If you have experience in signal processing, you might know this by another name.
This is called the low pass filter.
The reasoning behind that is this Slow fluctuations have low frequencies, whereas fast fluctuations
have high frequencies.
Generally speaking, the low frequency movements are what we actually care about because they're usually
larger in magnitude.
The high frequency movements are usually very tiny and tend not to be that significant.
So by using a low pass filter, we are essentially passing our input signal into an algorithm that removes
the high frequencies.
We do not care about.
There is one small problem with our low pass filter.
Note that our output y of t always depends on the previous value y of t minus one.
But if we are trying to generate the first output y of one, then what is y of zero?
A convention is to simply set this value to zero.
But what happens when we do this?
The answer is that at the beginning of our output, all the values are biased towards zero.
Now, if you've never implemented this before or you've never taken one of my courses where you got
an opportunity to implement this, it would be very useful to try and implement it yourself to see what
I'm talking about firsthand.
It should be a pretty simple exercise and you can try this on any time series you choose, such as the
price of your favorite stock.
Now, what does it mean that the beginning values are biased towards zero?
Well, this is clearly not good because it takes our filter some time to catch up and to start producing
useful outputs.
As a side note, in other fields, there are simple solutions to this, such as simply setting y of
one to be equal to x of one instead of zero.
However, in deep learning we employ a method known as bias correction.
So what is bias Correction?
Bias correction means that instead of using what we would usually use Y of T, we adjust this value
by some factor to get y hat of t.
Specifically, we divide by one minus beta to the power of t.
One way to see that this works is by noting that since beta is a number less than one beta to the power
t will approach zero as t gets large.
Therefore, when t is large, we essentially divide by a number close to one and y hat of t is very
close to y of T, which makes sense.
This is because bias correction is only really needed when T is small.
So what happens when t is small?
The simplest way to see this is to plug some numbers in.
Let's suppose that beta is equal to 0.99.
Then y of one is equal to beta times y of zero plus one minus beta times x of one.
Since y of zero is zero, this is just 0.01 times x of one.
Clearly this is not good since we are only taking 1% of the true input x of one.
However, what do we get for y hat of one?
Well, we take y of one and divide it by one minus beta to the power one.
Of course, that's just one minus beta.
But one minus beta is just 0.01 as we have just seen.
Therefore, y hat of one is just 0.01 times x of one divided by 0.01, which is just x of one itself.
Thus y hat of one is no longer biased.
It's exactly equal to x of one.
How about when t is equal to two?
In this case, y of two is equal to beta times y of one plus one minus beta times x of two, which is
equal to 0.99 times y of one plus 0.01 times x of two.
However, we know that y of one is equal to 0.01 times x of one.
So we can plug that in.
We then get y of two is equal to 0.0099 times x of one plus 0.01 times x of two.
So now we basically have about 1% of x of one and about 1% of x of two.
Still not a good estimate for Y of two.
But what happens when we do bias correction?
We take y of two and divide that by one minus beta squared.
Now the numbers get a little messy, so you'll have to do the calculations on your own at home.
But what you should end up with is this you should get y hat two is equal to 0.497 times x of one plus
0.50, three times X of two.
So does this make sense?
This makes perfect sense.
We take about half of X of one and about half of x of two.
We take a little more from x of two.
Since X of two is more recent than X of one.
Furthermore, notice how these adjusted weights always add up to one, which makes a lot of sense.
Okay, so how can we incorporate this into our atom optimization?
Well, we know that we are doing two exponential moving averages, one for M and one for V.
Therefore, we simply replace what we had before with these bias corrected versions of M and V.
So now, instead of subtracting the learning rate, multiplying by M and dividing by the square root
of V, we simply replace M and V with their bias corrected versions M hat and V hat.
In total, our final algorithm is this Before we start our training loop, we initialize M 0 to 0 V
0 to 0 and the time index T to zero.
Then we enter our training loop.
Inside the loop, we increment the time index by one.
Next we find the M of T using the usual recursive equation with the hyper parameter decay rate.
Beta one.
Next we do the same thing for v of T using beta two.
Next we perform bias correction to get m hat of t and v hat of t.
Finally, we perform our gradient update using the usual equation.
Note again that this is for some generic parameter vector theta.
In practice this represents a collection of all the parameters of your model.
So you will need M's and V's of the same size as all of your parameter vectors, matrices and tensors.
The final thing I want to discuss in this lecture is what values do we choose for these hyper parameters?
Previously, I told you that atom optimization is very robust.
That is the same values work for a wide range of problems.
With practice and experience.
You may find that this is the case with TensorFlow and PyTorch.
Note that these default values are built into the optimizers so you rarely have to think about them.
However, if you do ever end up implementing atom from scratch, here are the suggested default values
for the learning rate.
A typical default value is ten to the minus three.
For beta one, we use 0.9.
As you recall, this is a typical value when we are using momentum.
For beta two, we use 0.999.
As you recall, this is a typical value when we are using rmsprop.
For Epsilon, a typical default value is ten to the minus eight.
As a final thought to conclude this lecture, I want to mention that while Adam is a common default
choice, you will still encounter scenarios where it is not the best choice.
In fact, some time after many people had adopted Adam as their go to default choice of Optimizer,
a paper was written that showed a regular stochastic gradient descent with momentum performed better
than Adam.
This paper is called The Marginal Value of Adaptive Gradient Methods in Machine Learning.
Personally, before the invention of Adam and even after the invention of Adam SGD with momentum was
often my own default choice.
This reminds me of a rule that I often repeat to my students.
This rule is that machine learning is experimentation, not philosophy.
Oftentimes, students just want to be told one way of doing things so that they have less choices to
make by themselves, which I suppose is more comfortable.
But the reality is, unless you try it yourself, you will never know what the real answer is.
To put this in a different way, ask yourself this question What should you do when you want to know
the output of a computer program?
Specifically a computer program where you train your model on some data set using a variety of optimizers?
Well, it would be silly and inefficient to ask me or anyone else what the output of the program will
be.
Similarly, it would be incorrect to try and guess what the output of the program will be.
Instead, if you want to know the output of a computer program, let me tell you an ancient secret that
only true gurus know.
The answer is to simply run the computer program and look at the output yourself.
As always, I am glad to be the source of many surprising and unusual facts.
Thanks for listening and I'll see you in the next lecture.
