--- Beginner's Coding Tips ---

So in this lecture we are going to discuss how you, as a beginner should approach the coding lectures
in this course.
The reason I've created this lecture is that I've noticed some beginner students are a bit confused
about the approach we take to the code lectures.
Specifically, they might be wondering why I'm not coding live on the screen or why they can't code
along.
What I hope you'll see by the end of this lecture is that these questions are just misinformed.
Any confusion you may have is based on incorrect assumptions.
Specifically, this lecture is going to answer a few questions.
Namely, number one, should you code along?
The answer is no.
Number two, can you code along if you really want to?
The answer is yes.
Number three, How can you practice what you've learned in this course?
And what are the exercises in this course?
So if you had any or perhaps all of these questions, this lecture is for you.
Okay.
So the first question we're going to tackle is should you code along?
As stated, my recommendation is no.
This may surprise some of you, especially if you are a beginner or if you've taken other beginner courses
online.
In those courses.
Coding along is typically encouraged from my experience.
In fact, I'm not immune to this myself.
If you take some of my oldest courses, which came out approximately five years before this course,
I also encourage students to code along because I assume that this was the normal thing to do.
Of course, after five years of teaching, I've realized why this is not a good idea at all.
In fact, this live coding approach is not good for either of us, neither you nor myself.
So let me start with why coding along is bad for you.
Firstly, it gives you an extremely false impression of how this code is written.
This is not how code is written in the real world, especially complex code like the code in this course.
Doing things by going along with the instructor makes sense in other areas, like playing guitar or
doing yoga, but it makes no sense in coding.
Unlike guitar and yoga, coding is a mental thing.
It's about what goes on inside your mind.
Guitar and yoga are physical things.
They are about what you can do with your body.
A coding lecture where the instructor types everything from start to end might take maybe 10 or 20 minutes,
but this is only because the code was prepared beforehand.
In reality, this code required thinking in order to design the structure research in order to learn
the syntax for any APIs that were used and debugging to fix mistakes.
This does not teach you how to write the code from scratch because this code was not written from scratch,
as you are seeing it typed live.
And by the way, if anyone tells you they write code without making mistakes, they are not only lying
but also too inexperienced to even know that what they are saying is impossible.
In effect, all you are really doing is wasting time watching the instructor type in the screen.
What you should be paying more attention to is the thinking process behind the coding.
That is to say, coding is more about thinking, not about typing.
Coding complex problems does not happen linearly from start to end.
One example of that is test driven development, also known as TDD, which is a common process used
in modern software engineering companies such as Google and Facebook.
This process is used with the expectation that you will make mistakes and thus it is much more realistic.
So let's reiterate this coding live from start to end.
Not realistic test driven development.
This is a real coding process that is used by professional companies around the world.
So instead of mindlessly watching someone type on the screen and believing that anyone actually codes
anything, this way, let's review what you should be doing instead.
Firstly, you should be thinking about the overall structure of the code as I describe it to you and
as I described the reasons why it is structured the way it is.
Secondly, you should be paying attention to the syntax.
And of course, without knowing the syntax, you can't write any of this code yourself, which is presumably
what you want to do in the future.
So now let's answer the other side of this question, which is why coding live is bad for me.
We've just established why it's not going to help you.
Well, it's pretty simple.
If I'm trying to type and talk at the same time, then I'm trying to do two things at once.
But if I'm only talking, then I can focus on that one thing and therefore I can go into much greater
detail and be much more precise in what I say.
This ends up being good for you because it means you're getting better information transfer.
And what I'm saying will be much smoother than if I tried to come up with it while typing at the same
time.
So not only is typing pointless as we previously established, it's also better to not type because
it helps me communicate better with you.
Okay.
So now let's move on to question number two, which is can you code along If you really want to disregard
my previous advice, the answer to this question is yes.
Now, before some student comes along and asks, why do you even have to say this?
Isn't this obvious?
Let me just shut that down right now and tell you, for some students it's not obvious.
Given that I've been asked this question a few times.
You see, I think these students may have been confused, perhaps assuming that because I wasn't typing
that, they also could not type.
Of course, now that I'm saying this out loud, you should realize this is complete nonsense.
For example, I can show you this piece of code.
You can type this out right now if you like.
I don't have to type it for you to type it.
There is no requirement that I'm actually typing on the keyboard for you to start typing.
Again, this is not like yoga for yoga.
Yes.
You want to see your teacher doing the moves.
Now, again, I know this is obvious, but some students really have asked me this question, so please
bear with me.
Okay.
So now let's move on to question number three, which I've also discussed elsewhere.
But I'll answer again here for good measure.
And this is how can you practice what you've learned in this course, given that I've now deprived you
of this interactive exercise of coding along?
Well, let's remind ourselves that coding along is not actually a good exercise.
Remember, you are not learning anything about the coding process when you do that, because that's
not even how the coding process was done.
If the coding lecture was ten minutes, the coding process probably took maybe 60 minutes, maybe even
days.
So that should be the time commitment you're looking at when you do an exercise, at least at this level
of machine learning.
Now, to really answer this question, we have to go a bit meta, and that's to discuss the two kinds
of courses that can exist in machine learning.
The first kind, of course, is the theory course where I teach you, say the naive Bayes algorithm,
and then you go and implement that algorithm in code.
In that kind, of course, implementing the algorithm is the exercise, and that's a really powerful
skill to have.
That means you can take an idea and make that idea practical by turning it into a working computer program.
And of course, anyone who invents new machine learning models must have this skill.
The by definition, if you invent a new machine learning algorithm, then there is no existing code
for it.
You will be the one who makes the first working example.
And if you can't code, then it will never be made.
So in this case, the concept of exercise is very straightforward.
You're given an idea, an algorithm, and your exercise is to put that into working code.
And obviously you should do this before looking at my code, which is the solution to the exercise.
That is, don't look at the solution to the exercise before doing the exercise yourself.
Now, that doesn't really apply to this course because this is not a theory course.
This course is an API course.
I'm teaching you how to use an existing library where your goal is mostly to learn about the syntax
rather than the concepts.
Although we do still touch on concepts in this class, we're not going to do something like derive back
propagation because that's irrelevant to the library we're learning about.
So in this type of class, it would be impossible for you to write out any code before I show it to
you, because obviously you simply won't know the syntax In this type of class.
There are two kinds of exercises and projects.
The first kind is the kind I'm going to explicitly give you.
That is, you'll hear me say in the lecture, Please do this exercise.
And after hearing that, it will be your choice whether or not you want to go ahead and do that exercise.
As a side note, please note that for this class, the explicit exercise prompts and datasets are included
only in the VIP version.
And if you don't know what that means, please contact me using the contact form on my website which
is at lazy programmers.
Me.
Now, the second kind of exercise is implicit.
That is, although I'm not going to explicitly tell you to do something, it should be obvious that
you would do it anyway.
My assumption is that we're all responsible, mature adults.
You know why you're in this course and you know why you want to learn about machine learning.
And that's probably because you want to apply it to some data set you have.
For example, if you work in manufacturing, perhaps you're interested in using computer vision to identify
defective parts.
Well, after taking this course, you should be able to apply the code.
I've shown you to your own data sets.
Now, here's the really neat thing about machine learning.
This process does not require any new code.
Therefore, you're going to simply be applying the code you've already learned.
This will give you practice in writing that code yourself.
But again, this code is more or less the same as what you've learned in this course.
I encapsulate this with my rule.
All data is the same.
What this means is that the same code will work no matter the data set.
For example, take a neural network.
We know that neural networks can work for something like fraud detection.
Well, now, suppose that I want to use a neural network for a new task like disease prediction.
Surprisingly, this does not require any new code at all.
All that's left to do is to simply plug in your data set.
And this is why we can have libraries like Scikit Learn, which is used by researchers in many fields
like climate prediction, biology and finance.
The data in biology is completely different from the data in finance, and yet the machine learning
code to process this data is exactly the same.
And this is what we mean when we say all data is the same in the eyes of your machine learning model.
And that is how you will practice what you've learned.
Although you can't come up with the syntax for an API out of thin air, what you can do is internalize
it by rewriting it again and again on new data sets, which are not just the generic data sets from
this course, but data sets you really care about in your life.
Furthermore, you'll need to test different hyperparameters on your own, which will force you to think
about things like choosing the best learning rate, finding the right number of layers and so forth.
Now, of course, not all of you already have jobs where you have access to datasets that you want to
use.
In this case, you can still apply this approach by choosing any dataset you want to use.
The beauty of this is you get to choose what you care about.
I can't tell you what that is, but I'll trust that you can figure this out on your own.
And so let me just make this explicit.
You shouldn't depend on me to tell you to do this.
This is just the obvious thing to do.
If you know why you're here and you're a productive and proactive student who is serious about learning
this subject, then this is simply the obvious course of action.
Now, I want to end this lecture with a question for you.
The students of this course, over the years, I've seen students get triggered by this lecture, meaning
that they got really angry or offended by the things I've said.
Now, personally, I don't understand why that's the case.
So if that was the case for you or if you disagreed with anything I said in this lecture, please let
me know about it on the Q&amp;A.
I'm always available to listen to your concerns, so the sooner you can let me know, the sooner they
will be dealt with.

--- Where To Get the Code Troubleshooting ---

In this lecture, I'm going to discuss common mistakes in obtaining the code for this course.
The first and most common mistake I want to mention is that students often mistakenly search for the
notebooks on GitHub.
I'll reiterate now that the Python notebooks are not on GitHub.
The second mistake I want to mention is that students don't follow the steps outlined on the code link
page.
In particular, if you get a warning message saying you must be logged in to do that, what you'll want
to do is create an account, log in, and then try that same previous code link again.
That's what is meant by you must be logged in to do that.
This means in order for the code link to work, you must be logged in.
The mistake some students make is that after logging in, they do not attempt to use the code link again,
but rather try to browse around the website looking for the code themselves.
Sometimes they come across the same courses they're taking on this platform, not realizing it's a different
course on a different platform.
If you do this, you might end up a little frustrated thinking that you're being asked to pay for the
same course twice.
This is obviously not the case.
It's simply that you didn't use the provided code link while also being logged in simultaneously.
The next common mistake I want to mention is that people using VPNs end up ignoring the warning message
on the signup page regarding VPNs.
If you're using a VPN, you won't get a verification email, but the sign up form is still available,
so you might try to sign up and wonder why your verification email never arrived.
As such, if you're wondering why your verification email hasn't arrived, please double check and make
sure that you're not seeing this warning.
If you did end up signing up using a VPN, you can still get your verification email simply by clicking
the link to do so.
But you must do this only after turning off your VPN.
Please note that the VPN detection APIs out there are not perfect, so if you're getting this message
and you are certain you are not using a VPN or any other kind of anonymizer, then please contact me
and I will personally assist you.
Additionally, please note that this does not apply to VPNs only.
Any kind of Anonymizer is treated similarly.
This includes proxies, servers, web hosts, and iCloud Private Relay.
Now you might be wondering, why does this platform block VPNs and other anonymizers?
You might object to this on principle if you're one of those people.
I understand this can be a contentious issue for you.
In fact, I use a VPN myself most of the time, and that's how I know so much about this area.
The simple answer is fraud, spammers, hackers, and other unsavory individuals.
Many large platforms nowadays block VPNs, including Facebook, Reddit, Twitter, Instagram, imager,
and more.
Even the website you're using right now sometimes blocks certain VPN addresses.
Rest assured, since I'm using a VPN myself, I'm getting blocked as often as you are as I surf the
web.
If these large companies filled with some of the smartest people in the world haven't figured out a
workaround, then it's unlikely to change any time soon and will remain the standard across the internet.
The next minor mistake I want to address is in regards to the GitHub repo.
One mistake some students make is that after clicking the link, they browse other folders in the repo,
thinking they might be relevant to this course.
But just remember that unless otherwise stated, the folder being linked to is the only relevant folder
for this course.
The GitHub link takes you directly to that folder, so there's no need to look at the other folders
unless you are also taking those courses.
Now, if you're new to git or GitHub, please see the FAQ lecture called how to use GitHub and Extra
Coding tips, or contact me via the Q&amp;A if it's still not clear.

There are two key points I want to make
187
before I give you the algorithm.
188
First, I'm not going to give you much intuition
189
about how it works.
190
Second, I'm not going to derive the theory
191
behind why it works.
192
The reason I want to mention these two key points
193
is that you should realise you don't need
194
these two pieces of information, in order to translate
195
pseudocode into code.
196
A lot of time, these three concepts,
197
intuition, theory, and implementation,
198
work to reinforce each other.
199
The reason I'm focusing on implementation right now
200
is because it's the one people most often miss,
201
or think they don't need at all,
202
when really it's an extremely important part
203
of the learning process.
204
Okay, so the pseudocode is as follows.
205
In my predict function, as you already know,
206
I'm going to take in some input data, X.
207
My predictions will be Y hat equals X times W.
208
You might already recognise this as linear regression.
209
However, forget what you know about linear regression,
210
and pretend that this is just some formula I gave you.
211
What I can tell you is that we are creating
212
some kind of regression model.
213
It should be clear from this equation
214
that the parameters of the model are contained
215
in this vector of weights, W.
216
I would probably also tell you that W is a vector
217
that's the same size as the number of feature vectors in X.
218
Of course, this must be true in order
219
for the matrix multiplied to be valid.
220
This acts as kind of a sanity check,
221
so you can make sure everything is making sense.
222
In my fit function, I want to perform this loop
223
some number of times.
224
Since we know from the previous slide
225
that the parameters of the model are contained in W,
226
then it should be clear that inside the fit function,
227
what we want to do is update W.
228
Not surprisingly, that's what's happening here,
229
another good sanity check.
230
Now because you're already familiar with linear regression,
231
you probably already recognise this as gradient descent.
232
Again, I want you to pretend that you don't know that.
233
But what do you know?
234
You know that iterate of algorithms
235
are common in machine learning,
236
and that gradient descent is among the most common.
237
You could probably infer that this is some form
238
of gradient descent, but you don't need to know
239
what it is in order to write it in code.
240
What else do we know?
241
You know what a for loop is, and you know
242
how to write one in Python.
243
You know that X is an N by D matrix
244
containing the input data, and that Y is an N-size vector
245
containing the targets.
246
You know that Y hat is an N-size vector
247
containing the predictions.
248
You know that W is a D-size vector
249
containing the model parameters.
250
And you know how to add, subtract,
251
and multiply these objects.
252
So we can write this algorithm without even knowing
253
how it works.
254
Of course, a lot about how it works
255
can just be inferred from this formula.
256
Here is what you don't know.
257
You don't know T, the number of times
258
the loop is supposed to run.
259
You don't know eta, the learning rate.
260
This is completely fine.
261
You can't let this lack of knowledge
262
stop you in your tracks.
263
The truth of the matter is,
264
there are going to be situations where you're not told
265
the exact numbers to plug in.
266
In many cases, the answer is going to be,
267
it depends on the problem.
268
The important thing is, you have to get used to the idea
269
of trial and error, and you have to make educated guesses,
270
based on what you already know.
271
So what can you do?
272
Well, you know that the learning rate
273
is supposed to be a small number.
274
How small?
275
It depends on the problem.
276
Typically, it's a number less than one, like 0.1.
277
If that doesn't work, you might try lowering it.
278
Typically, we lower it by factors of 10.
279
So for example, the next learning rate we would try
280
is 10 to the minus two, 10 to the minus three and so on.
281
Typically, in machine learning, whether you're looking
282
at a supervised or unsupervised algorithm,
283
there is an associated cost or objective function
284
that you're trying to minimise.
285
Let's suppose I tell you it's squared error,
286
which is typically what we would use for regression.
287
Well, now you have a way to choose the number of iterations
288
of the loop, and the learning rate.
289
How do we do this?
290
So inside your fit function, you would plot the cost
291
as a function of iteration.
292
I always recommend doing this,
293
no matter what algorithm you're looking at.
294
In general, you always want the cost to converge.
295
The pattern you typically see
296
is that there is a steep drop at the beginning,
297
and it slowly flattens out
298
as the number of iterations increases.
299
To choose the number of iterations, you want to stop
300
when the curve is sufficiently flat.
301
You might recognise this as a diminishing returns scenario.
302
The returns diminish because at the beginning,
303
we reduce the cost by a lot in just a few iterations.
304
At the end, we can do a lot of iterations,
305
but we reduce the cost by almost nothing.
306
An alternative is, you could use a separate validation set,
307
and stop when the validation cost increases,
308
but that is not the focus of this lecture.
309
Unlike the training cost, the validation cost
310
is not guaranteed to decrease at every round,
311
because that is not what we are minimising with respect to.
312
You can also use this same plot
313
to help improve your learning rate.
314
If the cost explodes or becomes not a number,
315
your learning rate is probably too high.
316
If your cost converges too slowly,
317
you can try increasing your learning rate.
318
So what does your final code look like?
319
Well, we know we want to create a class
320
with at least the predict and fit functions.
321
You don't have to use a class, but I find that
322

323
and provides useful structure.
324

325

326

327
you know how to add, you know how to subtract.
328
These are basic arithmetic operations
329
that I'm sure you know already.
330
If you don't know how to do these in Numpy,
331
then I have a completely free Numpy course
332
on the Numpy Stack that you can take.
333
Here is the second part of the code,
334
where we actually create an instance of the class,
335
and then use it.
336
I've also included the squared error cost function,
337
just for completion's sake.
338
What's cool about this template is that it doesn't
339
really change from one algorithm to the next.
340
The structure is basically always going to be this way,
341
at least for supervised learning and unsupervised learning.
342
So as we discussed before, it doesn't matter
343
what algorithm you're implementing,
344
whether it be linear regression, logistic regression,
345

346

347
And learning what goes inside these functions

4

--- How to Uncompress a .tar.gz file ---

Hey, everyone.
And welcome back.
In this lecture, I'm going to show you how to uncompress a file that comes in tar.gz format for Mac
and Linux users.
Linux especially.
This is very simple because this is a very common format.
For Windows, it might seem a little strange because it's not typically a format that you see in Windows,
but nonetheless, it's still very easy to follow this simple recipe I'm going to show you.
Firstly, let's talk about what this type of file is.
It looks a little strange because there are two extensions dot tar and dot JS.
You can think of them as steps.
So originally I had this raw file and then I added the dot tar and then I added the dot js.
So to uncompress this file we have to do the reverse and in the opposite order.
Now you might wonder, well what are these two steps?
Firstly, the JS stands for GZip, which is the compression algorithm that's used.
The tar means that it's possible to join separate files and folders together into the same file.
Hence the term tar as in physical tar, which is a sticky substance.
Okay, so if we download a file, how do we get back to the original contents?
I'm going to cover the Mac and Linux use case first since that's the simplest.
So all you need to do is go into the command line CD into the folder where the file is and type in tar
minus x vsf and then the file name.
So even though there are two steps, the Tar program is going to take care of both steps for you.
If you want to know what these arguments mean, you can type in man tar into the command line and read
the documentation.
So Windows is where students often have problems.
The most common scenario is that students only do one of the steps, which is the unzipping, but they
don't do the untiring.
So one solution is if you have Cygwin installed, you can just use the same command as on Linux.
Since Cygwin was designed to give you all the common Linux commands on your Windows machine.
Another solution is to use the program seven zip, which can come in handy for several other file formats
as well.
You can download it from a seven zip org.
Once you've installed it, you'll be able to right click on the file and select extract.
There are several options for extract like extract files or extract here.
You can choose the one you want, but notice how the output of this file still ends in tar.
That means you need to do the second step, which is to untar the tar file.
So to do that you have to right click on the tar file and again select extract.
Once you've done these two steps, you should have the original files back.
So just to reiterate, there are two steps.
And for both steps, you just right click on the file and go to the seven zip menu and select extract.

--- Proof that using Jupyter Notebook is the same as not using it ---

In this lecture, I'm going to talk about Jupyter Notebook.
Occasionally I get this strange question from students asking why I don't do things in Jupyter Notebook.
Let me explain why I think this is strange.
Firstly, it's my position that it's completely unnecessary and actually doesn't change anything.
To use Jupyter Notebook or not, let me repeat that.
Using Jupyter Notebook is exactly the same as not using Jupyter Notebook.
There's no difference other than the fact that it looks different.
In other words, the screen is a different color.
Obviously such a difference is trivial.
In this lecture, I'm going to demonstrate how that's the case.
One major reason I dislike Jupyter Notebook is because it causes too many students to be unaware of
how Python really works.
If you're only comfortable inside notebook and when you see Python code in a text file or anywhere else
and you get scared or intimidated, that's not good.
That python code that's in the text file is exactly the same as what would be in the notebook.
There's really nothing scary about it.
Programmers working at real jobs eventually need to write code that's going to be deployed and run automatically.
In other words, your final code is going to sit in a python file that runs by itself without Jupyter
Notebook.
So if you're going to have any hope of using these skills in a real job, you had better be comfortable
with writing Python code outside of Jupyter Notebook.
You would also better be aware that there's actually zero difference between writing code in a Jupyter
notebook and writing code in IPython or the console.
Here's one example I like of how you might use Python in the quote unquote real world.
Let's say you write a script that emails your boss to tell him you're going to be late for work.
And let's say you don't actually want to send this email manually, but you want it to get sent automatically
every Friday morning so that your boss doesn't yell at you for coming into work late after you had partied
too hard Thursday night.
Well, that's very simple.
All I have to do is on my server, create what's called a cron tab.
In it, I just enter the code for when I want this command to run.
And then to the right of that, I specify the command that I want to run.
That's just python space and then the script name.
As you can see, it's simply how you would run this Python script from the command line.
And now every Friday at 9:45 a.m., this script is going to send the same email to your boss to tell
him you'll be late.
Well, let's not get off track here.
The point of this is you really don't want to be using Jupyter Notebook for something like this.
I think one perceived advantage of Jupyter Notebook is that you can see the results of intermediate
calculations.
However, this is merely a perceived advantage and not a real advantage because you can do the exact
same thing even when you're not inside.
Notebook.
Firstly, as I'm sure you've seen by now, IPython also prints out the results after you enter a command.
IPython is called a repl which stands for read eval print loop.
And that's generally what they all do, no matter what language you're in.
So whether that's Python, Ruby, PHP or any other language, the key word here is print.
Why is that?
Well, one of my golden rules for writing and debugging code is when in doubt, print it out.
I can't tell you how many times I've gotten a question on the Q and A when.
It could have been easily answered by inserting a print statement into the existing code.
Anyway, what's the point of this long discussion about printing things out?
Well, it's that if you think Jupyter Notebook is the only program that helps you do this, you need
to expand your horizons a little bit.
You should in fact, always be doing this.
If you're not using an abundant amount of print statements while coding, you are not doing it right.
Remember, programming is not philosophy.
You're not supposed to be running a program in your head.
That's like trying to do long division in your head when you have a calculator in your hand.
So just by printing things out, you can be more efficient.
Stop trying to guess what a program will do and just let the program itself tell you what it's doing.
The key takeaway here is you should always be printing things out.
The fact that Jupyter Notebook shows you the result of each block of code is not simply a happy surprise.
But another important lesson here is that if you want to use Jupyter Notebook, there's absolutely nothing
stopping you from doing so.
In other words, using Jupyter Notebook is 100% compatible with everything we are already doing.
In fact, if you recall, your goal in these courses is not to run my code, but to write your own code.
And of course, since it's your code, you can write it however you want, including Jupyter Notebook.
In the rest of this lecture, I'm going to prove to you that you can take any script from our course
repository, which we know runs in the console because that's how I always demonstrate it and show you
that this exact same code runs in Jupyter Notebook.
Let's begin.
Okay, so let's say I'm in the folder numpy class and I'm interested in the code inside classification

As you can see, what I have right now is this code inside a text editor.
Now, if you're not aware of what a text editor is, it's just a program that shows you the contents
of a text file and lets you edit those contents.
It's the ideal program for writing code.
Now, occasionally, if you're writing in a language like Java or Swift, you might want to use an IDE,
but even then, it's totally optional.
These days I prefer to write Java in a plain text editor like Sublime text as well.
In any case, normally one does not need to use an IDE for writing Python code.

pasted it in and run it.

But the downside of that is you don't get to see the intermediate outputs.
But again, that's what print statements are for.
All right.
So what can we conclude from this exercise?
Well, we can see that this code runs exactly the same inside Jupyter Notebook as it does everywhere
else.
That's why I always say Python code is Python code no matter where it is.
If you want to use Jupyter Notebook to run the course code, there's absolutely nothing stopping you
from doing so.

--- Python 2 vs Python 3 ---

Hey, everyone, and welcome back to this class.
This is a short lecture to explain that you are free to use whichever version of Python you want in
this course.
Just one side note before we begin.
While I've updated all the code in the repository to work with Python three, you still need to git
pull to ensure that you have the latest version.
I know that some people forked the repo on GitHub, but I strongly recommend not doing this as you won't
get the latest updates as easily.
If you do have a fork, consider just getting rid of it or at least make sure you pull the latest changes
often.
A lot of people consider Python three to be the new version of Python and therefore it's the one everyone
should use.
But that is a very limited perspective.
First, Python three is actually not that new.
In fact, at the time this lecture was made, 2018.
Python three is almost ten years old.
In fact, Python 2.6 was released at the same time as Python three.
So Python 2.6 and Python 3.0 are actually the same age.
Some people think that because three is a bigger number than two, that means we should use three.
Of course, you can see why this is a somewhat superficial way of reasoning.
The bigger issue is this If you join a large company using Python today and they build their technology
stack many years ago, they are probably using Python two.
And if you're in a large company, it's not easy to just recreate your entire code base.
That might take months or years of effort.
No reasonable company that makes millions of dollars per day is going to halt their operations just
so they can switch Python versions.
Consider your choices if you were a manager or a business owner.
You have a list of things to get done.
Your clients want these things done and they're going to pay you lots of money to get them done.
So if you have a choice between your engineers working on something that will make money versus working
on something where you not only get paid no money, but that actually costs you money because you have
to pay the engineers to do the work.
Which are you going to choose?
Most people I know would choose the option to continue making money.
And so, for better or worse, a lot of folks are still using Python two.
That means if you join such a company, you'll be forced to learn Python two syntax.
There will simply be no way around you having to learn Python two.
At the same time, the differences between Python two and Python three are usually trivial.
For example, instead of print with a space after it, print works like a function, so it has to have
parentheses.
Another example is that we use range instead of x range, which both return generators rather than lists.
In Python two range returns a list which can be inefficient.
So we try not to use that when we're using python two.
In Python two division using a slash can do different things depending on what its arguments are.
So for example, if both the numerator and denominator are integers, then the result will be an integer.
But if at least one argument is a float, then the result will be a float.
This is the same way Division works in C-style languages, so it's pretty typical behavior in Python
three.
A slash always does float division, so even if your numerator and denominator are both integers, the
result will be a float.
If you want to do integer division, you have to use a double slash.
Other examples are given on this page on my website.
Lazy Programmers slash Python 2 to 3 tips.
Of course, aside from these things, there are a lot more less frequent discrepancies you may come
across.
So if you come across something that doesn't work, you're encouraged to use the Q&amp;A, since that is
the best place for you to get help.
However, you most likely won't need help since the changes are trivial 99% of the time.
So there's no need to give up if you see that the code you're working with is for Python two and not
Python three.
Most likely just a few lines need to be changed as per the error messages you get and you'll be up and
running in no time.

--- Is Theano Dead? ---

Hey, everyone, and welcome back to this class.
I created this lecture to answer a question I've been getting a lot recently.
As you may have heard, Yoshua Bengio recently announced that development on piano is going to stop
after version 1.0.
I posted about this on my website.
Lazy Programmers Me on September 29th, 2017.
In case you want to go check out the post, a lot of people have interpreted this announcement to mean
that Fiano is dead.
I think that's a bit of an overstatement.
And in this lecture I will explain why.
So first, let's talk about why the N0 is not quite dead.
Well, if you go over to their GitHub page, you'll see that their latest release is in fact one point
0.1.
So if you were thinking they hit 1.0 and just dropped everything, you can see why that is not quite
right.
As of the end of 2017.
The latest version is one point 0.1.
We have yet to see if there will be releases after this.
Secondly, consider what version 1.0 being released really means.
In these modern days of open source, software, developers are generally very conservative in their
numbering.
You'll see projects in version zero point something for years, even when there are huge communities
around them.
Case in point the latest version of NumPy as of the creation of this lecture is 1.14.
The latest version of scipy is 1.0.
The latest version of Pandas is zero dot 20 1.1.
The latest version of scikit learn is 0.9.1.
These are all libraries that we have been using extensively for years, and half of them haven't even
reached 1.0.
So the fact that the developers of Tiano thought their project deserved a 1.0 release is a huge milestone.
This is in fact the most perfect version of Tiano that has ever existed, so why not make use of it?
It's also important to keep in mind that this is software.
Software is code.
Code is not like a car.
For example, if you don't maintain your car, eventually it will fall apart and it won't work anymore.
Code is not like that.
Just because you don't maintain your code doesn't mean it stops working.
So rest assured, you don't have to be afraid that no one is maintaining piano.
It's not going to break on you.
Also keep in mind that pretty much all of the modern, deep learning libraries you see today, not including
Theano, are heavily inspired by piano.
There are some differences, but many similarities.
As you can probably tell from my courses, I am mostly concerned with fundamentals.
What do I mean by that?
Well, as an example, I believe that in order to teach someone how to add, you have to teach them
a fundamental understanding about addition.
You can't only teach someone how to memorize one plus one equals two, one plus two equals three, and
so on.
Addition is a great example because there are an infinite number of possibilities you can have when
doing addition.
In fact, it is impossible to understand unless you know the fundamental principles.
I believe learning all things should be the same way.
So when you learn piano, it's not about the syntax, it's about learning the basic fundamentals of
how the piano does things.
It's very different from regular numpy programming.
You're not programming with numbers.
You're programming with variables.
So when you write an assignment statement like A equals B plus C, you're not assigning the values of
B plus C into A in piano.
B and C don't have any values.
So that statement doesn't make sense to begin with.
Instead, all you're doing is telling the D.A. how A is to be calculated in the future when you give
it values for B and C.
That's a fundamental principle that carries over to TensorFlow, pytorch, Cmtc and so forth.
So learning piano is not about memorizing syntax.
It's about understanding these fundamental principles.
They easily carry over to other libraries which are similar in many respects because they were inspired
by piano.
Also keep in mind that most deep learning libraries came out much later than piano.
They are still in their very early stages of development.
In some cases, that means they don't even have a lot of features yet.
For example, array indexing.
Easy to do in piano, not so easy or impossible in other libraries.
I go more in depth about these differences in my piano versus TensorFlow lecture.
In Deep Learning Part two, otherwise known as Modern Deep Learning in Python.
So if you're looking for a certain special functionality, be mindful that the library you're working
with may not even include it to begin with.
Another important fact that I've repeated before is that Fiano is a great learning tool.
It's absolutely fantastic for learning how to code neural networks.
This is mainly due to the fact that the syntax in Viano looks a lot like the math you see on paper.
As an example, let's look at the equation for a neural network layer and compare it to numpy, syntax
and theano syntax.
So not only does it look like the math, it looks pretty much exactly the same as numpy, which is great
because you already know numpy.
So it's very easy to go from numpy to piano.
The syntax is essentially the same.
As an anti example to this.
Let's look at some code.
So here's the code for creating the neural network layers and specifying the cost.
Here is the code for the training loop, which of course isn't an actual loop because the loop happens
internally.
So out of this 100 lines of code, where can we even find the equations for a neural network layer?
They are, in fact, nowhere to be found.
Let's look at this code for loading in the data.
We can see how unintuitive this looks.
It's not clear what format the data has to be in for these functions to work.
Nor is it clear how we can customize these functions for different types of data.
This isn't to say that Cmtc is bad.
I just picked a random example, but it just goes to show that compared to a piano and what we know
about scikit learn and numpy, it's not easy to go from one to the other.
The most important drawback, however, is that this code bears almost no relation to the theory and
algorithms we discuss in deep learning.
Theano is nice because there is a smooth transition from math to numpy to piano code.
It all pretty much looks the same.
Everything you see in the math and the pseudo code happens in the actual code.
With libraries like this, you end up looking through documentation and trying to figure out what is
the right function to call.
And as I mentioned before, it may not even exist.
So you'll just be looking and looking forever.
Finally, let's look at this from a practical standpoint.
I use this same example in my lecture about Python two versus Python three, because it's a real example.
And if you encounter it, you have no choice but to conform.
So consider the situation where you're in a large company making millions of dollars.
Clients are paying you to add functionality to your existing product.
The more stuff you make, the more money you make.
Think about how unreasonable it would be to tell your clients, Hey, I'm going to stop working on some
stuff I promised you because the devs want to recreate our neural networks in TensorFlow instead of
piano.
That sounds ridiculous to the client.
There's no visible effect.
As a client, it sounds like you're telling me you want to spend months writing code so that you can
replace a neural network with another neural network that gives me the exact same result as the old
neural network.
Well, firstly, you're not getting paid for that.
So your choices are the following.
Number one, continue building with what you have and make money.
Or number two, let your devs spend a lot of time rewriting code that is invisible to everyone except
them.
Do not get paid anything for it and in fact lose money because you still have to pay those devs.
As a manager or business owner, the choice seems obvious.
Continue making money.
So the point is, if you're joining a team who has already built their stack using Theano, then that's
what you'll continue to use.
If their systems are using the and they require debugging, you'll have to know theano very well in
order to debug the code saying stop your business for three months so we can rewrite all your machine.
Learning code is an unreasonable prospect to say the least.
So I hope this lecture has convinced you that immediately shying away from piano upon hearing that development
has stopped is a bit of an overreaction.
I hope you can understand the many positives of working in piano, including how it can actually aid
you in learning about deep learning.

--- How to Succeed in this Course (Long Version) ---

Hey, everyone, and welcome back to this class.
This is a short lecture I like to give in each of my courses on how to succeed in this course.
This is very important to talk about because you're learning a difficult technical subject.
It's easy to get stuck or even worse, give up because you're just not getting things.
Maybe there are too many gaps in your knowledge, so you can't connect the dots.
Maybe some assumption you've made is incorrect.
And keep in mind, there's a lot of material in this course.
It can be overwhelming if you're new to this field of study and you're not familiar with the conventions
and the typical patterns we use.
Now, if you've already seen this lecture in the past, you are more than welcome to skip it.
If you remember what's in it and you're already using these guidelines.
The first guideline is this Remember that I want you to succeed.
In fact, that's why I'm giving you these guidelines in the first place.
So if at any point when you're watching these lectures and you become confused, just ask on the Q&amp;A.
Also known as the discussion forum.
Typically I respond within hours, sometimes even within minutes.
If I happen to be on the site and I see a notification.
I answer any and all questions.
So even if you think it's stupid, just ask anyway.
One example I get from time to time is where do I get the code for this course?
And of course, my answer is typically, well, it's in the lecture called Where to get the code for
this course.
At the end of the day, no one cares if your question was trivial or silly.
If it's something keeping you from progressing in the course, I want to answer it.
Now, very rarely I get people saying, Well, I was so confused that I couldn't even think of the right
question to ask.
And this is where you have to take some responsibility for your own learning.
You have to ask yourself, what did the instructor assume?
I know that I didn't actually know.
Maybe I can look into those things and that will help me formulate a question.
Maybe there's some background knowledge I'm missing.
A lot of people like to have exercises to practice when they're taking a course.
The act of thinking of the right question to ask is itself an exercise.
It might not be the type of exercise you thought you'd be doing, but in many ways this is more profound.
This is an exercise that requires you to think and to change how you think.
And ultimately, that is the best value you can get out of a course.
Speaking of background knowledge, this is one of the most important guidelines in this lecture.
You may have noticed that in the course description I've listed some prerequisites.
Or in other words, things you should know before starting this course.
Now, you might wonder why do I ask you to know these things?
Why don't I just teach them in the course?
And while we do review some topics in this course, it's not the focus.
There's such a wide variety of students in this course.
Students from almost every country in the world, students of all ages, students of all backgrounds.
What does this mean?
That means what's review for one person is going to be totally new for another.
Some people are going to tell me they don't know calculus.
Some people are going to be very far behind with their Python programming skills.
But if we start teaching Python programming skills, other people are going to say, Hey, why are we
doing Python?
Isn't the topic of this course deep learning?
And I tend to agree that we should stay on topic.
So how can we reconcile this?
Well, it doesn't make sense for me to give you an entire computer science education in this one course,
because a lot of that would be irrelevant for a lot of people.
The only logical thing to do is for all of us to meet at a common meeting point.
How is that point defined?
Well, it's defined by the course prerequisites.
In fact, they are so important that in certain instances I've listed the course prerequisites twice,
just in case you missed it the first time.
And I'm also reminding you now for good measure.
Now, one pitfall is that people sometimes assume they know things that they don't really know.
So if you took calculus 20 years ago and you got a C and you skipped class, and the times that you
did show up for class, you were sleeping, well, probably you don't know that much calculus.
But remember, I want you to succeed.
I want you to be able to learn this material.
I can point you to resources where you can pick up this missing background knowledge.
But the key is you have to ask.
In some cases, there are certain places where I'm not allowed to post certain links.
So we have to be mindful of the rules of the platform we're using.
Now, occasionally people ask me, Well, why don't you just teach a course for pure beginners?
Why don't you teach these courses in such a way that they don't require lots of math and lots of prerequisites?
Well, I don't want to turn this into an easy beginners type, of course, because for one, that's
not what I'm interested in personally.
And number two, don't forget that this is state of the art stuff.
State of the art generally isn't easy, and if it were easy, that would mean everyone is doing it.
And if a thousand other people can do the same thing as you, well, what's a potential employer going
to think?
What are your chances of getting that job when all 1000 of you have the exact same skills?
So we don't want skills that are easy to get.
We want skills that are hard to get, skills that put you at the top of your game, skills that not
many people have.
If I'm hiring someone, I want the guy who stands out.
Not the guy who says the same thing as everyone else.
Finally, the final guideline for how to succeed in this course.
I mentioned earlier that there's a lot of material so much that it can be hard to keep track of.
In addition to taking your own notes and doing all the derivations yourself, how can you be absolutely
sure you're understanding this material?
I would, in fact, consider this even more important than the derivations.
Not everyone is going to be a pro at math, so it's understandable if you want to skip a few steps.
Sometimes it's possible to compartmentalize some concepts or delegate learning them for some other time,
but still understanding the high level picture and how it fits in with everything else.
What you cannot skip is the implementation.
Implementing these algorithms in code is the real test for whether or not you understand the material.
And this is not about reinventing the wheel or that you shouldn't roll your own whatever, because someone
else out there can do it better than you.
That is a software engineering principle, not a principle of learning.
In production, you don't want to write your own hash map or your own string class.
And yet what do we usually do as an exercise in a typical intro to computer science class?
Well, we do precisely that.
We implement hash maps and we implement strings.
And why do we do this?
Because we want to have a better understanding of how these things work.
If you make an assumption that's incorrect, well, your code is probably just not going to work.
Merely using a hash map and using a string only gives you a very superficial understanding.
That's beginner stuff.
That's stuff everyone knows.
That's not stuff that's going to help you stand out from the competition.
And it's true that you'll never use your own homemade hash map or your own homemade string in any code
you write.
Unless, of course, you are a professional that focuses on that sort of thing.
They'll probably be inefficient and they'll probably have bugs, but that's okay because you're not
doing it to make it perfect.
You're doing it to learn.
You'll notice that the general pattern that this course follows is that we always discuss theory and
then we implement that theory in code.
So we're always going from theory to code, implementing everything that we learned.
If you can try to implement something before watching the coding lecture.
In the appendix, I have a lecture called How to Code by Yourself.
That goes much more in depth into this topic, along with multiple strategies for how to approach implementation
exercises.
So to summarize, these are the guidelines I think are going to help you succeed in this course.
Number one, make generous use of the Q and A, even when you can't think of the right question to ask.
Number two, make sure you meet the prerequisites to this course.
If you're not sure what they are or you're not sure how, see guideline number one.
Number three, try your best to implement everything we learn.
This is the true test for whether or not you understand something.
Remember that it's perfectly normal to get stuck once in a while.
I get stuck all the time when I'm learning.
That doesn't mean I'm not smart, nor does it mean the book or video I'm learning from is bad.
It means that there's a disconnect between what the author believes I knew and what I actually know.
And as a learner, I do my best to close that gap.
A good skill to have is learning how to compartmentalize concepts.
So here are the things I understand.
Here are the things I don't understand, but here are the things I understand about the things I don't
understand.
And these are the holes.
So you know what you're missing and you know where they fit.
And you can work to fill in those gaps over time.
One thing I've been doing quite a bit recently is adding review lectures to my courses.
These aren't meant to teach you everything from scratch since that's going to take you a long time,
but they are meant to help you recall certain things you may have forgotten.
So if you have an idea for a review lecture that you'd like to see added to this course, please feel
free to make a suggestion using the Q&amp;A.

--- Is this for Beginners or Experts? Academic or Practical? Fast or slow-paced? ---

Hey, everyone, and welcome back to this class.
This lecture is designed to answer some common questions I get about how difficult this course is and
where it fits in terms of academia, the professional world and its practical applicability.
So rather than answering this question individually over and over again, it seems like a better idea
to make a lecture about it and answer everybody at the same time.
A lot of people become confused because this is their first machine learning course and they just don't
know where it fits in the grand scheme of things.
So is this course academic or practical, and is it for beginners or experts?
Is it fast paced or slow paced?
I'm going to answer the second question first.
Is this course for beginners or experts?
Interestingly, the problem with this question is a problem for machine learning, too.
Let me explain why this question is itself problematic.
One of the challenges in natural language processing is that of ambiguity.
When someone says some words, the problem is those words can mean multiple things.
And it just so happens that I have some courses on NLP.
So this is a problem I think about often.
So how does that apply here?
Well, when we use the words beginner and expert, we have to ask a beginner.
From whose perspective?
An expert from whose perspective?
For some of you, you might think that a beginner is a random person.
You take off the street who knows nothing.
But even that is ambiguous.
There are people out there who do not even really know how to use a computer beyond turning it on and
checking their email and browsing the web.
There are people out there who are pros at Microsoft Office, so they know how to use a computer and
make a Microsoft Word document or a spreadsheet.
But they've never done any programming before.
There are people out there who are very good developers and have lots of programming experience, but
they have no idea what machine learning is.
So all of these people are beginners in some way, but each of them would require a vastly different
amount of work to prepare for this course.
It's the same situation with experts.
Most people consider anyone who knows more than them to be an expert.
But of course, how much you know and how much some other person knows is relative.
If you know how to plug and play into scikit learn and nothing else, you might seem like an expert
to someone who only knows Excel.
But you'll seem like a beginner to someone who's read Bishop's Pattern Recognition and Machine Learning
book.
So just to finalize our answer to this question, is this course for beginners or experts?
This question is misinformed to begin with because beginners and experts are not well defined terms.
Instead, we have to go back to what is the specific skill set you need before starting this course.
And of course that's defined by the prerequisites which are listed in the course description.
So precision is the key here.
We want to know what is the precise list of skills that I need before taking this course.
This is much better than ambiguous terms like beginner and expert.
Another matter is that people are going to measure their own skills differently.
That's a much tougher issue to deal with.
Sure, I can say you need to know Python coding before starting this course, but you can be sure that
there's a big difference between someone who just learned Hello World and for loops versus someone working
at a big financial institution.
Now, even in those big financial institutions, there can be big gaps in performance.
Even within your own team.
There can be big gaps in performance.
I've seen guys who have been working for 15 years do worse than guys who've been working less than one
year.
So even if I say you need to know Python coding, there can be a lot of variation in skill among those
who consider themselves good at it.
A similar situation happens with deep learning.
I can say you need to know how neural networks work, but there's a big difference between someone who
can code them from scratch and has experience using them versus someone who just watched some online
animations for a few hours.
If you had to bet on who knows neural networks better, who would you pick?
So to really answer this question, we have to turn to the course itself.
If I say you need to know Python coding and you claim that you do, but you don't understand some Python
code in this course, then simply put, your python coding skills are not yet up to par.
So you have to improve your python coding skills by asking questions on the Q and A and taking the appropriate
steps to cover your gaps.
Quite often I get people who are just too confident in their own abilities when unfortunately the reality
is there is a huge discrepancy between what they think they know and what they actually know.
What's funny is these people sometimes say this course requires PhD math.
I assure you there's absolutely no math in any of my courses that requires a PhD or that only a PhD
can understand.
My courses contain only undergraduate math at most.
Sometimes people even claim to have a PhD and then claim that it's still too hard, which is funny because
that essentially invalidates your entire PhD.
The entire point of a PhD is to turn you into an independent researcher.
And so if you're supposed to be at the level of doing independent research, but you can't understand
some undergraduate math, well, that's not very good.
Essentially what it boils down to is people are really trying to say everything I don't understand is
not important.
Everything I do understand is important.
And therefore, everything you say that I don't understand, I don't need to know.
You can see why this thinking is dangerous.
If you truly believe that everything you do not know is not important, then you'll never learn anything
new.
Now, this brings us to our third question.
Is this course fast paced or slow paced?
And I think you can see where I'm going with this.
This is yet another example of ambiguity.
Fast paced.
To whom?
Whether it's fast paced or not depends on which person is taking the course.
So if someone is well prepared, then things will be just right.
If someone already knows most of these topics, then it will probably be too slow.
If someone chose not to prepare for the course or they have an inadequate understanding of the prerequisites,
then it's going to be too fast.
So if I expected you to know something and you didn't, then you have to look it up, which is going
to make the course feel like it's going too fast.
So here is the real test.
If I say something depends on calculus of probability and you claim to know calculus and probability,
but you don't understand the thing that depends on calculus and probability, you have to ask yourself
honestly, do I really understand calculus and probability?
Or did the instructor invent his own kind of calculus and probability that I don't yet understand?
Well, of course that's not very likely.
It's most likely that you thought you understood calculus and probability, but you really don't.
Now on to our initial question.
What kind of course is this?
Is it academic?
Is it for professionals?
Is it practical?
So to understand this, you have to understand where machine learning is usually taught and how it is
usually taught.
Firstly, machine learning is usually taught an upper year computer science and engineering programs
in university and college.
So these are students who already took calculus.
One, two, three linear algebra, differential equations, probability and statistics, discrete math
programming and possibly more, all in their first 2 or 3 years of college.
So by the time they get to third and fourth year, they know all this stuff and they're ready to apply
it.
So a typical academic course might cover a whole library of machine learning algorithms.
So you might learn K nearest neighbor logistic regression, K-means clustering PCA and so on all in
the same course.
Typically you'd cover one algorithm per lecture, which is about two hours.
Overall, you might have something like 12 or maybe 20 of those lectures in a term.
And by cover, I mean mostly via math, geometry and derivations.
These courses don't typically have you at your computer doing programming work.
If you do end up doing programming, it's usually part of a lab assignment, which is actually just
a very small part of the course compared to the rest.
So it's mostly theoretical dealing with equations, solving equations, reasoning about equations.
Those equations typically involve probabilities and information theory.
So you have two hours to go through the theory for one algorithm.
And then if there's coding involved, which there usually isn't, you do it on your own time.
The lectures themselves do not involve any coding.
But there's an even bigger problem with these academic courses.
And what is that?
What happens is that the grades turn out to be very low.
Out of 100%.
The average might be around 25% or up to 40%.
Now, you might think, wow, so everyone fails.
And of course that's not the case since everyone gets shifted based on the average.
So what is the real problem here?
A lot of people ask me, well, why don't you cover every machine learning algorithm in one big massive
course and just call it machine learning?
Well, the answer is that if you try to do too much, you only end up understanding 25% of the material,
which I think is clearly demonstrated here.
And as they say, the proof is in the pudding.
If you look at these courses where students are trying to digest ten different algorithms at the same
time, what is the result?
The result is they don't understand any of them.
If you try to understand too many things, you end up understanding nothing.
I take the perspective that if you're going to take a course, you should aim to understand 100% of
the material.
Each course is broken up into appropriate levels based on how difficult they are and by topic.
So, for example, you can typically take calculus one which focuses on differential calculus.
Calculus two which focuses on integral calculus and calculus three which focuses on multivariable calculus.
You would never learn all of these in just one huge course called math because that's just too much
stuff to understand all at once.
I want you to understand 100% of the material, even 50% or 75% is not good enough, in my opinion.
If you only understand 75% of the material and the next course depends on the 25% that you didn't understand,
well then you're not going to make it to the next level.
Now let's look at the opposite end of the spectrum, what most people might consider to be a practical
course.
I don't consider these to be practical courses and I'll explain why later, but I'll use the word practical
here in quotes, since that's what some of the more beginner students will refer to them as.
So how does a practical course typically proceed?
Well, first, you are never really taught how the algorithms work other than at a very high level.
You might learn how they work by way of analogies and metaphors.
So, for example, suppose you're learning about gradient descent.
Well, the instructor might relate this to a ball rolling down a hill.
Now some beginner students, they might conclude that I understand a ball rolling down a hill.
Therefore I understand gradient descent, but they neglect to understand how to take derivatives.
They neglect to understand how to choose a good learning rate and most importantly, they neglect how
to implement gradient descent in code.
So it's totally wrong to assume that just because you can visualize a ball rolling down a hill, that
you understand gradient descent.
Why?
Well, the problem is everybody can imagine a ball rolling down a hill.
Your grandparents, who can't even turn on a computer can understand a ball rolling down a hill.
So don't equate understanding an analogy to understanding the real topic.
So what's the next thing these practical courses do?
The next thing they usually teach you is how to plug into an API or in other words, make use of a library
someone else wrote.
This is also not ideal.
Typically this involves just 2 or 3 lines of code.
You have to wonder if machine learning just boils down to 2 or 3 lines of code.
Why is it such a big deal these days?
Why did it take decades for people to realize its usefulness?
And of course, the answer is that there's much more to machine learning than just these 2 or 3 lines
of code.
When you use a deep learning library like Keras, you're not doing deep learning.
You are using a deep learning interface.
You're simply programming.
It's more accurate to say I can program with deep learning libraries.
Rather than saying I actually know deep learning.
But some of you might say, well, that's all I really want to do.
All I need is an API and for the API to give me an answer, I don't care about how it works.
But then here is where you run into trouble.
Recall that in addition to all data is the same.
We know that all machine learning interfaces are the same.
So the question becomes which API do you choose?
If they're all the same?
How can you make an informed choice about which one to use?
Well, the reality is if you don't know how these things work, then you can't answer this question.
This is why some of these so called practical courses can cover 20 algorithms in the same course.
It's because they never actually talk about how the algorithms work.
Since the API is the same every time, it's very easy to just do the same thing 20 times and then not
bother with the details.
So this gives people a lot of confidence that they were able to do the same thing 20 times.
But it's dangerous thinking because you don't realize you really only learned one thing.
You just repeated it 20 times.
So how about when it comes to the professional world?
Sometimes people tend to believe that in your profession you'll be leaning more toward the practical
end of the spectrum, where you don't really need to know how a machine learning model works.
You just have to know its API.
But what it really comes down to is what your job actually is.
If you're a regular, everyday programmer and you just want to see how a machine learning model might
perform on your data, then you're going to use a pre-built library most likely.
That's just the 2 or 3 lines of code I referenced earlier.
However, that does not mean you don't still have to conform to best practices.
Make sure you're not mixing your test set with your train set.
Make sure you're doing proper validation and so on.
And when it comes to tuning your model, then you'll have to be able to dig down into the details and
see which hyper parameters are possible to change and which are likely to have the most impact.
So only knowing how to use an API is only going to get you past the first stage.
You can plug in your data and at least your code has the right syntax and doesn't crash.
But of course your code not crashing doesn't necessarily mean it's right.
Back on the other end of the spectrum, you have programmers and scientists whose sole job it is to
do machine learning.
These guys are machine learning experts.
They know the math inside and out and have a full computer science education.
And so to even be able to communicate your ideas effectively, you have to be comfortable with the theory
and not just how to plug into an API with two lines of code.
These scientists are building custom models, really digging down into the math to not only use, but
invent new things and to be able to invent new things.
You really need to know the math very well.
And so if you're on a team like this, you can't just know how to write two lines of code.
Your skill set has to be far beyond that.
So you have theoretical background has to be very solid if you want to be able to communicate effectively
with other scientists.
Now, a lot of people say, well, I don't ever want to be a scientist, so I don't really care how
these algorithms work.
Unfortunately, that's a very bad attitude to have in the workplace.
Suppose, for example, I am running a business.
Now an employee of mine, he wants to show off and say he's learning about machine learning.
He says, Oh, I'm taking this machine learning course online.
Therefore, I will become a machine learning master.
But his approach to machine learning is to learn as little as possible.
He says he doesn't care about the math.
He only wants the practical stuff.
He only wants to know the bare minimum.
He tells me he doesn't care how it works.
He's just going to plug into some API because that's what his buddy told him he could do.
As a business owner, I really don't want this guy working for me.
I run a business that sells something and the life of the company itself and the livelihoods of the
employees all depend on our products being the best they can possibly be.
So if someone comes to me and says, I don't care about being the best, I just care about doing the
bare minimum.
My response is, I don't want that guy on my team.
In fact, he should be fired.
So be careful if this is your approach to machine learning.
No business owner wants to hear that your approach to the business is to do the bare minimum and to
put off some math because you think it's too hard.
That's a scary prospect for a business owner.
Now, if we go back to our original question, maybe you've realized we haven't answered it yet.
What kind, of course is this?
Well, you can think of it as a marriage between these approaches.
We want to cover all the math and derivations because that's very important if you want to be able to
communicate effectively with other scientists, to be able to debug your code effectively and to be
able to invent new approaches.
This is also what we mean by machine learning.
We mean really knowing machine learning and not just programming with an API.
So the theoretical background is very important.
At the same time, we're not going to introduce 20 different algorithms in the same course because unlike
a typical academic course where you can pass just by understanding 25% of the material in this course,
it's structured in a way that if you have any missing gaps, it's going to be impossible for you to
move on to the next stage.
But academic courses don't contain much coding, if at all.
So in this course, every algorithm we learn about will be implemented in code.
My motto is if you can't implement it, then you don't understand it.
You can pretend to understand it.
And when you talk about it, you might sound like you understand it, but implementation is the true
test.
It's like a math exam rather than writing an essay.
When you write an essay, you might be able to fool the teacher into thinking you understand something.
But in a math exam, either your answers are right or your answers are wrong.
In this context, either your code works or it doesn't.
It's very clear cut.
When it comes to practicality, we cover that, too.
We demonstrate our algorithms both on real world data like text and images, and we demonstrate our
algorithms on two dimensional datasets so that you can visualize what an algorithm is doing.
Both of these are important texts and images are quite possibly the most practical data there is.
Some billion dollar companies exist and thrive because of those capabilities.
2D datasets are critical as well because as humans, that's the only thing we can see.
But at the same time, we don't try to pull the wool over your eyes and pretend like you need 20 different
datasets to practice on.
Instead, we learn something much more valuable how to use an algorithm on an infinite number of datasets
because we are able to abstract the idea that all data is the same.
Remember that all the computer sees is a list of numbers.
It doesn't know that those numbers represent height measurements or an image pixel.
And of course, by experiencing both ends of the spectrum, both the theory and the implementation learn
and then finally plugging data into your model that's going to make you very well rounded professionally.
To conclude this lecture, I want to pose a final question.
How does all this knowledge help you?
Why does understanding the approach of this course make you better at machine learning in general?
Well, my hope is that this gives you a very high level, bird's eye view of the landscape.
You can see the different approaches of people from different backgrounds.
Academic people typically don't see what other non-academic people are doing, and those people who
hate math typically don't see what people who love math are doing.
So with this bird's eye view, you can see everybody.
If you're learning machine learning, it helps you understand who am I competing with when I apply for
a job and what kinds of jobs are out there and what would be adequate preparation?
If you're hiring for a machine learning team, it helps you understand the different backgrounds of
individuals.
You have to compare to one another to understand their strengths and weaknesses and what they bring
to the table.

In reinforcement learning, the idea is more like you have a robot living in the real world.

So the learning paradigm in reinforcement learning is sequential.
This is opposed to supervised and unsupervised learning where your data set usually resides in some
file on your hard drive.

But ensemble methods are a great fit.
Deep learning is very dependent on hyper parameters, and if you choose incorrectly, your model will
perform very poorly.
Sometimes it requires immense computing power to find good hyper parameters.
This is an active area of research.
It is not yet solved.
This is why you can implement what you see in a deep learning paper.
But suppose the author left out some seemingly insignificant detail, so you end up having to make an
assumption and then your results end up totally different.
So deep learning is fragile, but luckily ensemble methods are not.
